# ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY LAB

- Engineering chemistry lab is a practical course that introduces the students to the basic concepts and techniques of chemistry applied to engineering problems.
- The main objectives of the lab are:
  - To familiarize the students with the use of laboratory equipment and instruments.
  - To develop the skills of observation, measurement, calculation, and analysis of experimental data.
  - To demonstrate the principles and applications of chemical reactions, equilibrium, kinetics, electrochemistry, corrosion, water analysis, and polymerization.
  - To enhance the understanding of the theoretical concepts learned in the engineering chemistry course.
- The lab consists of several experiments that cover the following topics:
  - Acid-base titration: To determine the concentration of an unknown acid or base solution using a standard solution and an indicator.
  - Redox titration: To determine the concentration of an unknown oxidizing or reducing agent using a standard solution and a redox indicator.
  - Complexometric titration: To determine the hardness of water by titrating with EDTA solution using Eriochrome Black T as an indicator.
  - Potentiometry: To measure the electromotive force (EMF) of a cell and to determine the standard electrode potential of a metal electrode.
  - Conductometry: To measure the electrical conductivity of a solution and to determine the equivalent conductance and degree of dissociation of an electrolyte.
  - pH-metry: To measure the pH of a solution and to determine the dissociation constant of a weak acid or base.
  - Spectrophotometry: To measure the absorbance of a solution and to determine the concentration of a solute using Beer-Lambert law.
  - Corrosion: To study the factors affecting the rate of corrosion of iron and to measure the corrosion rate using weight loss method.
  - Water analysis: To determine the alkalinity, chloride, dissolved oxygen, and biochemical oxygen demand of a water sample.
  - Polymerization: To synthesize a polymer by free radical polymerization and to determine its molecular weight by viscosity method.
- The lab report should include the following sections:
  - Title: The name of the experiment and the date of the lab session.
  - Objective: The purpose and aim of the experiment.
  - Theory: The relevant background information and formulas used in the experiment.
  - Procedure: The detailed steps followed to perform the experiment.
  - Observations: The raw data collected during the experiment in tabular form.
  - Calculations: The necessary calculations performed using the data to obtain the results.
  - Results: The final outcomes of the experiment with proper units and significant figures.
  - Discussion: The analysis and interpretation of the results and the comparison with the theoretical values or literature values.
  - Conclusion: The summary of the main findings and the sources of error and uncertainty in the experiment.
  - References: The list of the books, articles, websites, or any other sources used in the experiment.



## Course Objectives:

- To introduce the basic concepts and principles of artificial intelligence (AI) and its applications.
- To familiarize the students with the methods and techniques of knowledge representation, reasoning, planning, search, and machine learning in AI.
- To develop the skills and abilities to design, implement, and evaluate AI systems and agents using various tools and languages.
- To expose the students to the current trends and challenges in AI research and practice.

### 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles, applications, or techniques, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments can be used for various purposes, such as quality control, research and development, environmental monitoring, forensic analysis, clinical diagnosis, etc.
- Analytical instruments consist of three main components: a sample introduction system, a detector, and a data processing system.
- A sample introduction system is responsible for preparing, transporting, and introducing the sample into the detector. It can include devices such as syringes, pumps, valves, columns, nebulizers, etc.
- A detector is the part of the instrument that converts the physical, chemical, or biological signal from the sample into an electrical signal that can be measured and recorded. It can include devices such as photodiodes, electrodes, ion sources, etc.
- A data processing system is the part of the instrument that analyzes, displays, and stores the data obtained from the detector. It can include devices such as computers, software, printers, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different characteristics, such as sensitivity, selectivity, accuracy, precision, resolution, range, etc., that affect their performance and suitability for different applications.
- Analytical instruments can also have different advantages and limitations, such as cost, speed, reliability, complexity, maintenance, etc., that need to be considered when choosing or designing an instrument for a specific purpose.

### 2. To enable the students to understand about the analysis of chloride content, hardness, alkalinity of water.

- Chloride content is the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in water. Chloride ions are derived from natural sources such as rocks, soil, and seawater, as well as from human activities such as agriculture, industry, and sewage. Chloride content is an important indicator of water quality, as high levels of chloride can affect the taste, odor, and corrosion of water and pipes. Chloride content can be measured by titration with silver nitrate (AgNO3) solution, using potassium chromate (K2CrO4) as an indicator. The end point of the titration is marked by the formation of a brick-red precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2CrO4).

- Hardness is the measure of the ability of water to precipitate soap. Hard water contains high concentrations of calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) ions, which react with soap to form insoluble scum. Hard water can also cause scaling and clogging of pipes, boilers, and appliances. Hardness can be classified into temporary and permanent hardness. Temporary hardness is caused by the presence of bicarbonates (HCO3-) of calcium and magnesium, which can be removed by boiling the water. Permanent hardness is caused by the presence of chlorides, sulfates, and nitrates of calcium and magnesium, which cannot be removed by boiling. Hardness can be measured by titration with ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) solution, using eriochrome black T (EBT) as an indicator. The end point of the titration is marked by the change of color of the indicator from wine red to blue.

- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. Alkaline water contains high concentrations of hydroxide (OH-), carbonate (CO32-), and bicarbonate (HCO3-) ions, which can react with acids to form water and salts. Alkalinity is an important parameter of water quality, as it affects the pH, buffering, and corrosion of water and pipes. Alkalinity can be measured by titration with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid (H2SO4), using phenolphthalein and methyl orange as indicators. The end point of the titration with phenolphthalein is marked by the disappearance of the pink color of the indicator, which corresponds to the total alkalinity of the water. The end point of the titration with methyl orange is marked by the change of color of the indicator from yellow to orange, which corresponds to the phenolphthalein alkalinity of the water. The difference between the total alkalinity and the phenolphthalein alkalinity is the bicarbonate alkalinity of the water.

### 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of a liquid.

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a liquid is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in moles per liter. The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, where 0 is the most acidic, 7 is neutral, and 14 is the most basic. Some examples of liquids with different pH values are lemon juice (pH 2), water (pH 7), and ammonia (pH 11).
- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is expressed in units of force per unit length, such as newtons per meter (N/m). Surface tension causes liquids to form droplets, bubbles, and films. It also affects the shape of liquid surfaces and the capillary action of liquids in narrow tubes. Some factors that influence surface tension are temperature, impurities, and surfactants. Some examples of liquids with different surface tensions are water (72.8 mN/m), ethanol (22.3 mN/m), and mercury (485 mN/m).
- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a liquid is to flow. It is expressed in units of dynamic viscosity, such as pascal-seconds (Pa·s) or poise (P). Viscosity depends on the internal friction between the molecules of a liquid, which is affected by their size, shape, and interactions. Viscosity also varies with temperature and pressure. Some examples of liquids with different viscosities are water (0.001 Pa·s), honey (10 Pa·s), and tar (1000 Pa·s).



### 4. To enable the students to understand about the preparation of different resins.

- Resins are natural or synthetic organic substances that are usually solid or semi-solid, insoluble in water, and have a high melting point.
- Resins are widely used in various industries, such as paints, adhesives, plastics, rubber, and pharmaceuticals.
- Resins can be classified into two main types: thermoplastic and thermosetting.
- Thermoplastic resins are resins that can be softened by heating and hardened by cooling, without undergoing any chemical change. They can be repeatedly melted and reshaped. Examples of thermoplastic resins are polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, and nylon.
- Thermosetting resins are resins that undergo an irreversible chemical reaction when heated, forming a rigid and infusible network. They cannot be remelted or reshaped. Examples of thermosetting resins are phenol-formaldehyde, urea-formaldehyde, epoxy, and polyester.
- The preparation of different resins depends on the type and properties of the raw materials, the desired characteristics of the final product, and the processing methods.
- Some general steps involved in the preparation of resins are:
  - Selection and purification of the raw materials, such as monomers, solvents, catalysts, and additives.
  - Polymerization or condensation of the monomers, either in bulk, in solution, in suspension, or in emulsion, to form the resin.
  - Modification or curing of the resin, by adding cross-linking agents, fillers, pigments, stabilizers, or other additives, to improve the mechanical, thermal, chemical, or optical properties of the resin.
  - Shaping or molding of the resin, by extrusion, injection, compression, or casting, to form the desired shape and size of the product.
  - Finishing or coating of the resin, by applying surface treatments, such as polishing, painting, or laminating, to enhance the appearance, durability, or functionality of the product.



### 5. To enable the students to understand about the synthesis of organic compounds such as adipic acid and paracetamol by conventional and green route.

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid that is used as a raw material for the production of nylon-6,6, a synthetic polymer widely used in various applications.
- Paracetamol is an analgesic and antipyretic drug that is widely used for the relief of pain and fever.
- Conventional route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexane or a mixture of cyclohexanol and cyclohexanone with nitric acid, which produces large amounts of nitrous oxide, a greenhouse gas, as a by-product.
- Conventional route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the acetylation of phenol with acetic anhydride, which produces acetic acid as a by-product and requires high temperature and pressure.
- Green route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexene with hydrogen peroxide in the presence of a catalyst, such as sodium tungstate, in a microemulsion, which produces water as the only by-product and requires mild temperature and pressure .
- Green route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the reaction of 4-aminophenol with acetic acid in the presence of a catalyst, such as sodium acetate, in water, which produces water as the only by-product and requires moderate temperature and pressure.
- Green route of synthesis of adipic acid and paracetamol are environmentally friendly, as they reduce the use of harmful solvents, reagents, and catalysts, and minimize the generation of waste and emissions  .
- Green route of synthesis of adipic acid and paracetamol are also economically feasible, as they reduce the energy consumption, improve the yield and purity of the products, and enable the recycling and reuse of the reaction media  .

## LIST OF EXPERIMENTS

An experiment is a scientific procedure that aims to test a hypothesis, answer a question, or discover something new. Experiments usually involve manipulating one or more variables and measuring their effects on other variables. Experiments can be classified into different types based on their purpose, design, and execution. Some of the common types of experiments are:

- **Controlled experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher controls all the factors that could affect the outcome, except for the one that is being tested. The factor that is being tested is called the independent variable, and the outcome that is measured is called the dependent variable. The researcher also uses a control group, which is a group that does not receive the treatment or manipulation of the independent variable, to compare with the experimental group, which does receive the treatment or manipulation. Controlled experiments are often used to establish causal relationships between variables. For example, a controlled experiment to test the effect of a new drug on blood pressure would involve randomly assigning patients to either receive the drug or a placebo, and measuring their blood pressure before and after the treatment.

- **Natural experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher does not manipulate any variables, but instead observes the effects of a natural event or phenomenon on the variables of interest. Natural experiments are often used when controlled experiments are not possible, ethical, or practical. For example, a natural experiment to study the impact of a volcanic eruption on climate change would involve measuring the changes in temperature, precipitation, and atmospheric composition before and after the eruption.

- **Field experiments**: These are experiments that are conducted in a natural setting, rather than in a laboratory or a controlled environment. Field experiments are often used to study the behavior of humans or animals in their natural habitats, or the effects of interventions or policies in real-world contexts. For example, a field experiment to test the effect of a cash transfer program on poverty reduction would involve randomly selecting some households to receive the cash and others to not receive it, and measuring their income, consumption, and well-being over time.

- **Quasi-experiments**: These are experiments that resemble controlled experiments, but lack some of the key features, such as random assignment, control group, or pretest. Quasi-experiments are often used when controlled experiments are not feasible, ethical, or desirable. For example, a quasi-experiment to evaluate the effect of a school reform on student achievement would involve comparing the test scores of students who attended the reformed school with those who attended a similar but unreformed school, without randomly assigning the students to the schools.

- **Correlational experiments**: These are experiments that measure the relationship between two or more variables, without manipulating any of them. Correlational experiments are often used to explore the patterns, trends, or associations among variables, but they cannot establish causality. For example, a correlational experiment to examine the relationship between smoking and lung cancer would involve measuring the smoking habits and the incidence of lung cancer among a large sample of people, but not intervening to change their smoking behavior.



### 1. Calibration of Analytical Equipment and apparatus

Calibration is the process of evaluating and adjusting the precision and accuracy of measurement equipment. Proper calibration of an instrument allows people to have a safe working environment and produce valid data for future reference. Calibration also ensures the compliance with quality standards and regulations.

Some of the points to consider for calibration of analytical equipment and apparatus are:

- Make an annual schedule for calibrating each instrument/equipment indicating the dates when the instrument/equipment is to be calibrated.
- For all new instruments, calibrate the instrument more frequently (once in three months for a year followed by once in six months), and for existing instruments follow SOP for Calibration.
- The frequency of calibration will depend on usage, environmental conditions, stability, and criticality of the instrument/equipment. It is best to decide the intervals on the basis of in house standard operating procedure guidelines which in turn are based on equipment supplier recommendations.
- Use appropriate standards and reference materials for calibration that are traceable to national or international standards. For example, use certified mass standards for calibrating balances.
- Document the calibration results and procedures in a calibration report or certificate that includes the identification of the instrument/equipment, the date and time of calibration, the standards and reference materials used, the calibration method, the calibration data, the calibration uncertainty, and the signature of the person performing the calibration.
- Label the instrument/equipment with a calibration sticker that indicates the date of calibration, the date of next calibration, and the calibration status (pass or fail).
- If the instrument/equipment fails the calibration, take corrective actions such as repairing, adjusting, or replacing the instrument/equipment, and re-calibrate it before using it for measurements. Also, investigate the impact of the calibration failure on the previous measurements and take necessary actions to ensure the quality of the data.
- Keep the instrument/equipment clean and in good working condition. If problems with the equipment are observed, the calibration interval should be shortened or the equipment should be taken out of service.



### 2. Determination of Hardness of water sample by EDTA method

Hardness of water is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water. These ions form insoluble precipitates with soap and interfere with the cleaning action. Hardness of water can be classified into temporary hardness and permanent hardness. Temporary hardness is caused by the presence of bicarbonates of calcium and magnesium, which can be removed by boiling the water. Permanent hardness is caused by the presence of chlorides, sulfates and nitrates of calcium and magnesium, which cannot be removed by boiling.

EDTA (Ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) is a chelating agent that can form stable complexes with metal ions in water. EDTA can be used to titrate the hardness of water by reacting with the calcium and magnesium ions and changing the color of an indicator. The indicator used is Eriochrome Black T, which is a metallochromic indicator that changes color from wine red to blue when it binds to metal ions.

The procedure for determining the hardness of water by EDTA method is as follows:

- Take a sample volume of 20 ml (V ml) of water in a conical flask.
- Dilute the sample to 40 ml by adding 20 ml of distilled water.
- Add 1 ml of ammonia buffer to bring the pH to 10 ± 0.1. The buffer consists of a mixture of ammonium chloride and ammonia solution.
- Add 1 or 2 drops of Eriochrome Black T indicator solution. The indicator will turn the solution wine red due to the presence of calcium and magnesium ions.
- Titrate the sample with EDTA solution of known concentration (C mol/L) from a burette until the color changes from wine red to blue. Note the volume of EDTA solution used (B ml).
- Repeat the titration with a blank solution of distilled water and note the volume of EDTA solution used (A ml).
- Calculate the hardness of water using the formula:

Hardness of water (mg/L as CaCO3) = (B - A) x C x 1000 / V

The hardness of water can be expressed in terms of calcium carbonate equivalent, which is the mass of calcium carbonate that would produce the same hardness as the sample. The molar mass of calcium carbonate is 100 g/mol. The molar ratio of EDTA to calcium or magnesium is 1:1. Therefore, 1 mol of EDTA can react with 100 g of calcium carbonate. Hence, the factor 1000 in the formula is used to convert the molarity of EDTA to the mass of calcium carbonate equivalent.



### 3. Determination of Alkalinity of water sample.

- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids.
- Alkalinity is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium, sodium, and potassium in water.
- Alkalinity is important for water quality because it affects the pH, corrosion, and disinfection processes of water.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid, using a suitable indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange.
- The titration endpoint is reached when the indicator changes color, indicating that all the alkaline substances in the water sample have been neutralized by the acid.
- The amount of acid used to reach the endpoint is proportional to the alkalinity of the water sample.
- The alkalinity can be expressed in different units, such as milligrams per liter (mg/L) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3), milliequivalents per liter (meq/L), or degrees of French hardness (°f).
- The alkalinity can be classified into different types, such as total alkalinity, phenolphthalein alkalinity, methyl orange alkalinity, carbonate alkalinity, bicarbonate alkalinity, and hydroxide alkalinity, depending on the indicator used and the pH range of the titration.
- The procedure for determining the alkalinity of a water sample is as follows:

  - Collect a representative water sample in a clean glass bottle and store it in a cool place away from sunlight.
  - Rinse a 50 mL burette with distilled water and then with the standard acid solution. Fill the burette with the acid solution and note the initial reading.
  - Rinse a 250 mL conical flask with distilled water and then with the water sample. Transfer 50 mL of the water sample to the flask using a pipette.
  - Add 2 or 3 drops of phenolphthalein indicator to the flask. If the solution turns pink, proceed to the next step. If the solution remains colorless, the phenolphthalein alkalinity is zero and the methyl orange alkalinity can be determined directly.
  - Titrate the solution with the acid solution until the pink color disappears. Note the final reading of the burette and calculate the volume of acid used. This is the phenolphthalein alkalinity.
  - Add 2 or 3 drops of methyl orange indicator to the same flask. The solution should turn orange or red.
  - Titrate the solution with the acid solution until the color changes to yellow. Note the final reading of the burette and calculate the volume of acid used. This is the methyl orange alkalinity.
  - Calculate the total alkalinity, carbonate alkalinity, bicarbonate alkalinity, and hydroxide alkalinity using the following formulas:

    - Total alkalinity = phenolphthalein alkalinity + methyl orange alkalinity
    - Carbonate alkalinity = 2 x phenolphthalein alkalinity
    - Bicarbonate alkalinity = methyl orange alkalinity - phenolphthalein alkalinity
    - Hydroxide alkalinity = phenolphthalein alkalinity - carbonate alkalinity



### 4. Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a solution of unknown concentration (analyte)  .
- pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H3O+]  .
- Determination of pH by titrimetric method involves using a pH meter to monitor the change in pH of the analyte solution as the titrant is added   .
- A pH meter consists of a glass electrode that responds to the hydrogen ion activity in the solution, and a reference electrode that provides a constant potential  .
- The potential difference between the two electrodes is proportional to the pH of the solution, and can be measured by a voltmeter or a potentiometer  .
- A plot of the pH of the solution versus the volume of the titrant added is called a pH curve or a titration curve  .
- The shape of the pH curve depends on the nature and strength of the acid and base involved in the titration .
- The point where the pH curve changes sharply is called the endpoint or the equivalence point, and it indicates the completion of the reaction between the titrant and the analyte  .
- The volume of the titrant at the endpoint can be used to calculate the concentration of the analyte, using the stoichiometry of the reaction and the known concentration of the titrant  .
- The pH at the endpoint depends on the type of the titration. For example, in a strong acid-strong base titration, the pH at the endpoint is 7. In a weak acid-strong base titration, the pH at the endpoint is greater than 7. In a weak base-strong acid titration, the pH at the endpoint is less than 7 .

### 5. Determination of surface tension of given liquid

- Surface tension is the property of a liquid that makes its surface behave like a stretched elastic membrane.
- Surface tension is caused by the cohesive forces between the molecules of the liquid, which tend to minimize the surface area of the liquid.
- Surface tension can be measured by various methods, such as capillary rise method, drop weight method, ring method, etc.
- One of the common methods to determine the surface tension of a given liquid is the ring method, also known as the Du Noüy method.
- In this method, a thin metal ring, attached to a spring balance, is dipped in the liquid and then pulled out slowly.
- The liquid adheres to the ring and forms a thin film, which exerts a downward force on the ring due to surface tension.
- The spring balance measures the upward force required to detach the ring from the liquid film.
- The surface tension of the liquid is given by the formula:

$$\gamma = \frac{F}{4\pi R}$$

where $\gamma$ is the surface tension, $F$ is the force measured by the spring balance, and $R$ is the radius of the ring.
- The procedure to perform the experiment is as follows:

  - Clean the ring and the vessel containing the liquid with a suitable solvent and dry them.
  - Adjust the zero of the spring balance and note the initial reading.
  - Dip the ring in the liquid and then pull it out slowly until the liquid film breaks.
  - Note the final reading of the spring balance and calculate the force as the difference between the final and initial readings.
  - Repeat the steps for at least five times and take the average value of the force.
  - Measure the diameter of the ring using a screw gauge and calculate the radius.
  - Substitute the values of the force and the radius in the formula and calculate the surface tension of the liquid.
  - Record the observations and results in a tabular form and report the surface tension with appropriate units and significant figures.



### 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer.

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied force.
- A viscometer is an instrument that measures the viscosity of a fluid by observing its flow through a narrow tube or a capillary.
- The flow rate of a fluid through a capillary depends on the pressure difference across the capillary, the length and radius of the capillary, and the viscosity of the fluid.
- The Hagen-Poiseuille equation relates these variables as follows:

`Q = (πr^4 ΔP) / (8ηL)`

where Q is the volumetric flow rate, r is the radius of the capillary, ΔP is the pressure difference, η is the viscosity, and L is the length of the capillary.

- To determine the viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer, the following steps are followed:

  - Fill the viscometer with the liquid to be tested and immerse it in a water bath at a constant temperature.
  - Allow the liquid to flow through the capillary under the influence of gravity and measure the time taken for a fixed volume of liquid to flow from one mark to another on the viscometer.
  - Repeat the experiment for different volumes of liquid and calculate the average flow time.
  - Measure the pressure difference across the capillary by using a manometer or a pressure gauge.
  - Measure the length and radius of the capillary by using a ruler or a micrometer.
  - Substitute the values of Q, r, ΔP, and L in the Hagen-Poiseuille equation and solve for η.
  - Report the viscosity of the liquid with the appropriate units and the percentage error.



### 7. Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator.

- Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a double salt of iron(II) sulfate and ammonium sulfate, with the formula (NH4)2Fe(SO4)2·6H2O.
- It is commonly used as a standard solution for titrating oxidizing agents, such as potassium permanganate, in redox reactions.
- The strength of a ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be determined by titrating it with a standard solution of potassium permanganate, using an external indicator such as starch or potassium ferricyanide.
- The reaction between ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium permanganate is as follows:

(NH4)2Fe(SO4)2·6H2O + KMnO4 + H2SO4 -> Fe2(SO4)3 + K2SO4 + (NH4)2SO4 + MnSO4 + H2O

- The end point of the titration is marked by the disappearance of the pink color of potassium permanganate, or by the appearance of a blue color due to the formation of Prussian blue when using starch or potassium ferricyanide as indicators.
- The strength of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be calculated by using the following formula:

Strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate = (Normality of KMnO4 x Volume of KMnO4 x 392) / Volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate

- Where 392 is the equivalent weight of ferrous ammonium sulfate, and normality is the number of gram equivalents of the substance per liter of solution.



### 8. Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator.

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a reducing agent such as ferrous ammonium sulphate.
- The reaction between potassium dichromate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is as follows:

`K2Cr2O7 + 6FeSO4 + 7H2SO4 -> Cr2(SO4)3 + 3K2SO4 + 6FeSO4 + 7H2O`

- The endpoint of the titration can be detected by the change of colour of the solution from green to reddish-brown, due to the formation of ferric ions.
- This colour change is the internal indicator of the titration, and no external indicator is required.
- The strength of potassium dichromate can be calculated by using the following formula:

`Strength of K2Cr2O7 = (Volume of FeSO4 x Normality of FeSO4 x Equivalent weight of K2Cr2O7) / Volume of K2Cr2O7`

- The equivalent weight of potassium dichromate is 49.03 g/mol, and the normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate is 0.1 N.
- The procedure of the experiment is as follows:

  - Prepare a standard solution of ferrous ammonium sulphate by dissolving 3.92 g of the salt in 250 mL of distilled water.
  - Transfer 25 mL of this solution to a conical flask and add 10 mL of dilute sulphuric acid.
  - Prepare a solution of potassium dichromate by dissolving about 2.5 g of the salt in 250 mL of distilled water.
  - Fill a burette with this solution and note the initial reading.
  - Titrate the ferrous ammonium sulphate solution with potassium dichromate solution until the colour changes from green to reddish-brown.
  - Note the final reading of the burette and calculate the volume of potassium dichromate used.
  - Repeat the titration for two more times and take the average volume of potassium dichromate used.
  - Calculate the strength of potassium dichromate by using the formula given above.



### 9. Determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder

Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium hypochlorite [Ca(OCl)<sub>2</sub>] and calcium chloride [CaCl<sub>2</sub>]. It is used as a disinfectant and a bleaching agent. The available chlorine in bleaching powder is the amount of chlorine that can be liberated when the powder is treated with dilute acids. The available chlorine is expressed as a percentage of the mass of the bleaching powder.

The principle of the determination is based on the reaction of bleaching powder with excess iodide in acidic medium to produce iodine, which is then titrated with a standard solution of sodium thiosulfate (hypo) using starch as an indicator.

The reactions involved are:

Ca(OCl)<sub>2</sub> + 2HCl → CaCl<sub>2</sub> + H<sub>2</sub>O + Cl<sub>2</sub>

Cl<sub>2</sub> + 2KI → 2KCl + I<sub>2</sub>

I<sub>2</sub> + 2Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub> → Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>4</sub>O<sub>6</sub> + 2NaI

The procedure for the determination is as follows:

- Weigh accurately about 1 g of bleaching powder and transfer it to a 250 mL conical flask.
- Add about 100 mL of distilled water and shake well to dissolve the powder.
- Add 10 mL of 10% potassium iodide solution and 10 mL of 1 M hydrochloric acid and swirl the flask to mix the contents.
- Titrate the liberated iodine with 0.1 N sodium thiosulfate solution using a burette until the solution becomes pale yellow.
- Add a few drops of starch solution as an indicator and continue the titration until the blue color disappears.
- Record the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used as V<sub>1</sub> mL.
- Repeat the procedure with a blank titration using distilled water instead of bleaching powder and record the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used as V<sub>2</sub> mL.
- Calculate the percentage of available chlorine in the bleaching powder using the formula:

% available chlorine = (V<sub>2</sub> - V<sub>1</sub>) x N x 35.45 x 100 / W

where N is the normality of sodium thiosulfate solution, W is the mass of bleaching powder taken in grams, and 35.45 is the equivalent mass of chlorine.



### 10. Determination of chloride content in water sample

- Chloride is one of the major anions found in water and wastewater. It originates from natural sources such as seawater, salt deposits, and mineral springs, as well as from human activities such as the use of salt for deicing, agricultural runoff, industrial effluents, and domestic sewage.
- Chloride can affect the taste, corrosion, and biological activity of water. The World Health Organization (WHO) recommends a guideline value of 250 mg/L for chloride in drinking water, based on aesthetic considerations.
- Chloride can be determined by various methods, such as titration, colorimetry, ion chromatography, and ion-selective electrodes. One of the most common and simple methods is the Mohr method, which is a precipitation titration using silver nitrate as the titrant and potassium chromate as the indicator.
- The principle of the Mohr method is that silver nitrate reacts with chloride ions in a 1:1 ratio to form a white precipitate of silver chloride, according to the equation:

    AgNO<sub>3</sub> + Cl<sup>-</sup> → AgCl<sub>(s)</sub> + NO<sub>3</sub><sup>-</sup>

- The endpoint of the titration is detected by the formation of a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate, which occurs when all the chloride ions have been consumed and excess silver ions react with chromate ions, according to the equation:

    2AgNO<sub>3</sub> + K<sub>2</sub>CrO<sub>4</sub> → Ag<sub>2</sub>CrO<sub>4</sub><sub>(s)</sub> + 2KNO<sub>3</sub>

- The Mohr method is applicable for water samples with chloride concentrations between 10 and 1000 mg/L. The sample should have a neutral or slightly alkaline pH (7-10) and should not contain interfering substances such as bromide, iodide, cyanide, sulfide, or thiocyanate, which can also precipitate with silver nitrate.

- The procedure for the Mohr method is as follows  :

    1. Take 50 mL of sample (V mL) and dilute to 100 mL with distilled water in a volumetric flask.
    2. If the sample is highly colored, add 3 mL of aluminum hydroxide and shake well, allow to settle, filter, wash and collect filtrate.
    3. Bring the sample pH to 7-8 by adding acid or alkali if necessary, using pH paper or a pH meter to check.
    4. Transfer 50 mL of the sample to a conical flask and add 1 mL of potassium chromate indicator (5% w/v solution).
    5. Titrate the solution against standard silver nitrate solution (0.01 or 0.02 N) until a reddish-brown precipitate is obtained. Record the volume of silver nitrate used (T mL).
    6. Repeat the titration with a blank (distilled water) and a standard (sodium chloride solution of known concentration) to check the accuracy and precision of the method.

- The chloride concentration in the sample can be calculated from the following formula:

    C = (T - B) x N x 35.45 x 1000 / V

    where C is the chloride concentration in mg/L, T is the volume of silver nitrate used for the sample in mL, B is the volume of silver nitrate used for the blank in mL, N is the normality of the silver nitrate solution, and V is the volume of the sample taken for titration in mL.

- The result can be reported as mg/L of chloride or meq/L of chloride by dividing by the equivalent weight of chloride (35.45).

- The sources of error and uncertainty in the Mohr method include:

    - Inaccurate preparation and standardization of the silver nitrate solution
    - Incomplete precipitation or co-precipitation of silver chloride
    - Imprecise measurement of the sample volume and the titrant volume
    - Incorrect pH adjustment of the sample
    - Interference from other anions or cations in the sample
    - Subjective detection of the endpoint color change

- The Mohr method can be modified or replaced by other methods to overcome some of these limitations,



### 11. Preparation of Phenol formaldehyde (PF) resin

Phenol formaldehyde (PF) resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the condensation reaction of phenol and formaldehyde. PF resin has high mechanical strength, chemical resistance, and thermal stability. It is widely used as an adhesive, coating, and molding material.

The preparation of PF resin involves the following steps:

- **Step 1**: Mixing of phenol and formaldehyde in a reaction vessel. The molar ratio of phenol to formaldehyde determines the degree of cross-linking and the properties of the final resin. A 1:1 ratio produces linear chains, while a higher ratio produces branched chains .
- **Step 2**: Catalysis of the reaction by either an acid or a base. The catalyst affects the rate, temperature, and pH of the reaction, as well as the structure and solubility of the resin. Common catalysts are hydrochloric acid, sulfuric acid, or ammonia .
- **Step 3**: Polymerization of the phenol and formaldehyde molecules by the formation of methylene bridges (-CH2-) between the phenolic hydroxyl groups. The reaction can be either a one-step process (resole) or a two-step process (novolac). Resole resins are self-curing and have a higher degree of cross-linking, while novolac resins require a curing agent and have a lower degree of cross-linking.
- **Step 4**: Isolation and purification of the resin by filtration, washing, and drying. The resin can be further modified by adding fillers, plasticizers, or other additives to improve its properties.
- **Step 5**: Application of the resin as an adhesive, coating, or molding material. The resin can be cured by heat, pressure, or radiation, depending on the type and purpose of the resin.



### 12. Preparation of Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin

Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects. It is produced from urea and formaldehyde by a condensation reaction under alkaline or acidic conditions.

The preparation of UF resin can be divided into three main steps:

1. **Methylolation**: Urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 2-3:1 at pH 6-11 and heated to at least 80°C. This results in the formation of monomethylolurea and dimethylolurea, which are the main monomers of UF resin. The reaction is reversible and depends on the temperature, pH, and concentration of the reactants.    

2. **Condensation**: The methylolureas react further with urea and formaldehyde to form linear and branched oligomers and polymers. The reaction can be catalyzed by adding a mineral or organic acid (such as sulfuric acid, hydrochloric acid, or formic acid) to lower the pH to 0.5-3.5. The degree of polymerization and the molecular weight distribution of the resin depend on the reaction time, temperature, pH, and catalyst. The condensation reaction is also reversible and can be stopped by cooling or neutralizing the mixture.    

3. **Curing**: The UF resin is applied to a substrate (such as wood or paper) and heated under pressure to form a cross-linked network. The curing process involves the elimination of water and formaldehyde from the resin molecules, which results in the formation of methylene and methylene ether bridges between the urea units. The curing conditions (such as temperature, pressure, and time) affect the properties and performance of the final product. The cured UF resin has high strength, hardness, and resistance to heat and chemicals, but also emits formaldehyde, which can cause health and environmental problems.

### 14. Determination of Cell Conductance of a solution

- Cell conductance of a solution is the measure of how well the solution can conduct electric current.
- It is the reciprocal of the resistance of the solution, which depends on the concentration, charge and mobility of the ions in the solution.
- To measure the cell conductance of a solution, a special device called a conductance cell is used. It consists of two electrodes of known area and distance, immersed in the solution.
- The resistance of the solution between the electrodes is measured by using a Wheatstone bridge or a conductivity meter.
- The cell constant, K, is the ratio of the distance between the electrodes, l, to the area of cross-section of the electrodes, a. It has the units of cm<sup>-1</sup>.
- The cell constant is determined by using a solution of known specific conductance, such as KCl. The specific conductance, κ, is the conductance of a solution of unit length and unit cross-sectional area. It has the units of S cm<sup>-1</sup>.
- The cell conductance, G, is related to the specific conductance, κ, by the equation G = κK. Therefore, by measuring the cell conductance and knowing the cell constant, the specific conductance of any solution can be calculated.
- The specific conductance of a solution varies with the concentration of the solution. The molar conductance, Λ<sub>m</sub>, is the conductance of a solution containing one mole of the solute in a given volume of the solution. It has the units of S cm<sup>2</sup> mol<sup>-1</sup>.
- The molar conductance, Λ<sub>m</sub>, is related to the specific conductance, κ, by the equation Λ<sub>m</sub> = κ/M, where M is the molarity of the solution.
- The molar conductance of a solution increases with the decrease in concentration, as the ions become more free to move in the solution. The limiting molar conductance, Λ<sub>m</sub><sup>0</sup>, is the molar conductance of a solution at infinite dilution, when the ions are completely dissociated and do not interact with each other.
- The limiting molar conductance of a solution can be calculated by using the Kohlrausch's law, which states that the limiting molar conductance of an electrolyte is equal to the sum of the limiting molar conductances of the cation and the anion of the electrolyte. For example, Λ<sub>m</sub><sup>0</sup>(NaCl) = Λ<sub>m</sub><sup>0</sup>(Na<sup>+</sup>) + Λ<sub>m</sub><sup>0</sup>(Cl<sup>-</sup>).
- The limiting molar conductances of the ions can be determined experimentally by using the conductance measurements of different electrolytes and applying the Kohlrausch's law.

### 15. Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

- Esters are organic compounds that have the general formula RCOOR', where R and R' are alkyl or aryl groups.
- Esters can be hydrolyzed by water in the presence of an acid or a base catalyst to form carboxylic acids and alcohols.
- The hydrolysis of esters is a reversible reaction and follows the first-order kinetics with respect to the ester concentration.
- The rate constant of hydrolysis of esters can be determined by measuring the change in the concentration of the ester or the products over time, using various methods such as titration, spectrophotometry, or conductometry.
- One of the common methods to determine the rate constant of hydrolysis of esters is by titrating the hydrolyzed solution with a standard alkali solution and using phenolphthalein as an indicator.
- The procedure for this method is as follows:

  - Prepare a known volume and concentration of the ester solution in a conical flask and add a few drops of phenolphthalein indicator.
  - Add a known volume and concentration of the acid catalyst (usually HCl) to the flask and start the stopwatch.
  - Swirl the flask gently and periodically titrate a small aliquot of the solution with a standard alkali solution (usually NaOH) until a faint pink color persists for 30 seconds.
  - Record the volume of the alkali solution used and the time elapsed for each titration.
  - Repeat the titration at regular intervals until the ester is completely hydrolyzed.
  - Plot a graph of the volume of the alkali solution used versus the time elapsed.
  - The slope of the graph gives the rate of hydrolysis of the ester, which is equal to the rate constant multiplied by the initial concentration of the ester.
  - The rate constant can be calculated by dividing the slope by the initial concentration of the ester.
  - The rate constant can also be calculated by using the integrated rate equation for the first-order reaction:

    - ln([ester]t/[ester]0) = -kt
    - where [ester]t is the concentration of the ester at time t, [ester]0 is the initial concentration of the ester, k is the rate constant, and t is the time elapsed.
    - By plotting a graph of ln([ester]t/[ester]0) versus t, the slope gives the negative value of the rate constant.



### 16. Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

- Element detection is the process of determining the presence or absence of certain elements in an organic compound, such as carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, oxygen, halogens, sulphur and phosphorus.
- Identification of functional groups is the process of determining the type and location of the specific groups of atoms that are responsible for the characteristic chemical reactions of an organic compound, such as alcohols, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids, amines, etc.
- Element detection and identification of functional groups are important steps in the qualitative analysis of organic compounds, which helps to establish their structure and properties.
- Element detection can be done by various methods, such as:
  - Lassaigne's test: A general test for the detection of nitrogen, sulphur and halogens in an organic compound. It involves fusing the compound with sodium metal and extracting the sodium salts of the elements in aqueous solution. The solution is then tested for the presence of cyanide, sulphide and halide ions by various reagents.
  - Carbon and hydrogen estimation: A method to determine the percentage of carbon and hydrogen in an organic compound by burning a known mass of the compound in excess oxygen and measuring the masses of carbon dioxide and water produced.
  - Oxygen estimation: A method to determine the percentage of oxygen in an organic compound by subtracting the percentages of carbon, hydrogen and other elements from 100.
- Identification of functional groups can be done by various tests, such as:
  - Alcoholic –OH group: A functional group that consists of a hydroxyl group attached to an alkyl group. It can be detected by the following tests:
    - Lucas test: A test that involves adding a few drops of the compound to Lucas reagent (a mixture of zinc chloride and concentrated hydrochloric acid) and observing the formation of a turbid solution or a separate layer of alkyl chloride. The rate of reaction depends on the type of alcohol: tertiary alcohols react immediately, secondary alcohols react within 5 minutes, and primary alcohols do not react at room temperature.
    - Esterification test: A test that involves heating the compound with acetic acid and a few drops of concentrated sulphuric acid and smelling the resulting mixture for the fruity odour of an ester.
    - Iodoform test: A test that involves adding a few drops of the compound to a solution of iodine and sodium hydroxide and observing the formation of a yellow precipitate of iodoform. The test is positive for alcohols that have a methyl group attached to the carbon bearing the hydroxyl group, such as ethanol, propan-2-ol, etc.
  - Carbonyls (Aldehydes and Ketones): Functional groups that consist of a carbon atom double-bonded to an oxygen atom. They can be detected by the following tests:
    - 2,4-dinitrophenyl hydrazine test: A test that involves adding a small amount of the compound to 2,4-dinitrophenyl hydrazine reagent and shaking well. The formation of a yellow, orange or red precipitate of 2,4-dinitrophenyl hydrazone indicates the presence of a carbonyl group. The precipitate can be further purified and recrystallized to determine the melting point and identify the specific carbonyl compound.
    - Tollen's test: A test that involves adding a few drops of the compound to a freshly prepared solution of Tollen's reagent (a mixture of silver nitrate and ammonia) and warming gently. The formation of a silver mirror on the inner surface of the test tube indicates the presence of an aldehyde group. Ketones do not react with Tollen's reagent.
    - Fehling's test: A test that involves adding a few drops of the compound to a solution of Fehling's reagent (a mixture of copper sulphate and sodium hydroxide) and heating. The formation of a red precipitate of copper oxide indicates the presence of an aldehyde group. Ketones do not react with Fehling's reagent.
  - Aldehydes: A functional group that consists of a carbonyl group attached to a hydrogen atom or an alkyl group. They can be detected by the following tests:
    - Benedict's test: A test that involves adding a few drops of the compound to a solution of Benedict's

### Course Outcomes Bloom's Taxonomy

- Course outcomes are brief statements that describe what students will be expected to learn by the end of the course.
- Course outcomes should be aligned with the course content, activities, and assessments, and should reflect the level of cognitive skills that students need to demonstrate.
- Bloom's taxonomy is a model of cognitive skills that can be used to classify educational learning objectives and to design course outcomes .
- Bloom's taxonomy consists of six levels of cognition, from lower to higher: remember, understand, apply, analyze, evaluate, and create.
- Each level of cognition has a set of verbs that can be used to write course outcomes that indicate the expected level of student learning .
- For example, at the remember level, verbs such as define, list, recall, and identify can be used to write course outcomes that require students to recall factual information .
- At the create level, verbs such as design, construct, produce, and synthesize can be used to write course outcomes that require students to generate new ideas or products .
- Course outcomes should be specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART) to ensure clarity and accountability.
- Course outcomes should also be student-centered, meaning that they focus on what the students will do, not what the instructor will do.
- Course outcomes should be reviewed and revised periodically to ensure that they reflect the current needs and expectations of the students, the instructor, and the discipline.

### Level

- Level is a concept that measures the position of a point or a surface relative to a reference datum, such as the mean sea level or a horizontal plane.
- Level can also refer to the instrument or device that is used to determine or indicate the level of something, such as a spirit level, a water level, or a laser level.
- Level can also mean the degree of proficiency, skill, or attainment in something, such as a level of education, a level of difficulty, or a level of security.
- Some examples of level in different contexts are:

  - In geography, level can be used to describe the elevation or altitude of a place, such as the sea level, the ground level, or the mountain level.
  - In mathematics, level can be used to describe the rank or order of a set, a function, or a structure, such as the level of a polynomial, the level of a group, or the level of a hierarchy.
  - In physics, level can be used to describe the energy or potential of a system, a particle, or a field, such as the level of a quantum state, the level of a nucleus, or the level of a gravitational field.
  - In chemistry, level can be used to describe the concentration or amount of a substance, a compound, or a reaction, such as the level of a solution, the level of a pH, or the level of a catalyst.
  - In biology, level can be used to describe the organization or complexity of a living thing, a tissue, or a process, such as the level of a cell, the level of a gene, or the level of a metabolism.
  - In psychology, level can be used to describe the stage or phase of a mental process, a behavior, or a personality, such as the level of a cognition, the level of a motivation, or the level of a trait.
  - In sociology, level can be used to describe the position or status of a person, a group, or a society, such as the level of a class, the level of a culture, or the level of a development.
  - In linguistics, level can be used to describe the unit or layer of a language, a speech, or a communication, such as the level of a sound, the level of a word, or the level of a discourse.
  - In gaming, level can be used to describe the stage or section of a game, a character, or a challenge, such as the level of a map, the level of a skill, or the level of a difficulty.

- Level is a versatile and useful concept that can be applied to various fields and domains of knowledge. It can help us to compare, measure, classify, and analyze different phenomena and aspects of reality.

### CO2 Molecular Properties and Surface Tension

- Carbon dioxide (CO2) is a linear molecule with a bond length of 116.3 pm and a bond angle of 180 degrees.
- CO2 is a non-polar molecule with a dipole moment of zero and a symmetrical distribution of electrons.
- CO2 has a molar mass of 44.0095 g/mol and a critical point at 304.25 K and 7.38 MPa.
- CO2 is a gas at standard temperature and pressure, but can be liquefied or solidified under certain conditions.

- The surface tension of CO2 is the energy required to increase the surface area of the liquid phase due to intermolecular forces.
- The surface tension of CO2 depends on the temperature, pressure, and the presence of other substances in the solution .
- The surface tension of CO2 can be measured using various methods, such as the pendant drop method, the Wilhelmy plate method, or the capillary rise method.

Hello, I am Sydney, your AI assistant. I can help you with your query. Here is some information on the topic of viscosity, conductance of solution, chloride and iron content in the water.

### Viscosity
- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to deformation under shear stress. It is also known as the internal friction of a fluid.
- Viscosity depends on the temperature, pressure, and composition of the fluid. In general, viscosity decreases with increasing temperature and decreases with increasing pressure.
- Viscosity also depends on the molecular structure and interactions of the fluid. For example, water has a low viscosity because its molecules are small and have weak intermolecular forces. On the other hand, honey has a high viscosity because its molecules are large and have strong hydrogen bonds.
- Viscosity can be measured by various methods, such as capillary viscometer, rotational viscometer, or falling ball viscometer. The unit of viscosity is Pascal-second (Pa s) or milliPascal-second (mPa s).

### Conductance of solution
- Conductance of solution is a measure of the ability of a solution to conduct electric current. It is also known as the electrical conductivity of a solution.
- Conductance of solution depends on the concentration, temperature, and type of solute and solvent in the solution. In general, conductance increases with increasing concentration and temperature, and decreases with increasing viscosity.
- Conductance of solution also depends on the nature and mobility of the ions in the solution. For example, strong electrolytes, such as sodium chloride, have a high conductance because they dissociate completely into ions that can move freely in the solution. On the other hand, weak electrolytes, such as acetic acid, have a low conductance because they dissociate partially into ions that are less mobile in the solution.
- Conductance of solution can be measured by various methods, such as Wheatstone bridge, conductivity meter, or potentiometer. The unit of conductance is Siemens (S) or microSiemens (µS).

### Chloride and iron content in the water
- Chloride and iron are two common elements that can be found in natural or tap water. They can affect the quality and properties of the water, such as taste, odor, color, corrosion, and hardness.
- Chloride is a negatively charged ion that can originate from various sources, such as seawater, rock salt, road salt, or industrial waste. Chloride can increase the conductance and salinity of the water, and can also cause corrosion of metal pipes and fittings. The recommended limit for chloride in drinking water is 250 mg/L, according to the World Health Organization (WHO).
- Iron is a positively charged ion that can originate from various sources, such as soil, rocks, iron pipes, or groundwater. Iron can decrease the conductance and increase the hardness of the water, and can also cause staining of clothes, fixtures, and appliances. The recommended limit for iron in drinking water is 0.3 mg/L, according to the WHO.
- Chloride and iron content in the water can be measured by various methods, such as titration, colorimetry, spectrophotometry, or ion chromatography. The unit of chloride and iron content is milligram per liter (mg/L) or parts per million (ppm).

### K3

- K3 is a type of surface in mathematics that is named after three mathematicians: Kummer, Kähler, and Kodaira.
- A K3 surface is a complex algebraic surface that has no singularities, no curves, and a trivial canonical bundle. It also has a finite group of automorphisms and a Hodge diamond with only one non-zero entry in each row and column.
- K3 surfaces are important examples of Calabi-Yau manifolds, which are spaces that admit a Ricci-flat metric and have applications in string theory and mirror symmetry.
- K3 surfaces can be constructed in various ways, such as by taking a quartic surface in projective space, a double cover of the projective plane branched along a sextic curve, or a Kummer surface associated to an abelian surface.
- K3 surfaces have many interesting properties and invariants, such as the Picard number, the Euler characteristic, the Chern classes, the moduli space, the period map, the Torelli theorem, the global Torelli theorem, the Hodge conjecture, the Tate conjecture, and the Artin-Tate conjecture.



### CO-3 Measure the hardness and alkalinity of the water. K3

- Hardness and alkalinity are two important parameters of water quality that indicate the concentration of dissolved minerals and bases in water respectively.
- Hardness is caused by compounds of calcium and magnesium, and by a variety of other metals, such as iron, manganese, and zinc. Hard water can cause scaling, corrosion, and reduced efficiency of water appliances and pipes.
- Alkalinity measures the total amount of bases present in water, mainly bicarbonate, carbonate, and hydroxide ions. Alkalinity can buffer the water against pH changes and acidification. Alkalinity is related to hardness through the common ions of calcium and magnesium that are associated with bicarbonate and carbonate.
- To measure the hardness and alkalinity of water, titration methods are commonly used. Titration is a technique that involves adding a known amount of a solution with a known concentration (titrant) to a sample with an unknown concentration (analyte) until a color change or an endpoint is reached.
- To measure the hardness of water, a titrant of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) is used, which forms a complex with the hardness ions and changes the color of an indicator (such as eriochrome black T) from red to blue at the endpoint. The amount of EDTA used is proportional to the hardness of the water.
- To measure the alkalinity of water, a titrant of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) is used, which neutralizes the bases in the water and changes the color of an indicator (such as phenolphthalein or bromcresol green) from pink or blue to colorless at the endpoint. The amount of H2SO4 used is proportional to the alkalinity of the water.
- The units of hardness and alkalinity are usually expressed as milligrams per liter (mg/L) or parts per million (ppm) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3) equivalent . The classification of water hardness is as follows:

  - Soft: 0 to 60 mg/L as CaCO3
  - Moderately hard: 61 to 120 mg/L as CaCO3
  - Hard: 121 to 180 mg/L as CaCO3
  - Very hard: more than 180 mg/L as CaCO3

- The classification of water alkalinity is as follows:

  - Low: less than 20 mg/L as CaCO3
  - Moderate: 20 to 200 mg/L as CaCO3
  - High: more than 200 mg/L as CaCO3



### CO-4 Know the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol

Phenol is an aromatic compound with a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a benzene ring. It is also known as carbolic acid or phenic acid. Phenol has antiseptic and disinfectant properties and is used in the manufacture of plastics, dyes, explosives, and drugs.

There are several methods of preparing phenol from different sources, such as haloarenes, benzene, cumene, diazonium salts, and coal tar. Some of the common methods are:

- **From haloarenes:** Haloarenes are aromatic compounds with a halogen atom (-X) attached to a benzene ring. Examples of haloarenes are chlorobenzene, bromobenzene, and iodobenzene. When a haloarene is fused with sodium hydroxide (NaOH) at high temperature and pressure, sodium phenoxide (NaOC6H5) is produced. Sodium phenoxide is then acidified with dilute acid or carbon dioxide (CO2) to form phenol and sodium salt. This method is also known as the Dow process.

  - Example: Chlorobenzene + NaOH → Sodium phenoxide + NaCl
  - Sodium phenoxide + HCl → Phenol + NaCl

- **From benzene:** Benzene is a simple aromatic compound with six carbon atoms and six hydrogen atoms. When benzene is treated with concentrated sulfuric acid (H2SO4), it forms benzene sulfonic acid (C6H5SO3H). Benzene sulfonic acid is then fused with sodium hydroxide at high temperature to form sodium phenoxide, which is acidified to form phenol.

  - Example: Benzene + H2SO4 → Benzene sulfonic acid + H2O
  - Benzene sulfonic acid + NaOH → Sodium phenoxide + Na2SO4
  - Sodium phenoxide + HCl → Phenol + NaCl

- **From cumene:** Cumene is an organic compound with the formula C6H5CH(CH3)2. It is also known as isopropylbenzene. When cumene is oxidized by air in the presence of a catalyst, it forms cumene hydroperoxide (C6H5C(CH3)2OOH). Cumene hydroperoxide is then cleaved by heating with dilute acid to form phenol and acetone (CH3COCH3). This method is also known as the cumene process.

  - Example: Cumene + O2 → Cumene hydroperoxide
  - Cumene hydroperoxide + H2O → Phenol + Acetone

- **From diazonium salts:** Diazonium salts are organic compounds with the general formula R-N2+X-, where R is an aryl group and X is a halide ion. They are formed by the reaction of an aromatic amine (R-NH2) with nitrous acid (HNO2) in cold acidic solution. When a diazonium salt is treated with water, it forms phenol and nitrogen gas (N2).

  - Example: Aniline + HNO2 + HCl → Benzenediazonium chloride + H2O
  - Benzenediazonium chloride + H2O → Phenol + N2 + HCl

- **From coal tar:** Coal tar is a dark brown liquid obtained from the destructive distillation of coal. It contains many aromatic compounds, including phenol. Phenol can be extracted from coal tar by fractional distillation, followed by purification. This method is also known as the coal tar process.

  - Example: Coal → Coal tar + Coke + Coal gas
  - Coal tar → Phenol + Other aromatic compounds

### Formaldehyde
- Formaldehyde is a naturally occurring organic compound with the formula CH2O and structure H−CHO.
- Formaldehyde is a colorless, highly toxic, and flammable gas at room temperature.
- Formaldehyde is an intermediate in the oxidation (or combustion) of methane, as well as of other carbon compounds, e.g. in forest fires, automobile exhaust, and tobacco smoke.
- Formaldehyde is also produced by the action of sunlight and oxygen on atmospheric methane and other hydrocarbons, and becomes part of smog.
- Formaldehyde is used in the production of fertilizer, paper, plywood, and some resins.
- Formaldehyde is also used as a food preservative and in household products, such as antiseptics, medicines, and cosmetics.
- Formaldehyde can cause irritation of the eyes, nose, and throat, as well as respiratory problems, skin rashes, and allergic reactions.
- Formaldehyde is classified as a human carcinogen by the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) and the US National Toxicology Program (NTP) .
- Formaldehyde exposure is mainly from inhaling it in the air or absorbing it through the skin.
- Formaldehyde levels in indoor air can vary depending on the sources, ventilation, temperature, and humidity.
- Formaldehyde levels can be reduced by using products that contain low or no formaldehyde, increasing ventilation, and maintaining moderate temperature and humidity.

### K3

- K3 is a type of surface in mathematics that is named after three mathematicians: Kummer, Kähler, and Kodaira.
- A K3 surface is a complex algebraic surface that has no singularities, no curves, and a trivial canonical bundle. It also has a finite group of symmetries called the Picard group.
- K3 surfaces are important in various branches of mathematics and physics, such as algebraic geometry, number theory, string theory, and mirror symmetry.
- Some examples of K3 surfaces are:

  - The quartic surface in projective 3-space defined by the equation x^4 + y^4 + z^4 + w^4 = 0.
  - The intersection of two quadrics in projective 4-space, such as x^2 + y^2 + z^2 + w^2 = 0 and xw - yz = 0.
  - The resolution of singularities of the cubic surface in projective 3-space defined by the equation x^3 + y^3 + z^3 + w^3 = 0.

- K3 surfaces have many interesting properties and open problems, such as:

  - The Hodge conjecture, which predicts that every algebraic cycle on a K3 surface is a linear combination of divisors.
  - The Torelli theorem, which states that a K3 surface is uniquely determined by its period, which is a complex vector in a 20-dimensional space.
  - The moduli space of K3 surfaces, which is the space of all K3 surfaces up to deformation. It is a 20-dimensional complex manifold that has a natural metric called the Weil-Petersson metric.

### CO-5 Estimate the rate constant of reaction. K3

- The rate constant of a reaction, K3, is a measure of how fast the reaction proceeds at a given temperature and concentration of reactants.
- The rate constant of a reaction can be estimated by using the Arrhenius equation, which relates K3 to the activation energy, Ea, and the temperature, T, as follows:

  K3 = A exp(-Ea/RT)

  where A is the pre-exponential factor, R is the gas constant, and exp is the exponential function.

- The activation energy, Ea, is the minimum amount of energy required for the reactants to overcome the energy barrier and form products. It can be determined experimentally by measuring the rate constant of the reaction at different temperatures and plotting the natural logarithm of K3 versus the inverse of T. The slope of the plot is equal to -Ea/R, and the intercept is equal to ln A.

- The pre-exponential factor, A, is a constant that depends on the frequency of collisions and the orientation of the reactants. It can be estimated by using the collision theory, which assumes that the reaction occurs when the reactants collide with sufficient energy and proper orientation. The collision theory gives the following expression for A:

  A = Z f

  where Z is the collision frequency and f is the fraction of collisions with proper orientation.

- The collision frequency, Z, is the number of collisions per unit time per unit volume. It can be calculated by using the kinetic theory of gases, which gives the following expression for Z:

  Z = N^2 sqrt(8 k T / pi m)

  where N is the number of molecules per unit volume, k is the Boltzmann constant, and m is the average molecular mass of the reactants.

- The fraction of collisions with proper orientation, f, is the probability that the reactants collide in a way that allows the reaction to occur. It can be estimated by using the steric factor, P, which is a dimensionless number that accounts for the spatial arrangement of the reactants. The steric factor can be determined by comparing the experimental rate constant of the reaction with the theoretical rate constant predicted by the collision theory. The steric factor is given by:

  P = K3 / A

  where K3 and A are the experimental and theoretical rate constants, respectively.

- The history of Rome
- How to play chess

# Page 29 of 40

## Topic: Introduction to Artificial Intelligence

- Artificial intelligence (AI) is the branch of computer science that deals with creating machines or software that can perform tasks that normally require human intelligence, such as reasoning, learning, planning, decision making, natural language processing, computer vision, etc.
- AI can be classified into two main categories: weak AI and strong AI. Weak AI, also known as narrow AI, is the type of AI that is designed to perform a specific task or domain, such as playing chess, recognizing faces, or driving a car. Strong AI, also known as general AI or artificial general intelligence (AGI), is the type of AI that can perform any intellectual task that a human can, and potentially surpass human intelligence. Strong AI is still a hypothetical concept and has not been achieved yet.
- AI can also be classified based on the degree of human intervention or guidance required for its functioning: supervised learning, unsupervised learning, semi-supervised learning, and reinforcement learning. Supervised learning is the type of AI that learns from labeled data, such as images with captions or texts with categories. Unsupervised learning is the type of AI that learns from unlabeled data, such as images without captions or texts without categories. Semi-supervised learning is the type of AI that learns from a combination of labeled and unlabeled data, such as images with some captions or texts with some categories. Reinforcement learning is the type of AI that learns from its own actions and feedback, such as a robot that learns to walk or a game agent that learns to play.
- AI can also be classified based on the level of transparency or explainability of its inner workings: black-box AI and white-box AI. Black-box AI is the type of AI that produces outputs without revealing how or why it arrived at them, such as a neural network that classifies images or a deep learning model that generates text. White-box AI is the type of AI that produces outputs with clear and understandable explanations of how or why it arrived at them, such as a decision tree that classifies data or a rule-based system that generates text.

# ENGINEERING PHYSICS LAB KCS

- Engineering physics is an interdisciplinary field that combines physics and engineering to solve practical problems and develop new technologies.
- Engineering physics lab is a course that provides hands-on experience and training in various aspects of engineering physics, such as optics, mechanics, electronics, materials, and instrumentation.
- KCS is an acronym for Kennedy College of Sciences, which is a college of the University of Massachusetts Lowell that offers undergraduate and graduate programs in science and engineering disciplines.
- The objectives of the engineering physics lab course are to:
  - Develop experimental skills and techniques for measuring physical quantities and analyzing data.
  - Apply theoretical concepts and principles of physics to engineering problems and applications.
  - Enhance critical thinking and problem-solving abilities through designing, conducting, and reporting experiments.
  - Foster teamwork and communication skills through working in groups and presenting results.
- The topics covered in the engineering physics lab course may vary depending on the instructor and the curriculum, but some possible examples are:
  - Interference and diffraction of light
  - Polarization and optical activity
  - Spectroscopy and atomic physics
  - Photoelectric effect and solar cells
  - Hooke's law and simple harmonic motion
  - Newton's laws and conservation of momentum
  - Rotational dynamics and angular momentum
  - Electrical circuits and Kirchhoff's laws
  - Ohm's law and resistivity
  - Capacitance and RC circuits
  - Inductance and RL circuits
  - AC circuits and resonance
  - Magnetic fields and forces
  - Electromagnetic induction and Faraday's law
  - Oscilloscopes and function generators
  - Transistors and logic gates
  - Diodes and LEDs
  - Thermodynamics and heat transfer
  - Specific heat and calorimetry
  - Thermal expansion and stress
  - Young's modulus and elasticity
  - Sound waves and resonance
  - Doppler effect and ultrasound
  - Fluid mechanics and Bernoulli's principle
  - Surface tension and capillarity
  - Optical fibers and waveguides
  - Lasers and holography
  - Nanotechnology and nanomaterials
- The engineering physics lab course typically consists of weekly sessions that involve pre-lab assignments, lab experiments, data analysis, and lab reports. The pre-lab assignments are designed to prepare the students for the lab experiments by reviewing the relevant theory and concepts, and by answering some questions. The lab experiments are conducted in groups of two or three students, using the equipment and instruments provided in the lab. The data analysis involves processing and interpreting the experimental data, using appropriate graphs, tables, and calculations. The lab reports are written documents that summarize the objectives, procedures, results, and conclusions of the lab experiments, following a specific format and style. The lab reports are graded based on the accuracy, completeness, clarity, and originality of the content.
- The engineering physics lab course is a valuable and rewarding experience for students who are interested in pursuing a career or further education in engineering or physics. It helps them to develop the essential skills and knowledge that are required for working in the modern and dynamic fields of science and technology.

# To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles, applications, or techniques, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments can be used for various purposes, such as quality control, research and development, environmental monitoring, forensic analysis, clinical diagnosis, etc.
- Analytical instruments consist of three main components: a sample introduction system, a detector, and a data processing system.
- A sample introduction system is responsible for preparing, transporting, and introducing the sample into the detector. It can include devices such as syringes, pumps, valves, columns, nebulizers, etc.
- A detector is the part of the instrument that converts the physical, chemical, or biological signal from the sample into an electrical signal that can be measured and recorded. It can include devices such as photodiodes, electrodes, ion sources, etc.
- A data processing system is the part of the instrument that analyzes, displays, and stores the data obtained from the detector. It can include devices such as computers, software, printers, etc.
