# Analysis of chloride content, hardness, alkalinity

- Chloride content is the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in a water sample. Chloride ions are one of the major dissolved constituents of natural waters and can originate from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, or road salt.
- Hardness is the measure of the ability of water to precipitate soap. Hard water contains high concentrations of calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) ions, which react with soap to form insoluble scum. Hardness can also cause scaling and corrosion of pipes and boilers, and affect the taste and quality of water.
- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. Alkaline water contains high concentrations of bicarbonate (HCO3-), carbonate (CO32-), and hydroxide (OH-) ions, which can buffer the pH of water and prevent acidification. Alkalinity can also affect the solubility of metals and minerals, and the biological activity of water.

## Methods of analysis

- Chloride content can be determined by titration with silver nitrate (AgNO3) solution, using potassium chromate (K2CrO4) as an indicator. The end point of the titration is marked by the formation of a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2CrO4).
- Hardness can be determined by titration with ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) solution, using eriochrome black T (EBT) as an indicator. The end point of the titration is marked by the change of color of the indicator from wine red to blue.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titration with sulfuric acid (H2SO4) solution, using phenolphthalein and methyl orange as indicators. The end point of the titration with phenolphthalein is marked by the change of color of the indicator from pink to colorless, indicating the neutralization of hydroxide and carbonate ions. The end point of the titration with methyl orange is marked by the change of color of the indicator from yellow to orange, indicating the neutralization of bicarbonate ions.



# Water

Water is a substance composed of the chemical elements hydrogen and oxygen and existing in gaseous, liquid, and solid states. It is one of the most plentiful and essential of compounds. A tasteless and odourless liquid at room temperature, it has the important ability to dissolve many other substances.

Water is a tiny molecule. It consists of three atoms: two of hydrogen and one of oxygen. Water molecules cling to each other because of a force called hydrogen bonding. It's the reason why water can do amazing things.

Water is a shape-shifter. It exists in three states on Earth: liquid, gas, and solid. Liquid water is the most common form of water on Earth. It covers about 71% of the Earth's surface in oceans, lakes, rivers, and other bodies of water. Gas water is also known as water vapor. It is the invisible gas that forms when water evaporates or boils. Water vapor is present in the air and can form clouds, fog, rain, snow, and other types of precipitation. Solid water is also known as ice. It is the frozen form of water that occurs when water freezes below 0°C (32°F). Ice can form glaciers, icebergs, ice caps, snowflakes, and other shapes.

Water is the liquid that makes life on Earth possible. As water cycles from the air to the land to the sea and back again, water shapes our planet — and nearly every aspect of our lives. All living things, from tiny cyanobacteria to giant blue whales, need water to survive. Water is involved in many biological processes, such as photosynthesis, respiration, digestion, circulation, and excretion. Water also regulates the temperature of living organisms and the environment.

Water is a valuable resource for human activities. Water is used for drinking, cooking, washing, cleaning, irrigation, industry, energy, transportation, recreation, and more. Water is also a source of conflict and cooperation, as different groups of people compete for or share the limited freshwater resources on Earth. Water is a human right, as recognized by the United Nations, and a responsibility, as everyone has a role in protecting and conserving water for the present and future generations.

# Measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a solution is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H+].
- The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, where 0 is the most acidic, 7 is neutral, and 14 is the most basic. A solution with a pH of less than 7 is called acidic, and a solution with a pH of more than 7 is called basic or alkaline.
- The pH of a solution can be measured using indicators, such as litmus paper, phenolphthalein, or universal indicator, which change color depending on the acidity or basicity of the solution. Alternatively, the pH can be measured using a pH meter, which is a device that measures the electric potential difference between a reference electrode and a pH-sensitive electrode immersed in the solution.
- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is defined as the force per unit length that must be applied to the surface of a liquid to break or stretch it.
- Surface tension causes liquids to form spherical droplets, to minimize their surface area. It also causes liquids to rise or fall in narrow tubes, such as capillaries, depending on the attraction between the liquid and the tube wall. This phenomenon is called capillary action.
- Surface tension can be measured using various methods, such as the force balance method, the drop weight method, the pendant drop method, or the Wilhelmy plate method, which involve measuring the force or weight of a liquid drop or a solid plate in contact with the liquid surface.
- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a fluid is to flow. It is defined as the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate in a fluid: viscosity = shear stress / shear rate.
- The shear stress is the force per unit area that is applied parallel to the surface of a fluid, and the shear rate is the rate of change of the fluid velocity in the direction perpendicular to the surface.
- Viscosity depends on the temperature, pressure, and composition of the fluid, as well as the intermolecular forces and interactions between the fluid molecules. Generally, liquids have higher viscosity than gases, and viscosity decreases with increasing temperature and pressure.
- Viscosity can be measured using various methods, such as the falling ball method, the rotating cylinder method, the capillary tube method, or the cone and plate method, which involve measuring the force, torque, pressure, or time required to move a solid object or a fluid through a fluid.



# A liquid

- A liquid is one of the four states of matter, along with solid, gas, and plasma.
- A liquid is a fluid that has a definite volume but no fixed shape. It can flow and take the shape of its container.
- A liquid is composed of molecules that are loosely bound by intermolecular forces, such as hydrogen bonds or van der Waals forces.
- A liquid can undergo phase transitions to a solid (freezing) or a gas (evaporation or boiling) depending on the temperature and pressure.
- A liquid has some properties that are different from solids and gases, such as:
  - Surface tension: the tendency of a liquid to minimize its surface area and form droplets or bubbles.
  - Viscosity: the resistance of a liquid to flow or deform under stress.
  - Density: the mass per unit volume of a liquid, which usually decreases with increasing temperature.
  - Compressibility: the change in volume of a liquid due to applied pressure, which is usually much smaller than that of a gas.
  - Capillarity: the ability of a liquid to rise or fall in a narrow tube due to the balance of adhesive and cohesive forces.

# Preparation of different resins

Resins are organic compounds that are solid or semi-solid at room temperature and have a high molecular weight. Resins are usually derived from natural sources, such as plants or animals, or from synthetic sources, such as petroleum or coal. Resins have various applications, such as adhesives, coatings, paints, varnishes, plastics, rubber, and pharmaceuticals.

There are different types of resins, such as thermoplastic resins, thermosetting resins, and natural resins. The preparation of different resins depends on the source, the structure, and the properties of the resins.

## Thermoplastic resins

Thermoplastic resins are resins that can be melted and reshaped by heating and cooling. Thermoplastic resins are usually prepared by polymerization or polycondensation of monomers, such as ethylene, propylene, styrene, vinyl chloride, etc. Some examples of thermoplastic resins are polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, polyvinyl chloride, etc.

The general steps for preparing thermoplastic resins are:

- Monomer selection: The monomers are chosen based on the desired properties and characteristics of the resin, such as melting point, hardness, flexibility, transparency, etc.
- Polymerization or polycondensation: The monomers are reacted with each other or with other compounds, such as catalysts, initiators, or cross-linking agents, to form long chains of molecules called polymers. The reaction can be carried out in bulk, in solution, in suspension, or in emulsion, depending on the type of monomer and the process conditions.
- Purification: The polymer is separated from the unreacted monomers, solvents, catalysts, and other impurities by filtration, precipitation, extraction, distillation, or crystallization.
- Processing: The polymer is shaped into the desired form by extrusion, injection molding, blow molding, thermoforming, or other methods.

## Thermosetting resins

Thermosetting resins are resins that cannot be melted and reshaped by heating and cooling. Thermosetting resins are usually prepared by cross-linking of polymers, such as phenol-formaldehyde, urea-formaldehyde, melamine-formaldehyde, epoxy, etc. Some examples of thermosetting resins are bakelite, urea resin, melamine resin, epoxy resin, etc.

The general steps for preparing thermosetting resins are:

- Polymer selection: The polymers are chosen based on the desired properties and characteristics of the resin, such as curing temperature, hardness, durability, chemical resistance, etc.
- Cross-linking: The polymers are reacted with each other or with other compounds, such as hardeners, accelerators, or curing agents, to form three-dimensional networks of molecules called cross-links. The reaction can be carried out in one stage or in two stages, depending on the type of polymer and the process conditions.
- Curing: The cross-linked polymer is heated to a high temperature to complete the reaction and to solidify the resin. The curing can be done in a mold, in an oven, or in a press, depending on the type of resin and the application.
- Finishing: The cured resin is trimmed, polished, painted, or coated, depending on the desired appearance and performance of the resin.

## Natural resins

Natural resins are resins that are obtained from natural sources, such as plants or animals. Natural resins are usually extracted by tapping, distillation, solvent extraction, or mechanical separation. Some examples of natural resins are rosin, shellac, lacquer, amber, etc.

The general steps for preparing natural resins are:

- Source selection: The source of the resin is chosen based on the availability, quality, and quantity of the resin, such as pine trees, lac insects, lacquer trees, fossilized plants, etc.
- Extraction: The resin is collected from the source by cutting, tapping, scraping, boiling, or crushing the source. The resin may be in the form of a solid, a liquid, or a semi-solid, depending on the source and the extraction method.
- Purification: The resin is purified from the impurities, such as water, dirt, wax, or other substances, by filtration, evaporation, centrifugation, or crystallization.
- Processing: The resin is processed into the desired form by melting, molding, grinding, or dissolving the resin. The resin may be used as it is or mixed with other ingredients, such as pigments, fillers, or additives, to modify the properties and

```
# Synthesis of Adipic Acid

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid with the formula C6H10O4. It is mainly used for the production of nylon, polyurethanes, and other polymers.
- The conventional method for the synthesis of adipic acid is the oxidation of a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol (KA oil) with nitric acid .
- The oxidation of KA oil involves several steps, such as:
  - Dehydration of cyclohexanol to cyclohexanone, releasing nitrous acid.
  - Oxidation of cyclohexanone to 6-hydroxyimino-6-nitrohexane, releasing nitric oxide.
  - Hydrolysis of 6-hydroxyimino-6-nitrohexane to 6-hydroxyhexanoic acid and nitrous acid.
  - Oxidation of 6-hydroxyhexanoic acid to adipic acid, releasing water and nitric oxide.
- The oxidation of KA oil with nitric acid produces large amounts of nitrous oxide (N2O), a greenhouse gas and an ozone-depleting substance . Therefore, alternative methods for the synthesis of adipic acid from renewable and less harmful sources are desirable.
- Some of the alternative methods for the synthesis of adipic acid are:
  - Oxidation of cyclohexene or cyclohexane with hydrogen peroxide or oxygen, using various catalysts such as tungsten, molybdenum, or vanadium oxides.
  - Oxidation of glucose or its derivatives with nitric acid, hydrogen peroxide, or oxygen, using various catalysts such as gold, platinum, or palladium nanoparticles.
  - Biocatalytic conversion of glucose or its derivatives to adipic acid, using enzymes such as glucose dehydrogenase, gluconate dehydrogenase, or adipate dehydrogenase.
```

# Acid and Paracetamol by Conventional and Green Route

- Acid and paracetamol are two common drugs used for pain relief and fever reduction.
- Acid refers to acetylsalicylic acid, also known as aspirin, which is derived from salicylic acid, a natural compound found in plants such as willow bark.
- Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is derived from p-aminophenol, a synthetic compound obtained from nitration and reduction of phenol.

## Conventional Route

- The conventional route for the synthesis of acid involves the acetylation of salicylic acid with acetic anhydride in the presence of a catalyst, such as sulfuric acid or phosphoric acid, and heating the mixture to obtain the product and acetic acid as a by-product.
- The conventional route for the synthesis of paracetamol involves the acetylation of p-aminophenol with acetic anhydride in the presence of a catalyst, such as sodium acetate or pyridine, and heating the mixture to obtain the product and acetic acid as a by-product.

## Green Route

- The green route for the synthesis of acid and paracetamol aims to reduce the environmental impact of the conventional route by using principles of green chemistry, such as solvent-free synthesis, microwave-energy irradiation, and atom economy.
- The green route for the synthesis of acid involves the acetylation of salicylic acid with acetic anhydride in a microwave oven, without any catalyst or solvent, and obtaining the product and acetic acid as a by-product in a shorter time and with higher yield than the conventional route  .
- The green route for the synthesis of paracetamol involves the synthesis of p-aminophenol from phenol in three steps, using nitric acid, sodium borohydride, and acetic anhydride, and keeping the solvent from the second step to help maximize atom economy. The final step is the acetylation of p-aminophenol with acetic anhydride in a microwave oven, without any catalyst or solvent, and obtaining the product and acetic acid as a by-product in a shorter time and with higher yield than the conventional route  .



# LIST OF EXPERIMENTS

- An experiment is a scientific procedure that aims to test a hypothesis, answer a question, or demonstrate a known fact.
- Experiments usually involve manipulating one or more variables, measuring their effects, and comparing them with a control group.
- Experiments can be classified into different types based on their purpose, design, and execution.

## Types of experiments

- **Natural experiments**: These are experiments that exploit natural variations in the environment or historical events as sources of data. For example, studying the effects of a natural disaster, a policy change, or a genetic mutation on a population.
- **Quasi-experiments**: These are experiments that lack random assignment of subjects to treatment and control groups, but still attempt to control for confounding factors. For example, studying the effects of a school program, a drug, or a social intervention on a group of people who are not randomly selected.
- **Randomized controlled trials (RCTs)**: These are experiments that randomly assign subjects to treatment and control groups, and measure the outcomes of interest. RCTs are considered the gold standard for causal inference, as they can eliminate confounding factors and selection bias. For example, testing the efficacy and safety of a new vaccine, a medical device, or a behavioral intervention on a sample of volunteers.
- **Laboratory experiments**: These are experiments that are conducted in a controlled and artificial setting, where the experimenter can manipulate the variables of interest and isolate their effects. Laboratory experiments are often used to test theories, hypotheses, and mechanisms in various fields of science. For example, studying the behavior of atoms, molecules, cells, animals, or humans under different conditions.
- **Field experiments**: These are experiments that are conducted in a natural and realistic setting, where the experimenter can still manipulate the variables of interest, but has less control over the environment and other factors. Field experiments are often used to test the external validity and generalizability of theories, hypotheses, and interventions in various fields of science. For example, studying the impact of a marketing campaign, a political message, or a social norm on a population.



# Calibration of Analytical Equipment and Apparatus

- Calibration is the process of evaluating and adjusting the precision and accuracy of measurement equipment.
- Calibration is important for ensuring the quality and reliability of analytical results, as well as the safety of the laboratory and the environment.
- Calibration involves comparing the output of the instrument or equipment with a known reference standard, and correcting any deviation or error.
- Calibration should be performed according to standard operating procedures (SOPs) that specify the frequency, method, criteria, and documentation of the calibration process.
- The frequency of calibration depends on several factors, such as the type, usage, stability, and criticality of the instrument or equipment.
- Some examples of analytical instruments and equipment that require calibration are balances, pipettes, thermometers, pH meters, spectrophotometers, chromatographs, and titrators .
- Calibration can be done in-house by trained personnel, or by external service providers that are accredited or certified.
- Calibration records should include the identification of the instrument or equipment, the reference standard, the date and time of calibration, the calibration results, the corrective actions, and the signature of the person who performed the calibration.



# 2. Determination of Hardness of water sample by EDTA method.

- Hardness of water is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water.
- Hard water causes problems such as scaling of pipes, boilers, and appliances, soap scum formation, and reduced effectiveness of detergents and cleaners.
- EDTA (ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) is a chelating agent that can form stable complexes with metal ions such as calcium and magnesium.
- EDTA method is a titrimetric method that involves titrating a water sample with a standard solution of EDTA in the presence of a suitable indicator that changes color at the end point of the titration.
- The indicator used for EDTA method is Eriochrome Black T (EBT), which is a metallochromic indicator that forms a wine-red complex with free calcium and magnesium ions in water, and turns blue when all the metal ions are complexed with EDTA.
- The procedure of EDTA method is as follows:

  - Take a known volume of water sample in a conical flask and add a few drops of buffer solution (pH 10) to maintain a constant pH during the titration.
  - Add 2-3 drops of EBT indicator and swirl the flask. The solution should turn wine-red due to the presence of free metal ions.
  - Titrate the solution with a standard EDTA solution from a burette until the color changes from wine-red to blue, indicating the end point of the titration.
  - Record the volume of EDTA solution used and repeat the titration for accuracy.
  - Calculate the hardness of water sample using the formula:

    - Hardness (mg/L) = (Volume of EDTA solution used x Normality of EDTA solution x 1000) / Volume of water sample



# Determination of Alkalinity of Water Sample

- Alkalinity is the measure of the ability of water to neutralize acids.
- Alkalinity is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides in water.
- Alkalinity is important for water quality, aquatic life, and corrosion control.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid, using a suitable indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange.
- The endpoint of the titration is marked by a color change of the indicator, which depends on the pH of the solution.
- The amount of acid used to reach the endpoint is proportional to the alkalinity of the water sample.
- The alkalinity can be expressed in different units, such as milligrams per liter (mg/L) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3), milliequivalents per liter (meq/L), or degrees of hardness (dH).
- The alkalinity can be classified into different types, such as total alkalinity, phenolphthalein alkalinity, methyl orange alkalinity, carbonate alkalinity, bicarbonate alkalinity, and hydroxide alkalinity, depending on the pH range and the predominant species in the water sample.
- The alkalinity can be calculated using different formulas, depending on the type of alkalinity and the acid used for titration.
- The alkalinity can be affected by various factors, such as temperature, dissolved gases, salinity, and biological activity.



# Determination of pH by titrimetric method

Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a solution of unknown concentration (analyte) . The point at which the reaction is complete is called the endpoint or equivalence point. The volume of titrant added at the endpoint is called the equivalence volume.

pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H3O+] . pH can be determined by various methods, such as using indicators, pH meters, or potentiometers. Potentiometry is a method of measuring the potential difference between two electrodes in an electrochemical cell . One of the electrodes is a reference electrode, which has a constant and known potential, and the other is an indicator electrode, which responds to the analyte. The potential difference between the electrodes depends on the concentration of the analyte, and can be related to the pH by the Nernst equation.

A potentiometric titration is a type of titration in which the pH of the solution is measured by a potentiometer throughout the titration . A plot of the pH of the solution versus the volume of titrant added is called a pH curve or a titration curve. The shape of the curve depends on the nature and strength of the acid and base involved in the titration. The endpoint of the titration can be determined by finding the point of inflection of the curve, where the slope changes abruptly. The equivalence point of the titration can be calculated by using the stoichiometry of the reaction and the equivalence volume.

The following are the steps for determining the pH by titrimetric method :

- Prepare a standard solution of the titrant and a sample solution of the analyte.
- Calibrate the pH meter or the potentiometer using buffer solutions of known pH.
- Rinse the electrodes with distilled water and dry them gently with a tissue paper.
- Fill a burette with the titrant and record the initial volume.
- Transfer a measured volume of the analyte to a beaker or a flask and add a few drops of an indicator (optional).
- Immerse the electrodes in the analyte solution and record the initial pH.
- Add the titrant gradually from the burette to the analyte solution, stirring constantly, and record the pH and the volume of titrant after each addition.
- Continue the titration until the endpoint is reached, indicated by a color change of the indicator (if used) or a sharp change in the pH.
- Record the final volume of the titrant and the final pH of the solution.
- Plot the pH curve by using the pH and the volume of titrant data.
- Find the point of inflection of the curve and mark the equivalence point and the equivalence volume.
- Calculate the concentration of the analyte by using the stoichiometry of the reaction and the equivalence volume.
- Calculate the pH of the solution at the equivalence point by using the Nernst equation or an appropriate formula.



# 5. Determination of surface tension of given liquid

- Surface tension is a property of a liquid surface that makes it act as if it were a stretched elastic membrane    .
- Surface tension is caused by the attraction of the molecules in the surface layer by the bulk of the liquid, which tends to minimize the surface area   .
- Surface tension is measured in units of force per unit length, such as newtons per meter (N/m) or dynes per centimeter (dyn/cm) .
- Surface tension is responsible for many phenomena, such as the shape of liquid drops, the ability of some insects to walk on water, and the formation of soap bubbles  .
- Surface tension can be determined by various methods, such as the capillary rise method, the drop weight method, the drop volume method, the ring method, and the bubble pressure method.
- In this experiment, we will use the capillary rise method to determine the surface tension of a given liquid.
- The capillary rise method is based on the principle that when a capillary tube of small diameter is dipped into a liquid, the liquid rises or falls inside the tube depending on the relative magnitude of the adhesive and cohesive forces.
- The adhesive force is the attraction between the liquid molecules and the tube wall, while the cohesive force is the attraction between the liquid molecules themselves.
- If the adhesive force is greater than the cohesive force, the liquid will rise inside the tube and form a concave meniscus. This is the case for water and glass.
- If the cohesive force is greater than the adhesive force, the liquid will fall inside the tube and form a convex meniscus. This is the case for mercury and glass.
- The height of the liquid column inside the tube depends on the surface tension of the liquid, the radius of the tube, the density of the liquid, and the acceleration due to gravity.
- The relationship between these quantities is given by the following formula:

$$h = \frac{2\gamma \cos \theta}{\rho gr}$$

where $h$ is the height of the liquid column, $\gamma$ is the surface tension of the liquid, $\theta$ is the contact angle between the liquid and the tube wall, $\rho$ is the density of the liquid, $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity, and $r$ is the radius of the tube.

- To determine the surface tension of a given liquid, we need to measure the height of the liquid column, the radius of the tube, the density of the liquid, and the contact angle. The acceleration due to gravity is assumed to be constant and known.
- The procedure of the experiment is as follows:

  - Clean and dry a capillary tube of known radius and a measuring scale.
  - Dip the tube into the liquid and observe the rise or fall of the liquid inside the tube.
  - Measure the height of the liquid column from the surface of the liquid to the top of the meniscus using the scale.
  - Repeat the steps 2 and 3 for at least three different tubes of different radii.
  - Calculate the average height of the liquid column for each tube.
  - Measure the density of the liquid using a hydrometer or a balance and a graduated cylinder.
  - Estimate the contact angle between the liquid and the tube wall using a protractor or a reference table.
  - Substitute the values of the height, radius, density, and contact angle into the formula and solve for the surface tension of the liquid.
  - Report the result with the appropriate units and significant figures.



# 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied force.
- Viscosity depends on the intermolecular forces, temperature and pressure of the fluid.
- A viscometer is a device that measures the viscosity of a fluid by observing its flow through a narrow tube or a capillary.
- The most common type of viscometer is the Ostwald viscometer, which consists of a U-shaped glass tube with two bulbs and a constant diameter capillary connecting them.
- The Ostwald viscometer works on the principle that the time taken for a fixed volume of fluid to flow through the capillary is proportional to its viscosity.
- The viscosity of a fluid can be calculated using the following formula:

    `η = kρt`

    where η is the viscosity, k is the calibration constant of the viscometer, ρ is the density of the fluid and t is the time of flow.

- The calibration constant k can be determined by using a fluid of known viscosity, such as water, and measuring its time of flow.
- The procedure for determining the viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer is as follows:

    - Clean the viscometer with distilled water and dry it with air.
    - Fill the viscometer with the liquid to be tested and immerse it in a water bath at a constant temperature.
    - Adjust the liquid level in the upper bulb to the upper mark by sucking or blowing through the side tube.
    - Open the stopcock and start the stopwatch when the liquid level reaches the lower mark in the upper bulb.
    - Stop the stopwatch when the liquid level reaches the upper mark in the lower bulb and note the time of flow.
    - Repeat the experiment at least three times and take the average time of flow.
    - Measure the density of the liquid using a hydrometer or a pycnometer.
    - Calculate the viscosity of the liquid using the formula and the calibration constant of the viscometer.
    - Record the observations and results in a tabular form.



# Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator

- Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a double salt of iron(II) sulfate and ammonium sulfate, with the formula (NH4)2Fe(SO4)2·6H2O.
- It is often used as a standard solution for titrations of reducing agents, such as oxalic acid, potassium permanganate, or potassium dichromate.
- The strength of a ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be determined by titrating it with a standard solution of potassium permanganate, using an external indicator such as starch or potassium ferricyanide.
- The reaction between ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium permanganate is:

(NH4)2Fe(SO4)2 + KMnO4 + H2SO4 -> K2SO4 + (NH4)2SO4 + Fe2(SO4)3 + MnSO4 + H2O

- The reaction is a redox reaction, in which ferrous ions are oxidized to ferric ions, and permanganate ions are reduced to manganese(II) ions.
- The end point of the titration is marked by the disappearance of the pink color of permanganate, or by the appearance of a blue color of starch-iodine complex or a brown color of ferric ferrocyanide complex, depending on the external indicator used.
- The strength of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be calculated by using the following formula:

Strength (g/L) = (V1 x N1 x M x 1000) / V2

where V1 is the volume of potassium permanganate solution used (mL), N1 is the normality of potassium permanganate solution, M is the molar mass of ferrous ammonium sulfate (392 g/mol), and V2 is the volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution taken (mL).



# Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a reducing agent such as ferrous ammonium sulphate.
- The reaction between potassium dichromate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is as follows:

  $$K_2Cr_2O_7 + 6FeSO_4 + 7H_2SO_4 \rightarrow Cr_2(SO_4)_3 + 3K_2SO_4 + 6Fe_2(SO_4)_3 + 7H_2O$$

- The end point of the titration can be detected by the change in colour of the solution from green to reddish-brown, due to the formation of ferric ions.
- This colour change is the internal indicator of the titration, and no external indicator is required.
- The strength of potassium dichromate can be calculated by using the following formula:

  $$\text{Strength of K}_2\text{Cr}_2\text{O}_7 = \frac{6 \times \text{Normality of FeSO}_4 \times \text{Volume of FeSO}_4}{\text{Volume of K}_2\text{Cr}_2\text{O}_7}$$

- The normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate can be determined by titrating it against a standard solution of potassium permanganate, using phenolphthalein as an external indicator.
- The reaction between potassium permanganate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is as follows:

  $$KMnO_4 + 5FeSO_4 + 8H_2SO_4 \rightarrow K_2SO_4 + 2MnSO_4 + 5Fe_2(SO_4)_3 + 8H_2O$$

- The end point of the titration is marked by the appearance of a faint pink colour in the solution, due to the excess of potassium permanganate.
- The normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate can be calculated by using the following formula:

  $$\text{Normality of FeSO}_4 = \frac{\text{Normality of KMnO}_4 \times \text{Volume of KMnO}_4}{5 \times \text{Volume of FeSO}_4}$$



# Determination of Available Chlorine in Bleaching Powder

- Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium hypochlorite (Ca(OCl)2), calcium chloride (CaCl2), and calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH)2).
- It is commonly used as a disinfectant and a bleaching agent.
- The available chlorine in bleaching powder is the amount of chlorine that can be liberated when the powder is treated with an acid or water.
- The available chlorine is expressed as a percentage of the mass of the bleaching powder.
- The available chlorine can be determined by an iodometric method, which involves the following steps:

  - A known mass of bleaching powder is dissolved in water and filtered to obtain a clear solution.
  - A measured volume of the solution is titrated with a standard solution of potassium iodide (KI) in the presence of an acid (usually hydrochloric acid, HCl).
  - The reaction between bleaching powder and potassium iodide is as follows:

    Ca(OCl)2 + 2KI + 2HCl -> CaCl2 + 2KCl + I2 + H2O

  - The iodine (I2) liberated in the reaction is then titrated with a standard solution of sodium thiosulfate (Na2S2O3) using starch as an indicator.
  - The reaction between iodine and sodium thiosulfate is as follows:

    I2 + 2Na2S2O3 -> 2NaI + Na2S4O6

  - The end point of the titration is marked by the disappearance of the blue color of the starch-iodine complex.
  - The volume of sodium thiosulfate used is noted and used to calculate the amount of iodine and hence the available chlorine in the bleaching powder sample.



# Determination of chloride content in water sample

- Chloride ions are one of the major inorganic anions in water and wastewater.
- Chloride ions can be determined by various methods, such as argentometric, potentiometric, colorimetric, and gravimetric methods.
- Argentometric method is based on the precipitation of chloride ions with silver nitrate solution in the presence of an indicator.
- Potentiometric method is based on the measurement of the potential difference between a chloride-specific electrode and a reference electrode in a solution containing chloride ions.
- Colorimetric method is based on the formation of a colored complex between chloride ions and a reagent, such as mercuric thiocyanate or ferric thiocyanate, and measuring the absorbance of the solution at a specific wavelength.
- Gravimetric method is based on the precipitation of chloride ions as silver chloride and weighing the dried precipitate.

## Argentometric method (Mohr's method)

- This method is suitable for water samples with pH between 6.5 and 10 and chloride concentration between 10 and 1000 mg/L.
- The principle of this method is that water containing chloride ions is titrated with a standard silver nitrate solution until a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate is formed at the end point.
- The indicator used is potassium chromate, which supplies chromate ions that react with excess silver ions to form the precipitate.
- The reaction is quantitative and follows a 1:1 stoichiometry between silver nitrate and chloride ions.
- The procedure for this method is as follows:

  - Take 50 mL of sample (V mL) and dilute to 100 mL with distilled water in a conical flask.
  - If the sample is highly colored, add 3 mL of aluminum hydroxide and shake well, allow to settle, filter, wash and collect the filtrate.
  - Bring the sample pH to 7-8 by adding acid or alkali if necessary.
  - Add 1 mL of indicator (potassium chromate) and mix well.
  - Titrate the solution against standard silver nitrate solution (N/50 or 0.02 M) from a burette until a reddish-brown precipitate is obtained.
  - Note the volume of silver nitrate solution used (V1 mL).
  - Repeat the titration with a blank (distilled water) and note the volume of silver nitrate solution used (V2 mL).
  - Calculate the chloride concentration in the sample as follows:

    - Chloride concentration (mg/L) = (V1 - V2) x N x 35.45 x 1000 / V
    - Where N is the normality of silver nitrate solution and 35.45 is the equivalent weight of chloride ion.

## Potentiometric method

- This method is suitable for water samples with chloride concentration between 1 and 1000 mg/L.
- The principle of this method is that the potential difference between a chloride-specific electrode and a reference electrode is proportional to the logarithm of the chloride ion activity in the solution.
- The chloride-specific electrode is a solid-state electrode that consists of a silver wire coated with silver chloride and a membrane of silver sulfide or silver bromide.
- The reference electrode is usually a saturated calomel electrode (SCE) or a silver-silver chloride electrode.
- The procedure for this method is as follows:

  - Prepare a series of standard solutions of known chloride concentrations by diluting a stock solution of sodium chloride (1000 mg/L) with distilled water.
  - Immerse the chloride-specific electrode and the reference electrode in each standard solution and measure the potential difference with a potentiometer or a pH meter.
  - Plot the potential difference versus the logarithm of the chloride concentration and obtain a calibration curve.
  - Immerse the electrodes in the sample solution and measure the potential difference.
  - Read the chloride concentration from the calibration curve.

## Colorimetric method (Mercuric thiocyanate method)

- This method is suitable for water samples with chloride concentration between 0.1 and 10 mg/L.
- The principle of this method is that chloride ions react with mercuric thiocyanate in acidic medium to form a soluble complex of mercuric chloride and thiocyanate ions.
- The excess mercuric thiocyanate reacts with ferric ions to form a red-colored complex of ferric thiocyanate, which is proportional to the chloride concentration in the sample.
- The indicator used is ferric ammon



# Preparation of Phenol Formaldehyde (PF) Resin

- Phenol formaldehyde resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the reaction of phenol and formaldehyde.
- Phenol formaldehyde resin can be prepared by a step-growth polymerization reaction that can be either acid- or base-catalyzed .
- The ratio of phenol to formaldehyde affects the structure and properties of the resin. A 1:1 ratio produces linear chains, while a higher ratio of formaldehyde produces cross-linked networks .
- The steps involved in the preparation of phenol formaldehyde resin are :

  - Weighing the appropriate quantity of phenol and formaldehyde and mixing them in a reaction vessel.
  - Adding a catalyst, such as hydrochloric acid, sulfuric acid, or ammonia, to initiate the polymerization reaction.
  - Heating the mixture to a suitable temperature and controlling the reaction time to achieve the desired degree of polymerization and viscosity.
  - Cooling and neutralizing the resin and removing any excess water or formaldehyde by distillation or filtration.
  - Drying and curing the resin to form a hard and durable material.

- Phenol formaldehyde resin has various applications, such as in adhesives, coatings, laminates, molding compounds, and wood products.



# Preparation of Urea Formaldehyde (UF) Resin

- Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF resin is synthesized by the condensation reaction of urea and formaldehyde in the presence of a base or an acid catalyst .
- The reaction can be carried out in one or more steps, depending on the desired properties and applications of the resin  .
- The general steps for the preparation of UF resin are as follows:

  - Step 1: Preparation of methylolureas
    - Urea and formaldehyde are mixed in an aqueous solution in a molar ratio of 2-3:1 at pH 6-11.
    - The mixture is heated to at least 80°C to form methylolureas, which are intermediates that have one or more hydroxymethyl groups attached to the nitrogen atoms of urea .
    - The degree of methylation and the distribution of methylol groups depend on the reaction conditions, such as temperature, pH, time, and catalyst.
    - The methylolureas can be isolated by cooling, filtering, washing, and drying, or they can be used directly for the next step.
  - Step 2: Condensation of methylolureas
    - The methylolureas are further reacted with formaldehyde and/or urea under acidic or alkaline conditions to form UF resin  .
    - The condensation reaction involves the elimination of water molecules and the formation of methylene and/or methylene ether bridges between the methylol groups of different urea molecules .
    - The degree of condensation and the molecular weight of the resin depend on the reaction conditions, such as temperature, pH, time, catalyst, and formaldehyde-to-urea (F/U) ratio.
    - The F/U ratio determines the amount of free formaldehyde and the crosslinking density of the resin. A higher F/U ratio results in a higher formaldehyde emission and a faster curing rate, while a lower F/U ratio results in a lower formaldehyde emission and a slower curing rate.
    - The condensation reaction can be stopped by adding a neutralizing agent, such as ammonia, to adjust the pH of the resin .
    - The UF resin can be isolated by cooling, filtering, washing, and drying, or it can be used directly as an adhesive or a molding material.
  - Step 3: Curing of UF resin
    - The UF resin can be cured by heating, pressure, or catalysts to form a crosslinked network that is insoluble and infusible .
    - The curing conditions depend on the type and application of the resin, such as the temperature, time, pressure, catalyst, and filler.
    - The curing process involves the further condensation of the methylol groups and the formation of more methylene and/or methylene ether bridges between the urea molecules .
    - The curing process also involves the release of formaldehyde and water as byproducts, which can cause environmental and health concerns .
    - The cured UF resin has high strength, hardness, rigidity, and resistance to abrasion, heat, and chemicals, but it has low resistance to moisture, sunlight, and acids .



# Preparation of Adipic acid / Paracetamol

## Adipic acid

- Adipic acid is the organic compound with the formula (CH2)4(COOH)2. It is the most important dicarboxylic acid, mainly used as a precursor for the production of nylon.
- Adipic acid is produced from a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol called KA oil, the abbreviation of ketone-alcohol oil. The KA oil is oxidized with nitric acid to give adipic acid, via a multistep pathway .
- The oxidation of KA oil involves the following steps :
  - Formation of nitrosonium ion (NO+) by the reaction of nitric acid and air.
  - Nitrosation of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol to form nitrosocyclohexanone and nitrosocyclohexanol, respectively.
  - Rearrangement of nitrosocyclohexanone and nitrosocyclohexanol to form 6-nitrosohexanoic acid and 6-nitrosohexanol, respectively.
  - Oxidation of 6-nitrosohexanoic acid and 6-nitrosohexanol to form 6-nitrohexanoic acid and 6-nitrohexanol, respectively.
  - Hydrolysis of 6-nitrohexanoic acid and 6-nitrohexanol to form adipic acid and nitrous acid, respectively.
  - Recycle of nitrous acid to nitric acid by the reaction with air.

## Paracetamol

- Paracetamol is the organic compound with the formula C8H9NO2. It is an effective antipyretic and analgesic, widely used for the relief of fever, headaches, and other minor aches and pains.
- Paracetamol is prepared from p-aminophenol by acetylating it with acetic anhydride in the presence of 3-4 drops of concentrated sulphuric acid as catalyst .
- The acetylation of p-aminophenol involves the following steps :
  - Formation of acylium ion (CH3CO+) by the reaction of acetic anhydride and sulphuric acid.
  - Nucleophilic attack of p-aminophenol on acylium ion to form N-acetyl-p-aminophenol (paracetamol) and acetic acid.
  - Purification of paracetamol by recrystallization from water.



# Determination of Cell Conductance of a Solution

- Cell conductance of a solution is the reciprocal of its resistance, i.e. G = 1/R, where G is the conductance and R is the resistance.
- Cell conductance is measured in siemens (S) or ohms^-1^.
- Cell conductance of a solution depends on the concentration, charge, and mobility of the ions present in the solution.
- Cell conductance of a solution can be measured by using a special type of cell known as a conductivity cell and a Wheatstone bridge or a conductivity meter.
- A conductivity cell consists of two electrodes of known area and distance, immersed in the solution whose conductance is to be measured.
- The cell constant, C, is defined as the ratio of the distance between the electrodes, l, to the area of cross-section of the electrodes, a, i.e. C = l/a.
- The cell constant has units of m^-1^ and is a characteristic of the cell geometry and size.
- The cell constant can be determined by measuring the resistance of a cell filled with a solution of known conductivity, such as KCl.
- The conductivity, κ, of a solution is defined as the conductance of a unit volume of the solution, i.e. κ = G x V, where V is the volume of the solution.
- The conductivity has units of S m^-1^ and is a property of the solution.
- The conductivity of a solution can be calculated from the experimental resistance and the cell constant by using the formula: κ = C/R.

# 15. Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

## Introduction

- Esters are organic compounds that have the general formula RCOOR', where R and R' are alkyl or aryl groups.
- Esters can be hydrolyzed by water in the presence of an acid or a base catalyst to form carboxylic acids and alcohols.
- The hydrolysis of esters is a reversible reaction and follows the law of mass action.
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters depends on the concentration of the ester, the water, the acid or base catalyst, and the temperature.
- The rate constant of hydrolysis of esters can be determined experimentally by measuring the change in concentration of the ester or the products over time.

## Theory

- The general equation for the hydrolysis of esters in the presence of an acid catalyst is:

  RCOOR' + H2O ⇌ RCOOH + R'OH

- The rate of the forward reaction is given by:

  r_f = k_f[RCOOR'][H2O]

- The rate of the backward reaction is given by:

  r_b = k_b[RCOOH][R'OH]

- At equilibrium, the rates of the forward and backward reactions are equal, so:

  k_f[RCOOR'][H2O] = k_b[RCOOH][R'OH]

- Dividing both sides by [H2O] and rearranging, we get:

  K_c = k_f/k_b = [RCOOH][R'OH]/[RCOOR']

- Where K_c is the equilibrium constant for the reaction.

- If we assume that the concentration of water is constant and much larger than the concentration of the ester, we can simplify the expression for the rate of the forward reaction as:

  r_f = k[RCOOR']

- Where k is the pseudo-first-order rate constant, given by:

  k = k_f[H2O]

- The rate law for the hydrolysis of esters can then be written as:

  -d[RCOOR']/dt = k[RCOOR']

- Integrating this equation and applying the boundary conditions, we get:

  ln([RCOOR']_t/[RCOOR']_0) = -kt

- Where [RCOOR']_t is the concentration of the ester at time t, and [RCOOR']_0 is the initial concentration of the ester.

- The pseudo-first-order rate constant k can be determined by plotting ln([RCOOR']_t/[RCOOR']_0) versus t and finding the slope of the line.

## Procedure

- Prepare a solution of an ester (such as ethyl acetate) and an acid catalyst (such as hydrochloric acid) in a conical flask.
- Measure the initial pH of the solution using a pH meter and record it.
- Place the flask in a water bath maintained at a constant temperature (such as 25°C).
- Start a stopwatch and take a small aliquot of the solution at regular intervals (such as every 5 minutes) using a pipette.
- Transfer the aliquot to a beaker and titrate it with a standard solution of a base (such as sodium hydroxide) using a burette and an indicator (such as phenolphthalein).
- Note the volume of the base required to reach the end point and record it.
- Repeat the steps 4 to 6 until the pH of the solution in the flask does not change significantly.
- Calculate the concentration of the ester at each time interval using the following formula:

  [RCOOR']_t = [RCOOR']_0 - V_tM_b/M_e

- Where V_t is the volume of the base used at time t, M_b is the molarity of the base, and M_e is the molar mass of the ester.

- Plot ln([RCOOR']_t/[RCOOR']_0) versus t and find the slope of the line.
- The slope of the line is equal to -k, the pseudo-first-order rate constant of the hydrolysis of the ester.

## Precautions

- Wear gloves, goggles, and a lab coat while handling the chemicals and performing the experiment.

# Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

- Elements are the basic building blocks of matter. They are composed of atoms that have the same number of protons in their nuclei.
- Organic compounds are molecules that contain carbon and hydrogen, and often other elements such as oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur, phosphorus, etc.
- Functional groups are specific groups of atoms or bonds within organic molecules that are responsible for their chemical properties and reactions.
- Detection of elements and functional groups in organic compounds is important for determining their structure, classification, and behavior.
- Some common methods for element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds are:

  - **Combustion analysis**: This method involves burning a known mass of the organic compound in excess oxygen and measuring the masses of carbon dioxide and water produced. The percentage of carbon and hydrogen in the compound can be calculated from the mass ratios of these products. The percentage of oxygen can be found by subtracting the percentage of carbon and hydrogen from 100%. Other elements such as nitrogen and sulfur can also be detected by this method using appropriate reagents and apparatus.
  - **Lassaigne's test**: This test is used to detect the presence of nitrogen, sulfur, halogens, and phosphorus in an organic compound. It involves fusing a small amount of the organic compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube. The sodium reacts with these elements to form soluble sodium salts, which are extracted with water. The aqueous extract is then tested for the presence of these elements using various chemical reactions.
  - **Solubility test**: This test is used to determine the polarity and intermolecular forces of an organic compound. It involves dissolving a small amount of the organic compound in different solvents such as water, dilute acid, or dilute base. The solubility of the compound indicates its degree of polarity and the type of intermolecular forces it exhibits. For example, polar compounds are more soluble in polar solvents than in nonpolar solvents, and compounds with hydrogen bonding are more soluble in water than in other solvents.
  - **Functional group tests**: These tests are used to identify the specific functional groups present in an organic compound. They involve adding specific reagents to the organic compound and observing the changes in color, odor, precipitate formation, gas evolution, etc. Some common functional group tests are:

    - **Alcoholic –OH group**: This group can be detected by the following tests:

      - **Lucas test**: A few drops of the organic compound are added to Lucas reagent (a mixture of zinc chloride and concentrated hydrochloric acid) in a test tube. The formation of a turbid solution or a separate layer indicates the presence of an alcoholic –OH group. The rate of turbidity formation depends on the type of alcohol: tertiary alcohols react fastest, followed by secondary alcohols, and primary alcohols react very slowly or not at all.
      - **Esterification test**: A few drops of the organic compound are added to acetic anhydride and a few drops of concentrated sulfuric acid in a test tube. The mixture is heated gently and then cooled. The formation of a fruity odor indicates the presence of an alcoholic –OH group. The odor is due to the formation of an ester by the reaction of the alcohol with acetic anhydride.

    - **Carbonyl group (Aldehydes and Ketones)**: This group can be detected by the following tests:

      - **2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine test**: A few drops of the organic compound are added to 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine reagent (a solution of 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine in methanol and sulfuric acid) in a test tube. The formation of a yellow, orange, or red precipitate indicates the presence of a carbonyl group. The precipitate is a 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazone derivative of the aldehyde or ketone.
      - **Tollen's test**: A few drops of the organic compound are added to Tollen's reagent (a solution of silver nitrate and ammonia) in a test tube. The test tube is placed in a hot water bath for a few minutes. The formation of a silver mirror on the inner wall of the test tube indicates the presence of an aldehyde group. The aldehyde reduces the silver ions to metallic silver. Ketones do not react with Tollen's reagent.

# Course Outcomes and Bloom's Taxonomy

- Course outcomes are statements that describe what students are expected to know and be able to do by the end of a course.
- Bloom's taxonomy is a framework for classifying educational objectives into six levels of cognitive complexity: remember, understand, apply, analyze, evaluate, and create.
- Course outcomes should be aligned with Bloom's taxonomy to ensure that students achieve different levels of learning and demonstrate higher-order thinking skills.
- Course outcomes should be specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART) to provide clear and realistic expectations for students and instructors.
- Course outcomes should be written using action verbs that indicate the level of cognitive skill required by the students, such as define, explain, compare, contrast, solve, design, etc.
- Course outcomes should be assessed using appropriate methods and tools that match the level of cognitive skill and the type of learning outcome, such as quizzes, exams, assignments, projects, portfolios, etc.
- Course outcomes should be communicated to students at the beginning of the course and throughout the course to help them monitor their progress and self-regulate their learning.
- Course outcomes should be reviewed and revised periodically to ensure that they reflect the current needs and expectations of the students, the instructors, the discipline, and the society.



# Level

- A level is a tool or device that is used to measure or indicate whether a surface is horizontal (level) or vertical (plumb).
- A level consists of a horizontal tube or vial that contains a liquid (usually alcohol or water) and an air bubble. The position of the bubble indicates the alignment of the surface relative to the earth's gravity.
- A level can be used for various purposes, such as construction, carpentry, engineering, surveying, landscaping, photography, etc.
- There are different types of levels, such as spirit levels, laser levels, digital levels, water levels, etc. Each type has its own advantages and disadvantages depending on the application and accuracy required.
- Some common features of levels are:

  - Length: The length of the level determines the size of the surface that can be measured or aligned. Longer levels are more accurate but less portable and convenient.
  - Vials: The number and orientation of the vials on the level affect the versatility and functionality of the tool. Some levels have only one horizontal vial, while others have multiple vials for different angles and directions.
  - Sensitivity: The sensitivity of the level refers to how much the bubble moves when the surface is tilted. Higher sensitivity means more precision but also more difficulty in reading the bubble.
  - Material: The material of the level affects the durability, weight, and appearance of the tool. Common materials are wood, metal, plastic, or composite.
  - Calibration: The calibration of the level ensures that the tool is accurate and reliable. Calibration can be done by the manufacturer, by the user, or by a professional service. Calibration can be affected by factors such as temperature, humidity, altitude, etc.

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or identify the chemical, physical, or biological properties of a sample or a substance .
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on the principle or technique of analysis, such as spectroscopy, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, thermal analysis, separation analysis, microscopy, and hybrid techniques .

# CO2 Molecular Properties and Surface Tension

- Carbon dioxide (CO2) is a linear molecule with a bond length of 116.3 pm and a bond angle of 180 degrees.
- CO2 is a non-polar molecule with a dipole moment of zero and a symmetrical distribution of electrons.
- CO2 has a molecular weight of 44.0095 g/mol and a molar volume of 22.26 L/mol at standard temperature and pressure.
- CO2 has a critical temperature of 304.13 K and a critical pressure of 7.38 MPa.
- CO2 has a density of 1.977 kg/m3 at 0 °C and 1 atm, and a specific weight of 19.41 N/m3 at the same conditions.
- CO2 has a surface tension of 0.0148 N/m at 0 °C and 1 atm, and a surface tension of 0.0012 N/m at the critical point.
- Surface tension is the energy or work required to increase the surface area of a liquid due to intermolecular forces.
- Surface tension depends on the nature of the liquid and the solutes in the liquid, as well as the temperature and pressure.
- CO2 has weak intermolecular forces (London dispersion forces) compared to water, which has strong hydrogen bonds.
- CO2 has a lower surface tension than water, which means it is easier to spread or break the surface of CO2 than water.
- CO2 can dissolve in water and form carbonic acid, which lowers the pH and the surface tension of water.
- CO2 can also react with some solutes in water, such as monoethanolamine (MEA), and form partially carbonated solutions .
- The density and surface tension of CO2 loaded aqueous MEA solutions depend on the amine mass ratio, the CO2 loading, the temperature and the pressure .
- The density and surface tension of CO2 loaded aqueous MEA solutions generally decrease with increasing temperature and CO2 loading, and increase with increasing amine mass ratio and pressure .



# Viscosity

- Viscosity is the property of a fluid that measures its resistance to flow.
- Viscosity depends on the internal friction between the molecules of the fluid, which is affected by factors such as temperature, pressure, and composition.
- Viscosity is expressed by the formula:

    `η = F / (A * dv/dy)`

    where η is the viscosity, F is the force applied, A is the area of contact, and dv/dy is the velocity gradient of the fluid.
- The SI unit of viscosity is the pascal-second (Pa·s), which is equivalent to the newton-second per square meter (N·s/m^2^).
- Viscosity can also be expressed in terms of kinematic viscosity, which is the ratio of dynamic viscosity to density:

    `ν = η / ρ`

    where ν is the kinematic viscosity, η is the dynamic viscosity, and ρ is the density.
- The SI unit of kinematic viscosity is the square meter per second (m^2^/s).
- Some examples of fluids with different viscosities are:

    - Water: 0.001 Pa·s at 20°C
    - Honey: 10 Pa·s at 20°C
    - Molasses: 100 Pa·s at 20°C
    - Tar: 1000 Pa·s at 20°C

- Viscosity affects the flow rate, pressure drop, and energy consumption of fluids in pipes, pumps, turbines, and other devices.
- Viscosity also influences the formation and stability of emulsions, suspensions, and foams.



# K3 surface

A K3 surface is a type of algebraic surface that has some special properties. Here are some points to know about K3 surfaces:

- A K3 surface is a smooth, proper, geometrically connected algebraic surface over any field that has trivial canonical bundle and no non-zero global holomorphic 1-forms .
- A K3 surface is a minimal surface of Kodaira dimension zero, meaning that it has no curves of negative self-intersection and no non-trivial holomorphic vector fields .
- A K3 surface is simply connected, meaning that it has no non-trivial loops that can be continuously shrunk to a point .
- A K3 surface has 24 singular points in its Picard group, which is the group of divisors modulo linear equivalence .
- A K3 surface has a Hodge diamond of the form

```
1
0 20 0
1 0 1
0 20 0
1
```

which means that its cohomology groups have certain dimensions and types .
- A K3 surface has Euler characteristic 24, meaning that the alternating sum of its Betti numbers is 24 .
- A K3 surface is Calabi-Yau, meaning that it admits a Ricci-flat Kähler metric .
- A K3 surface is hyperkähler, meaning that it admits three complex structures that are compatible with the same Kähler form .
- A K3 surface is a complex manifold of dimension 2, meaning that it can be locally described by two complex coordinates .
- A K3 surface is a smooth 4-manifold, meaning that it can be locally described by four real coordinates .
- A K3 surface is a symplectic manifold, meaning that it has a closed non-degenerate 2-form that defines a Poisson bracket on its smooth functions .

Some examples of K3 surfaces are:

- The Fermat quartic surface, which is defined by the equation

```
x^4 + y^4 + z^4 + w^4 = 0
```

in the projective space P^3^ .
- The Kummer surface, which is obtained by taking the quotient of an abelian surface by the involution that negates all the coordinates .
- The Dwork family of quintic surfaces, which are defined by the equation

```
x^5 + y^5 + z^5 + w^5 + a x y z w = 0
```

in the projective space P^3^, where a is a parameter.
- The Schoen surface, which is obtained by gluing two copies of the Fermat quartic surface along a smooth curve of genus 19.

K3 surfaces are important objects in algebraic geometry, complex geometry, differential geometry, and mathematical physics. They have many applications and connections to topics such as moduli spaces, mirror symmetry, string theory, and 4-manifold theory  .



# CO-3 Measure the hardness and alkalinity of the water. K3

- Hardness of water is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water. It is expressed in terms of equivalent calcium carbonate (CaCO3).
- Alkalinity of water is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. It is expressed in terms of equivalent calcium carbonate (CaCO3) or milligrams per liter (mg/L) of bicarbonate (HCO3-), carbonate (CO3 2-) and hydroxide (OH-) ions in water.
- Hardness and alkalinity of water are important parameters for water quality assessment, treatment and use. They affect the corrosion, scaling, precipitation, buffering and pH of water.
- Hardness and alkalinity of water can be measured by titration methods using standard solutions of acids and bases.
- To measure the hardness of water, a sample of water is titrated with a standard solution of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) using a metal indicator such as Eriochrome Black T (EBT) or Calmagite. The endpoint of the titration is marked by a color change from red to blue. The volume of EDTA used is proportional to the hardness of water.
- To measure the alkalinity of water, a sample of water is titrated with a standard solution of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) using a pH indicator such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange. The endpoint of the titration is marked by a color change from pink to colorless (for phenolphthalein) or from yellow to orange (for methyl orange). The volume of H2SO4 used is proportional to the alkalinity of water.

# CO-4 Know the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol

- Phenol is an aromatic compound with the formula C6H5OH. It has a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a benzene ring.
- Phenol is a weak acid that can lose a proton to form phenoxide ion (C6H5O-). It can also undergo electrophilic aromatic substitution reactions with various electrophiles.
- Phenol can be prepared from various sources, such as coal tar, benzene, cumene, and chlorobenzene. Some of the common methods of preparation of phenol are:

  - From coal tar: Coal tar is a by-product of the destructive distillation of coal. It contains about 1% of phenol along with other aromatic compounds. Phenol can be extracted from coal tar by fractional distillation, followed by washing with sodium hydroxide solution to remove acidic impurities. The phenol can then be purified by crystallization or distillation.

  - From benzene: Benzene can be converted to phenol by treating it with concentrated sulfuric acid and nitric acid to form nitrobenzene. Nitrobenzene can then be reduced to phenylamine (aniline) by using tin and hydrochloric acid or iron and hydrochloric acid. Phenylamine can then be diazotized by treating it with sodium nitrite and hydrochloric acid to form phenyldiazonium chloride. Phenyldiazonium chloride can then be hydrolyzed by heating it with water to form phenol and nitrogen gas.

  - From cumene: Cumene is an alkylbenzene with the formula C6H5CH(CH3)2. It can be prepared by alkylation of benzene with propene in the presence of a catalyst. Cumene can then be oxidized by air to form cumene hydroperoxide, which can be cleaved by treating it with dilute sulfuric acid to form phenol and acetone.

  - From chlorobenzene: Chlorobenzene is an aryl halide with the formula C6H5Cl. It can be prepared by chlorination of benzene in the presence of a catalyst. Chlorobenzene can then be hydrolyzed by treating it with sodium hydroxide solution at high temperature and pressure to form phenol and sodium chloride. This method is also known as the Dow process.



# Formaldehyde & Urea Formaldehyde Resin, Adipic Acid and Paracetamol

## Formaldehyde

- Formaldehyde is a colorless, flammable, strong-smelling chemical that is used in building materials and to produce many household products.
- Formaldehyde is also a naturally occurring substance in the environment. It is produced by the combustion of organic matter and by the metabolic processes of living organisms.
- Formaldehyde is classified as a human carcinogen by the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) and the US National Toxicology Program (NTP). Exposure to formaldehyde can cause irritation of the eyes, nose, and throat, as well as respiratory and skin problems. Long-term exposure may increase the risk of certain cancers, such as nasopharyngeal cancer and leukemia.

## Urea Formaldehyde Resin

- Urea formaldehyde resin (UF) is a nontransparent thermosetting resin or polymer that is produced from urea and formaldehyde.
- UF is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects. UF has advantages such as low cost, high strength, water resistance, and fast curing.
- UF also has disadvantages such as high formaldehyde emission, poor weatherability, and low thermal stability. UF can release formaldehyde gas during its production, processing, and use, which can pose health and environmental hazards.
- UF can be modified by adding various additives, such as melamine, polyvinyl alcohol, and adipic acid dihydrazide, to reduce the free formaldehyde content and improve the performance of the resin.

## Adipic Acid

- Adipic acid is a white, crystalline organic acid that is mainly used as a precursor for the production of nylon.
- Adipic acid is also used as a food additive, a plasticizer, a lubricant, and a component of polyurethane and polyester resins.
- Adipic acid is produced industrially by the oxidation of cyclohexane or cyclohexanol with nitric acid, or by the hydrolysis of cyclohexane-1,6-dione. Adipic acid can also be synthesized from renewable sources, such as glucose, lignin, or fatty acids.
- Adipic acid is biodegradable and has a low toxicity. However, its production and use can cause environmental problems, such as greenhouse gas emissions, acid rain, and water pollution.

## Paracetamol

- Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is a widely used analgesic and antipyretic drug that is effective for mild to moderate pain and fever.
- Paracetamol is available over-the-counter or by prescription, and can be taken orally, rectally, or intravenously. Paracetamol is often combined with other drugs, such as opioids, caffeine, or antihistamines, to enhance its efficacy or reduce its side effects.
- Paracetamol is generally safe and well-tolerated when used at recommended doses. However, paracetamol can cause serious liver damage or even death when taken in excess or with alcohol. Paracetamol overdose is a common cause of poisoning and requires immediate medical attention.
- Paracetamol is synthesized from phenol and nitric acid, or from p-aminophenol and acetic anhydride. Paracetamol has a simple chemical structure and can be easily detected and quantified by various analytical methods.

A K3 surface is a type of algebraic surface that has some special properties. Here are some points to know about K3 surfaces:

- A K3 surface is a smooth proper geometrically connected algebraic surface that has trivial canonical bundle and no non-zero global holomorphic 2-forms .
- A K3 surface is a minimal surface of Kodaira dimension zero, meaning that it has no curves of negative self-intersection and no non-trivial holomorphic vector fields .
- A K3 surface is simply connected, meaning that it has no non-trivial loops that can be shrunk to a point .

# CO-5 Estimate the rate constant of reaction K3

- The rate constant of a reaction is a proportionality factor that relates the reaction rate to the concentrations of the reactants.
- The rate constant depends on the temperature, the activation energy, and the frequency factor of the reaction.
- The rate constant can have different units depending on the order of the reaction.
- For a termolecular reaction, such as A + B + C → products, the reaction rate is described by r=k3[A][B][C], where k3 is a termolecular rate constant.
- To estimate the rate constant of a termolecular reaction, one can use the Arrhenius equation: k3=Ae^(-Ea/RT), where A is the frequency factor, Ea is the activation energy, R is the gas constant, and T is the temperature.
- Alternatively, one can use experimental data to plot the natural logarithm of the rate constant versus the inverse of the temperature, and obtain the slope and the intercept of the linear regression. The slope is equal to -Ea/R and the intercept is equal to ln(A).
- If the reaction takes place in multiple steps, the overall rate constant can be calculated from the individual rate constants of each step, depending on the mechanism of the reaction .
- For example, if the reaction is a series of elementary steps, such as A → B → C → products, the overall rate constant is given by k=k1k2k3/(k1k2+k1k3+k2k3), where k1, k2, and k3 are the rate constants of each step.

# Page 30 of 40

## Introduction

This page is about the topic of Page 30 of 40. This is a topic that is related to the concept of pagination, which is the process of dividing a document or a collection of data into discrete pages. Pagination is useful for organizing and presenting information in a clear and accessible way. Pagination can also help with performance, usability, and aesthetics of a document or a data set.

## Types of Pagination

There are different types of pagination, depending on the purpose and the design of the document or the data set. Some common types of pagination are:

- **Numeric pagination**: This is the most basic and common type of pagination, where each page is assigned a number and the user can navigate through the pages by clicking on the numbers or using arrows. Numeric pagination is often used for documents that have a linear and sequential structure, such as books, articles, or reports.

- **Infinite scrolling**: This is a type of pagination where the user does not see any page numbers or boundaries, but instead the content is loaded continuously as the user scrolls down the page. Infinite scrolling is often used for data sets that have a dynamic and non-linear structure, such as social media feeds, news websites, or e-commerce platforms.

- **Load more button**: This is a type of pagination where the user sees a button at the end of the page that allows them to load more content on demand. Load more button is similar to infinite scrolling, but it gives the user more control and feedback over the loading process. Load more button is often used for data sets that have a large and variable size, such as search results, blogs, or galleries.

- **Cursor-based pagination**: This is a type of pagination where the user does not see any page numbers or boundaries, but instead the content is loaded based on a cursor or a pointer that indicates the position of the user in the data set. Cursor-based pagination is often used for data sets that have a complex and dynamic structure, such as databases, APIs, or streams.

## Advantages and Disadvantages of Pagination

Pagination has some advantages and disadvantages, depending on the type and the context of the document or the data set. Some of the advantages and disadvantages of pagination are:

- **Advantages**:

  - Pagination can improve the performance and efficiency of loading and rendering the content, by reducing the amount of data that needs to be transferred and processed at once.
  - Pagination can improve the usability and accessibility of the content, by allowing the user to navigate and locate the information they need more easily and quickly.
  - Pagination can improve the aesthetics and design of the content, by creating a consistent and coherent layout and appearance of the pages.

- **Disadvantages**:

  - Pagination can increase the complexity and difficulty of implementing and maintaining the content, by requiring more logic and code to handle the pagination logic and interface.
  - Pagination can decrease the engagement and satisfaction of the user, by creating a break or a gap in the content flow and continuity, and by requiring more user actions and interactions to access the content.

# Engineering Physics Lab

- Engineering Physics Lab is a course that introduces students to the basic concepts and methods of experimental physics.
- The course aims to develop the skills of observation, measurement, analysis, and interpretation of physical phenomena.
- The course also exposes students to various instruments and techniques used in physics experiments, such as oscilloscopes, multimeters, lasers, optical fibers, etc.
- The course consists of several experiments that cover topics such as mechanics, optics, electricity, magnetism, and modern physics.
- The experiments are designed to illustrate the principles and applications of physics in engineering and technology.
- The course requires students to perform the experiments in groups, record the data and observations, and write formal lab reports.
- The course also requires students to follow the safety rules and ethical standards of scientific research.

Some of the experiments that are typically included in the Engineering Physics Lab course are:

- Measurement of physical quantities and errors
- Verification of Hooke's law and Young's modulus
- Determination of the coefficient of viscosity of a liquid
- Verification of the laws of reflection and refraction
- Determination of the focal length of a lens
- Study of interference and diffraction of light
- Determination of the wavelength of a laser
- Study of polarization of light
- Study of the characteristics of a solar cell
- Study of the Hall effect
- Study of the photoelectric effect
- Study of the radioactive decay of a source

### 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles, applications, or techniques, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, electrochemistry, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments can provide qualitative or quantitative information about the composition, structure, or function of samples, such as identifying unknown substances, determining concentrations, measuring physical properties, etc.
- Analytical instruments can be used for various purposes, such as scientific research, quality control, environmental monitoring, medical diagnosis, forensic investigation, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different components, such as sources, detectors, transducers, amplifiers, filters, displays, etc., that work together to produce, manipulate, or record signals from the samples.
- Analytical instruments can have different characteristics, such as sensitivity, selectivity, accuracy, precision, resolution, range, etc., that affect their performance and reliability.
- Analytical instruments can have different advantages and limitations, such as speed, cost, complexity, portability, safety, etc., that determine their suitability and feasibility for different applications.
