### 2. To enable the students to understand about the analysis of chloride content, hardness, alkalinity of water.

- Chloride content is the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in water. Chloride ions are derived from natural sources such as rocks, soil, and seawater, as well as from human activities such as agriculture, industry, and sewage. Chloride content is an important indicator of water quality, as high levels of chloride can affect the taste, odor, and corrosion of water and pipes. Chloride content can be measured by titration with silver nitrate (AgNO3) solution, using potassium chromate (K2CrO4) as an indicator. The end point of the titration is marked by the formation of a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2CrO4).

- Hardness is the measure of the ability of water to precipitate soap. Hard water contains high concentrations of calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) ions, which react with soap to form insoluble scum. Hard water can also cause scaling and clogging of pipes, boilers, and appliances. Hardness can be classified as temporary or permanent, depending on the source of the ions. Temporary hardness is caused by the presence of bicarbonates (HCO3-) of calcium and magnesium, which can be removed by boiling the water. Permanent hardness is caused by the presence of sulfates (SO42-), chlorides, and nitrates (NO3-) of calcium and magnesium, which cannot be removed by boiling. Hardness can be measured by titration with ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) solution, using eriochrome black T (EBT) as an indicator. The end point of the titration is marked by the change of color of the indicator from wine red to blue.

- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. Alkaline water contains high concentrations of hydroxide (OH-), carbonate (CO32-), and bicarbonate (HCO3-) ions, which can react with acids to form water and salts. Alkalinity is an important parameter of water quality, as it affects the pH, buffering, and corrosion of water and pipes. Alkalinity can be measured by titration with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid (H2SO4), using phenolphthalein and methyl orange as indicators. The end point of the titration with phenolphthalein is marked by the change of color of the indicator from pink to colorless, indicating the consumption of all the hydroxide and carbonate ions. The end point of the titration with methyl orange is marked by the change of color of the indicator from yellow to orange, indicating the consumption of all the bicarbonate ions. The difference between the two end points is the amount of bicarbonate alkalinity, while the total amount of acid used is the amount of total alkalinity.

### 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of a liquid.

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a liquid is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in moles per liter. The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, where 0 is the most acidic, 7 is neutral, and 14 is the most basic. Some examples of liquids with different pH values are:

  - Lemon juice: pH 2
  - Vinegar: pH 3
  - Milk: pH 6.5
  - Water: pH 7
  - Baking soda solution: pH 9
  - Ammonia solution: pH 11
  - Bleach: pH 13

- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is expressed in units of force per unit length, such as newtons per meter (N/m) or dynes per centimeter (dyn/cm). Surface tension causes the liquid to behave like a thin elastic membrane that resists being stretched or broken. Some examples of surface tension phenomena are:

  - Water droplets form spherical shapes because surface tension minimizes the surface area for a given volume.
  - Insects can walk on water because their weight is less than the force of surface tension that supports them.
  - Soap bubbles are formed by a thin layer of water sandwiched between two layers of soap molecules that reduce the surface tension of water.
  - Detergents and surfactants lower the surface tension of water, making it easier to wet and clean surfaces.

- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a liquid is to flow. It is expressed in units of dynamic viscosity, such as pascal-seconds (Pa·s) or poise (P), or kinematic viscosity, such as square meters per second (m2/s) or stokes (St). Viscosity depends on the temperature, pressure, and composition of the liquid. Some examples of liquids with different viscosities are:

  - Honey: high viscosity, flows slowly and thickly
  - Water: low viscosity, flows easily and thinly
  - Oil: medium viscosity, flows moderately and smoothly
  - Glycerol: higher viscosity than water, but lower than honey
  - Mercury: lower viscosity than water, but higher than alcohol

- To measure the pH of a liquid, one can use a pH meter, a pH indicator, or a pH paper. A pH meter is an electronic device that measures the voltage difference between a reference electrode and a pH-sensitive electrode immersed in the liquid. A pH indicator is a chemical substance that changes color depending on the pH of the liquid. A pH paper is a strip of paper that is coated with a pH indicator and changes color when dipped in the liquid.

- To measure the surface tension of a liquid, one can use a force tensiometer, a drop shape analyzer, or a capillary rise method. A force tensiometer is a device that measures the force required to break or detach a liquid surface from a solid or another liquid surface. A drop shape analyzer is a device that measures the shape and size of a liquid drop hanging from a needle or resting on a surface. A capillary rise method is a technique that measures the height of a liquid column that rises in a narrow tube due to the balance of surface tension and gravity.

- To measure the viscosity of a liquid, one can use a viscometer, a rheometer, or a falling ball method. A viscometer is a device that measures the resistance of a liquid to flow through a tube or a gap under a constant pressure or a constant flow rate. A rheometer is a device that measures the deformation and stress of a liquid under a variable shear rate or a variable shear stress. A falling ball method is a technique that measures the time it takes for a ball to fall through a liquid under the influence of gravity and drag.



### 4. To enable the students to understand about the preparation of different resins.

- Resins are natural or synthetic organic substances that are usually solid or semi-solid, and have a high molecular weight and a complex structure.
- Resins are widely used in various industries, such as paints, adhesives, plastics, rubber, pharmaceuticals, cosmetics, etc.
- Resins can be classified into two main types: thermoplastic and thermosetting.
- Thermoplastic resins are resins that can be softened by heating and hardened by cooling, and can be reshaped repeatedly. Examples of thermoplastic resins are polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, etc.
- Thermosetting resins are resins that undergo irreversible chemical reactions when heated, and form cross-linked networks that cannot be melted or reshaped. Examples of thermosetting resins are phenol-formaldehyde, urea-formaldehyde, epoxy, etc.
- The preparation of different resins involves various steps, such as polymerization, condensation, curing, cross-linking, etc., depending on the type and properties of the resin.
- Polymerization is the process of joining small molecules (monomers) to form large molecules (polymers) by covalent bonds. Polymerization can be initiated by heat, light, catalysts, etc. Polymerization can be classified into two types: addition and condensation.
- Addition polymerization is the process of joining monomers without the elimination of any by-products. Examples of addition polymerization are the formation of polyethylene from ethylene, and the formation of polystyrene from styrene.
- Condensation polymerization is the process of joining monomers with the elimination of small molecules, such as water, alcohol, ammonia, etc. Examples of condensation polymerization are the formation of nylon from hexamethylenediamine and adipic acid, and the formation of polyester from ethylene glycol and terephthalic acid.
- Curing is the process of hardening and strengthening a resin by heating, cooling, or adding a curing agent. Curing can improve the mechanical, thermal, and chemical properties of the resin. Curing can be classified into two types: physical and chemical.
- Physical curing is the process of hardening a resin by cooling or solvent evaporation. Examples of physical curing are the hardening of wax and the drying of oil paints.
- Chemical curing is the process of hardening a resin by chemical reactions, such as cross-linking, oxidation, etc. Examples of chemical curing are the hardening of epoxy and the vulcanization of rubber.
- Cross-linking is the process of forming covalent bonds between different polymer chains, resulting in a three-dimensional network structure. Cross-linking can increase the rigidity, strength, and stability of the resin. Cross-linking can be induced by heat, light, radiation, catalysts, etc. Examples of cross-linked resins are phenol-formaldehyde, urea-formaldehyde, etc.



### 5. To enable the students to understand about the synthesis of organic compounds such as adipic acid and paracetamol by conventional and green route.

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid that is used as a precursor for the production of nylon-6,6, a synthetic polymer widely used in various applications.
- Paracetamol is an analgesic and antipyretic drug that is widely used for the relief of pain and fever.
- Conventional route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexane or a mixture of cyclohexanol and cyclohexanone with nitric acid, which produces large amounts of nitrous oxide, a greenhouse gas and an ozone depleter.
- Conventional route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the acetylation of phenol with acetic anhydride, which produces large amounts of acetic acid, a corrosive and toxic by-product.
- Green route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexene with hydrogen peroxide in a microemulsion system, which produces pure adipic acid in high yield and allows the recycling of the catalyst and the surfactant.
- Green route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the reaction of 4-aminophenol with acetic acid in water, which produces paracetamol in high yield and avoids the use of harmful organic solvents and acetic anhydride.
- The advantages of green route of synthesis are that they reduce the environmental impact, the energy consumption, the waste generation, and the cost of production, while improving the product quality, the safety, and the sustainability of the process.

## LIST OF EXPERIMENTS

An experiment is a scientific procedure that is carried out to test a hypothesis, observe a phenomenon, or measure an effect. Experiments usually involve manipulating one or more variables and measuring their effects on other variables. Experiments can be classified into different types based on their purpose, design, and method.

Some of the common types of experiments are:

- **Controlled experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher controls all the variables except the one that is being tested. The variable that is being tested is called the independent variable, and the variable that is being measured is called the dependent variable. The researcher also creates a control group that is identical to the experimental group except for the independent variable. The control group serves as a baseline for comparison. Controlled experiments are often used to establish causal relationships between variables.

- **Natural experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher does not manipulate any variables, but observes the effects of a natural event or phenomenon on the variables of interest. The natural event or phenomenon acts as the independent variable, and the researcher measures the dependent variable. Natural experiments are often used to study the effects of rare or unpredictable events that cannot be replicated in a laboratory.

- **Quasi-experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher manipulates the independent variable, but does not create a control group or randomly assign the participants to the experimental or control groups. The researcher may use existing groups or select the participants based on some criteria. Quasi-experiments are often used when controlled experiments are not feasible or ethical.

- **Field experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher conducts the experiment in a natural setting, such as a school, a workplace, or a community. The researcher may manipulate the independent variable, create a control group, or use existing groups. Field experiments are often used to study the effects of interventions or policies in real-world contexts.

- **Laboratory experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher conducts the experiment in a controlled and artificial setting, such as a laboratory. The researcher can manipulate the independent variable, create a control group, and randomly assign the participants to the experimental or control groups. Laboratory experiments are often used to study the effects of variables that can be precisely measured and controlled.

Some examples of experiments are:

- **The Milgram experiment**: This was a controlled experiment conducted by Stanley Milgram in the 1960s to study the effects of authority and obedience on human behavior. The participants were instructed to administer electric shocks to a learner (who was actually an actor) whenever the learner gave a wrong answer to a question. The shocks increased in intensity with each wrong answer, and the participants were urged to continue by an experimenter (who was actually a researcher). The experiment measured how far the participants would go in obeying the authority figure.

- **The Stanford prison experiment**: This was a quasi-experiment conducted by Philip Zimbardo in the 1970s to study the effects of social roles and power on human behavior. The participants were randomly assigned to play the roles of either prisoners or guards in a simulated prison. The experiment was supposed to last for two weeks, but it was terminated after six days due to the extreme and abusive behavior of the guards and the psychological distress of the prisoners.

- **The Hawthorne effect**: This was a natural experiment conducted by Elton Mayo and his colleagues in the 1920s and 1930s to study the effects of work conditions on productivity. The researchers observed the workers at the Hawthorne Works factory and changed various factors, such as lighting, temperature, breaks, and incentives. They found that the productivity of the workers increased regardless of the changes, and attributed this to the fact that the workers felt more motivated and valued because they were being observed and experimented on.

- **The marshmallow test**: This was a field experiment conducted by Walter Mischel and his colleagues in the 1960s and 1970s to study the effects of self-control and delayed gratification on future outcomes. The participants were preschool children who were given a choice between getting one marshmallow immediately or getting two marshmallows after waiting for 15 minutes. The experiment measured how long the children could resist the temptation of the marshmallow, and followed up with them later in life to assess their academic, social, and health outcomes.

- **The Asch conformity experiment**: This was a laboratory experiment conducted by Solomon Asch in the 1950s to study the effects of social pressure and conformity on human behavior. The participants were asked to judge the length of a line and compare it with three other lines of different lengths. The participants were surrounded



### 1. Calibration of Analytical Equipment and apparatus

Calibration is the process of evaluating and adjusting the precision and accuracy of measurement equipment. It is important to calibrate analytical equipment and apparatus to ensure the quality and reliability of the results obtained from them. Calibration also helps to maintain a safe working environment and comply with the regulatory standards.

Some of the steps involved in calibration of analytical equipment and apparatus are:

- Make an annual schedule for calibration of each instrument or equipment, indicating the dates when they are to be calibrated. The frequency of calibration may depend on the usage, the manufacturer's recommendations, and the in-house standard operating procedures .
- Use appropriate standards and reference materials for calibration. The standards should be traceable to national or international standards and have a known uncertainty. The reference materials should be stable, homogeneous, and representative of the samples to be analyzed .
- Follow the calibration procedures specified by the manufacturer or the in-house standard operating procedures. The procedures should include the steps to prepare the instrument or equipment, the calibration method, the acceptance criteria, and the documentation requirements .
- Record the calibration results and any adjustments made to the instrument or equipment. The records should include the date, time, operator, standard or reference material used, calibration method, calibration data, uncertainty, and any corrective actions taken .
- Label the instrument or equipment with the calibration status, date, and expiry. The label should be visible and durable. The instrument or equipment should not be used beyond the expiry date or if the calibration status is invalid .
- Review the calibration results and monitor the performance of the instrument or equipment. If the results are not satisfactory or the performance is deteriorating, the instrument or equipment should be recalibrated or repaired as soon as possible .



### 2. Determination of Hardness of Water by EDTA Method

Hardness of water is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water. These ions form insoluble precipitates with soap and interfere with the action of detergents. Hardness of water can be determined by various methods, but one of the most common and accurate methods is the complexometric titration using EDTA (ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) as the titrant.

EDTA is a chelating agent that can form stable complexes with metal ions by donating four pairs of electrons from its two nitrogen and four oxygen atoms. EDTA has a stronger affinity for calcium and magnesium ions than for other metal ions, so it can selectively titrate them in the presence of a suitable indicator.

The indicator used for this titration is Eriochrome Black T (EBT), which is a metallochromic indicator that changes color depending on the metal ion it is bound to. EBT forms a wine-red complex with calcium and magnesium ions at a pH of 10 ± 0.1, but when EDTA is added, it displaces EBT from the metal ions and forms a more stable complex. This causes the color of the solution to change from wine-red to blue, indicating the end point of the titration.

The procedure for the determination of hardness of water by EDTA method is as follows   :

- Take a sample volume of 20 mL (V mL) of water in a conical flask.
- Dilute the sample to 40 mL by adding 20 mL of distilled water.
- Add 1 mL of ammonia buffer to bring the pH to 10 ± 0.1.
- Add 1 or 2 drops of EBT indicator solution.
- The solution will turn wine-red due to the formation of EBT-metal complexes.
- Titrate the solution with EDTA solution of known concentration (C M) from a burette with vigorous shaking until the color changes from wine-red to blue.
- Note the volume of EDTA solution used (V E mL).
- Repeat the titration with a blank solution of distilled water and subtract the blank reading from the sample reading to get the corrected volume of EDTA solution used (V C mL).

The hardness of water can be calculated from the following formula:

Hardness of water (mg/L as CaCO3) = (V C x C M x 1000) / V

Where,

V C = corrected volume of EDTA solution used (mL)

C M = concentration of EDTA solution (mg CaCO3/mL)

V = volume of water sample (mL)

The hardness of water can be expressed in different units, such as ppm, grains per gallon, degrees Clark, degrees French, etc. The conversion factors are given below:

1 mg/L as CaCO3 = 1 ppm as CaCO3 = 0.058 grains per gallon as CaCO3 = 0.07 degrees Clark as CaCO3 = 0.1 degrees French as CaCO3

The hardness of water can be classified into different categories based on the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions, as shown in the table below:

| Category | Hardness (mg/L as CaCO3) |
|----------|--------------------------|
| Soft     | < 75                     |
| Moderately hard | 75 - 150          |
| Hard     | 150 - 300                |
| Very hard | > 300                    |

The hardness of water can also be divided into two types: temporary hardness and permanent hardness. Temporary hardness is caused by the presence of bicarbonates of calcium and magnesium, which can be removed by boiling the water. Permanent hardness is caused by the presence of sulfates, chlorides, and nitrates of calcium and magnesium, which cannot be removed by boiling the water. The total hardness of water is the sum of temporary and permanent hardness. The temporary and permanent hardness can be determined separately by titrating the water sample with EDTA before and after boiling, respectively. The difference between the total hardness and the permanent hardness gives the temporary hardness.



### 3. Determination of Alkalinity of water sample.

- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids.
- Alkalinity is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium, sodium, and potassium in water.
- Alkalinity is important for water quality because it affects the pH, corrosion, and disinfection processes of water.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid, using a suitable indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange.
- The titration curve of alkalinity shows the change of pH as the acid is added to the water sample. The curve has different regions depending on the types and concentrations of alkaline substances in the water sample.
- The titration curve can be divided into four regions: 
  - Region 1: The pH is above 8.3 and the water sample contains only hydroxides. The indicator is phenolphthalein and the color is pink. The endpoint is reached when the color changes to colorless. The volume of acid used is called P.
  - Region 2: The pH is between 8.3 and 4.5 and the water sample contains hydroxides and bicarbonates. The indicator is phenolphthalein and the color is colorless. The endpoint is reached when the color changes to pink. The volume of acid used is called P'.
  - Region 3: The pH is between 4.5 and 3.7 and the water sample contains bicarbonates and carbonates. The indicator is methyl orange and the color is yellow. The endpoint is reached when the color changes to orange. The volume of acid used is called M.
  - Region 4: The pH is below 3.7 and the water sample contains only carbonates. The indicator is methyl orange and the color is orange. The endpoint is reached when the color changes to red. The volume of acid used is called M'.
- The alkalinity of the water sample can be calculated from the volumes of acid used in the titration and the normality of the acid solution. The normality of the acid solution is the number of equivalents of acid per liter of solution. An equivalent of acid is the amount of acid that can donate one mole of hydrogen ions.
- The formulas for calculating the alkalinity are:

  - Total alkalinity = (P + M) x normality of acid x 50
  - Hydroxide alkalinity = P x normality of acid x 50
  - Carbonate alkalinity = (2P - M) x normality of acid x 50
  - Bicarbonate alkalinity = (M - P) x normality of acid x 50

- The units of alkalinity are milligrams per liter (mg/L) or parts per million (ppm) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3) equivalent. 
- The alkalinity of water can be classified as follows:

  - Low alkalinity: less than 50 mg/L of CaCO3
  - Moderate alkalinity: between 50 and 150 mg/L of CaCO3
  - High alkalinity: more than 150 mg/L of CaCO3



### 4. Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titration is a laboratory technique that involves adding a known amount of a solution (called the titrant) to another solution (called the analyte) until a chemical reaction is complete.
- The point at which the reaction is complete is called the endpoint, and it is usually indicated by a color change of an indicator or a change in the electrical properties of the solution.
- The pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, and it is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H+].
- The pH of a solution can be determined by titrating it with a standard solution of a strong acid or a strong base, depending on whether the analyte is a base or an acid, respectively.
- The titration curve is a plot of the pH of the solution versus the volume of the titrant added, and it shows how the pH changes during the titration.
- The equivalence point is the point at which the moles of the titrant are equal to the moles of the analyte, and it corresponds to the steepest part of the titration curve.
- The pH at the equivalence point depends on the strength of the acid and the base involved in the titration. For a strong acid-strong base titration, the pH at the equivalence point is 7. For a weak acid-strong base titration, the pH at the equivalence point is greater than 7. For a strong acid-weak base titration, the pH at the equivalence point is less than 7. For a weak acid-weak base titration, the pH at the equivalence point is close to the pKa or pKb of the weak acid or base.
- The titration method can be used to determine the concentration, purity, or identity of an unknown acid or base, or to measure the acidity or alkalinity of a solution.



Hello, I am Sydney, your AI assistant. I can help you with your study material. Here is the content for the topic of determination of surface tension of a given liquid.

### 5. Determination of surface tension of given liquid

- Surface tension is a property of liquids that causes them to behave as if they have a thin elastic skin on their surface.
- Surface tension is caused by the cohesive forces between the molecules of the liquid, which tend to minimize the surface area of the liquid.
- Surface tension can be measured by various methods, such as the capillary rise method, the drop weight method, the ring method, and the stalagmometer method.
- The capillary rise method is based on the principle that a liquid will rise or fall in a narrow tube (capillary) depending on the relative magnitude of the adhesive forces between the liquid and the tube, and the cohesive forces within the liquid.
- The drop weight method is based on the principle that the weight of a drop of liquid that detaches from a vertical capillary tube is proportional to the surface tension of the liquid.
- The ring method is based on the principle that the force required to detach a ring (usually made of platinum) from the surface of a liquid is proportional to the surface tension of the liquid.
- The stalagmometer method is based on the principle that the number of drops of a liquid that fall from a capillary tube of known diameter and length is inversely proportional to the surface tension of the liquid.

```markdown
# 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer

## Introduction

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied shear stress.
- Viscosity is important for many applications, such as lubrication, fluid dynamics, transport phenomena, and rheology.
- Viscosity can be measured by various methods, such as capillary viscometer, rotational viscometer, falling ball viscometer, etc.
- In this experiment, we will use a capillary viscometer, also known as an Ostwald viscometer, to determine the viscosity of a given liquid.

## Theory

- A capillary viscometer consists of a U-shaped glass tube with two bulbs and a narrow capillary section between them.
- The liquid to be tested is filled in the lower bulb and the upper bulb is connected to a constant pressure source, such as a water reservoir.
- When the pressure is applied, the liquid flows through the capillary section and the time taken for a fixed volume of liquid to pass through two marks on the tube is measured.
- The flow rate of the liquid is inversely proportional to its viscosity, according to the Hagen-Poiseuille equation:

  Q = (pi * r^4 * delta P) / (8 * eta * L)

  where Q is the flow rate, r is the radius of the capillary, delta P is the pressure difference, eta is the viscosity, and L is the length of the capillary.

- The viscosity of the liquid can be calculated by comparing the flow rate of the liquid with the flow rate of a reference fluid, such as water, whose viscosity is known at a given temperature.
- The relative viscosity of the liquid is defined as the ratio of the flow time of the liquid to the flow time of the reference fluid:

  eta_r = t / t_0

  where eta_r is the relative viscosity, t is the flow time of the liquid, and t_0 is the flow time of the reference fluid.

- The viscosity of the liquid can be obtained by multiplying the relative viscosity by the viscosity of the reference fluid:

  eta = eta_r * eta_0

  where eta is the viscosity of the liquid, and eta_0 is the viscosity of the reference fluid.

## Procedure

- Clean the capillary viscometer and dry it with a soft cloth.
- Fill the lower bulb with distilled water and connect the upper bulb to the water reservoir.
- Adjust the height of the reservoir so that the water level in the lower bulb is slightly above the upper mark on the tube.
- Open the valve and let the water flow through the capillary section until the water level reaches the lower mark on the tube.
- Start the stopwatch and record the time taken for the water level to rise from the lower mark to the upper mark on the tube. This is the flow time of the reference fluid, t_0.
- Repeat the measurement at least three times and take the average value of t_0.
- Drain the water from the viscometer and rinse it with the liquid to be tested.
- Fill the lower bulb with the liquid and repeat the steps 3 to 6 to obtain the flow time of the liquid, t.
- Measure the temperature of the liquid and the reference fluid and note it down.
- Calculate the relative viscosity, eta_r, and the viscosity, eta, of the liquid using the formulas given in the theory section.
- Compare the viscosity of the liquid with the values given in the literature or the standard tables and comment on the accuracy and precision of the experiment.

## Precautions

- The capillary viscometer should be clean and free of air bubbles before use.
- The liquid and the reference fluid should be at the same temperature during the experiment.
- The pressure difference should be constant and sufficient to maintain a steady flow of the liquid.
- The stopwatch should be started and stopped accurately at the marks on the tube.
- The measurements should be repeated several times and the average values should be used for calculations.
- The errors and uncertainties in the measurements should be estimated and reported.
```

### 7. Determination of the strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator

- Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a double salt of iron(II) sulfate and ammonium sulfate, with the formula FeSO4.(NH4)2SO4.6H2O.
- It is used as a primary standard in volumetric analysis, because it is stable in air and has a high equivalent mass.
- The strength of a ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be determined by titrating it with a standard solution of potassium permanganate (KMnO4), which is a strong oxidizing agent.
- The reaction between ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium permanganate is:

`5 FeSO4.(NH4)2SO4.6H2O + KMnO4 + 8 H2SO4 -> 5 Fe2(SO4)3 + K2SO4 + 2 MnSO4 + (NH4)2SO4 + 24 H2O`

- The end point of the titration is marked by the appearance of a faint pink color, due to the excess of potassium permanganate.
- However, the end point is not very sharp, because the ferrous ions are slowly oxidized by air in the presence of sulfuric acid, forming a brown precipitate of iron(III) hydroxide.
- To avoid this interference, an external indicator is used, such as diphenylamine sulfonate (DPS) or starch-iodide.
- An external indicator is added to a small amount of the titrant (potassium permanganate) in a separate flask, and a drop of this mixture is placed on a white tile or filter paper.
- As the titration proceeds, the drop of the mixture on the tile changes color, indicating the end point.
- The color change of the external indicator depends on the type of indicator used.
- For diphenylamine sulfonate, the color change is from colorless to blue-violet.
- For starch-iodide, the color change is from yellow to blue-black.

- The procedure for the determination of the strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator is as follows:

  - Prepare a standard solution of potassium permanganate by dissolving about 3.2 g of KMnO4 in 1 L of distilled water. Standardize the solution by titrating it against a known mass of sodium oxalate (Na2C2O4) in acidic medium.
  - Prepare a solution of ferrous ammonium sulfate by dissolving about 7 g of the salt in 250 mL of distilled water. Add 10 mL of dilute sulfuric acid to prevent hydrolysis of the salt.
  - Prepare an external indicator by dissolving about 0.1 g of diphenylamine sulfonate or starch-iodide in 100 mL of distilled water. Transfer some of the indicator solution to a small flask and add a few drops of potassium permanganate solution to it. Mix well and keep aside.
  - Pipette out 20 mL of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution into a conical flask and add 10 mL of dilute sulfuric acid. Heat the solution to about 60°C and titrate it with the potassium permanganate solution, using a burette.
  - Place a drop of the external indicator mixture on a white tile or filter paper. As the titration proceeds, observe the color change of the drop on the tile. When the drop changes color, stop the titration and note the burette reading. This is the end point of the titration.
  - Repeat the titration with two more aliquots of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution and take the average of the burette readings.
  - Calculate the strength of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution using the following formula:

    `Strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate = (Burette reading x Normality of KMnO4 x Equivalent mass of FeSO4.(NH4)2SO4.6H2O) / Volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution`

    where,

    - Burette reading is the volume of potassium permanganate solution used in mL
    - Normality of KMnO4 is the normality of the standardized potassium permanganate solution
    - Equivalent mass of FeSO4.(NH4)2SO4.6H2



### 8. Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator.

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a reducing agent such as ferrous ammonium sulphate.
- The reaction between potassium dichromate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is as follows:

`K2Cr2O7 + 6FeSO4 + 7H2SO4 -> Cr2(SO4)3 + 3K2SO4 + 6Fe2(SO4)3 + 7H2O`

- In this reaction, the orange color of dichromate ion (Cr2O7^2-^) changes to the green color of chromium ion (Cr^3+^) and the pale green color of ferrous ion (Fe^2+^) changes to the yellow color of ferric ion (Fe^3+^).
- The end point of the titration can be detected by the change of color from orange to green, which is the internal indicator.
- The procedure for the determination of the strength of potassium dichromate using internal indicator is as follows:

  - Weigh accurately about 0.5 g of potassium dichromate and dissolve it in 100 mL of distilled water in a volumetric flask. This is the standard solution of potassium dichromate.
  - Pipette out 10 mL of the standard solution into a conical flask and add 10 mL of dilute sulphuric acid.
  - Fill a burette with the given solution of ferrous ammonium sulphate and note the initial reading.
  - Titrate the potassium dichromate solution with the ferrous ammonium sulphate solution until the color changes from orange to green. Note the final reading of the burette.
  - Repeat the titration for two more times and take the average of the readings.
  - Calculate the strength of the ferrous ammonium sulphate solution using the formula:

    `Strength of FeSO4 = (Strength of K2Cr2O7 x Volume of K2Cr2O7 x 6) / (Volume of FeSO4 x 1000)`

    where the strength of potassium dichromate is 0.005 g/mL.



### 9. Determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder

Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium hypochlorite [Ca(OCl)<sub>2</sub>] and calcium chloride [CaCl<sub>2</sub>]. It is used as a disinfectant and a bleaching agent. The available chlorine in bleaching powder is the amount of chlorine that can be liberated when the powder is treated with dilute acid. The available chlorine is expressed as a percentage of the mass of the bleaching powder.

The procedure for determining the available chlorine in bleaching powder is as follows:

- Weigh a sample of bleaching powder and dissolve it in water.
- Transfer a measured volume of the solution to an Erlenmeyer flask and add excess of potassium iodide [KI] and acetic acid [CH<sub>3</sub>COOH].
- The chlorine in the bleaching powder reacts with the iodide to form iodine [I<sub>2</sub>].
- Titrate the liberated iodine with a standard solution of sodium thiosulfate [Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub>] using starch as an indicator.
- The end point is the disappearance of the blue color of the starch-iodine complex.
- The volume of sodium thiosulfate used is proportional to the amount of chlorine in the bleaching powder.
- Calculate the percentage of available chlorine using the following formula:

`% available chlorine = (V x N x 35.5 x 100) / (W x 1000)`

where V is the volume of sodium thiosulfate in mL, N is the normality of sodium thiosulfate, W is the mass of bleaching powder in g, and 35.5 is the equivalent mass of chlorine.



### 10. Determination of chloride content in water sample

- Chloride is one of the major anions found in natural waters. It originates from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, and domestic sewage.
- Chloride can affect the taste, corrosion, and biological activity of water. Therefore, it is important to monitor and control its concentration in drinking water, wastewater, and environmental samples.
- One of the common methods for determining chloride content in water sample is the **Mohr method**. It is based on the precipitation of chloride as silver chloride (AgCl) by adding a known amount of silver nitrate (AgNO3) solution in the presence of chromate indicator.
- The chromate indicator forms a red-brown precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2CrO4) when the silver nitrate is in slight excess. This indicates the end point of the titration.
- The amount of silver nitrate used is proportional to the chloride content in the water sample. The chloride concentration can be calculated by using the following formula:

`Cl- (mg/L) = (V1 * N1 * 35.45) / V2`

where V1 is the volume of silver nitrate solution used (mL), N1 is the normality of silver nitrate solution, 35.45 is the equivalent weight of chloride (g/equivalent), and V2 is the volume of water sample taken (mL).

- The steps for performing the Mohr method are as follows:

  1. Pipette a known volume of water sample (usually 50 mL) into a conical flask.
  2. Add 1-2 mL of potassium chromate indicator solution and mix well.
  3. Titrate the sample with standard silver nitrate solution from a burette until a permanent red-brown color appears. Record the final burette reading.
  4. Repeat the titration with a blank (distilled water) and a standard (known chloride solution) to check the accuracy and precision of the method.
  5. Calculate the chloride concentration in the water sample by using the formula given above.



### 11. Preparation of Phenol formaldehyde (PF) resin

Phenol formaldehyde (PF) resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the condensation reaction of phenol and formaldehyde. PF resin has high mechanical strength, chemical resistance, and thermal stability. It is widely used as an adhesive, a coating, a molding material, and a binder for wood products.

The preparation of PF resin involves the following steps:

- **Step 1**: Mixing of phenol and formaldehyde in a reaction vessel. The molar ratio of phenol to formaldehyde determines the degree of cross-linking and the properties of the final resin. A 1:1 ratio produces linear chains, while a higher ratio produces branched chains .
- **Step 2**: Catalysis of the reaction by either an acid or a base. The catalyst affects the rate, the selectivity, and the structure of the resin. Acid catalysts, such as hydrochloric acid or sulfuric acid, produce resins with higher molecular weight, higher viscosity, and faster curing. Base catalysts, such as ammonia or sodium hydroxide, produce resins with lower molecular weight, lower viscosity, and slower curing .
- **Step 3**: Removal of water and unreacted formaldehyde by distillation or vacuum. This step increases the concentration and the purity of the resin. The resin can be further modified by adding fillers, extenders, or modifiers to improve its properties .
- **Step 4**: Curing of the resin by heating, pressure, or radiation. This step causes the resin to cross-link and form a three-dimensional network. The curing conditions depend on the type and the application of the resin. The cured resin is insoluble, infusible, and thermally stable .



### 12. Preparation of Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin

Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects. It is produced from urea and formaldehyde by a condensation reaction that involves multiple steps and intermediate products. The general steps for the preparation of UF resin are:

- Step 1: Preparation of monomethylolurea (MMU). Urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1:1.5 to 2.5 in an aqueous solution at pH 6-11. The mixture is heated to 80-100°C for 10-30 minutes to form MMU, which is the first methylol derivative of urea. The reaction is reversible and depends on the temperature, pH, and concentration of the reactants. The equation for this step is:

`Urea + Formaldehyde ⇌ MMU + Water`

- Step 2: Preparation of dimethylolurea (DMU). MMU reacts further with formaldehyde to form DMU, which is the second methylol derivative of urea. The molar ratio of urea to formaldehyde is increased to 1:2.5 to 3.5 and the pH is maintained at 6-11. The mixture is heated to 80-100°C for 30-60 minutes to form DMU. The equation for this step is:

`MMU + Formaldehyde ⇌ DMU + Water`

- Step 3: Preparation of UF resin. DMU reacts further with formaldehyde and urea to form a complex mixture of oligomers and polymers with various degrees of methylation and condensation. The molar ratio of urea to formaldehyde is increased to 1:3.5 to 4.5 and the pH is lowered to 0.5-3.5 by adding a mineral or organic acid. The mixture is heated to 80-100°C for 60-120 minutes to form UF resin. The equation for this step is:

`DMU + Formaldehyde + Urea ⇌ UF resin + Water`

The final product is a viscous liquid that can be cured by heating or by adding a hardener. The curing process involves further condensation and cross-linking of the UF resin molecules, resulting in a rigid and insoluble network. The properties of the UF resin depend on the reaction conditions, the molar ratio of urea to formaldehyde, the degree of methylation and condensation, and the type and amount of hardener used. Some of the advantages of UF resin are its low cost, high strength, fast curing, and good adhesion to wood. Some of the disadvantages are its low water resistance, high formaldehyde emission, and limited shelf life.

### 14. Determination of Cell Conductance of a Solution

- Cell conductance of a solution is the reciprocal of its resistance, which is the opposition to the flow of electric current through the solution.
- Cell conductance of a solution depends on the concentration, charge, and mobility of the ions present in the solution, as well as the temperature and the geometry of the cell.
- A cell is a device that consists of two electrodes immersed in a solution and connected to a source of electric potential or a measuring instrument.
- A conductivity cell is a special type of cell that is used to measure the conductance of electrolytic solutions, which are solutions that can conduct electricity due to the presence of dissolved ions.
- A conductivity cell has two electrodes of known area and distance, which are usually made of platinum or stainless steel. The electrodes are either cylindrical or flat, and are either parallel or concentric to each other.
- The cell constant, C, is a characteristic of the conductivity cell that relates the conductance of the cell, G, to the conductivity of the solution, κ, by the equation:

  G = Cκ

- The cell constant has the units of inverse length (m^-1^) and depends on the geometry of the cell. It can be determined by measuring the conductance of a cell filled with a solution of known conductivity, such as a standard potassium chloride (KCl) solution.
- The conductivity of a solution, κ, is a measure of its ability to conduct electric current. It has the units of siemens per meter (S/m) and depends on the concentration, charge, and mobility of the ions in the solution, as well as the temperature.
- The conductivity of a solution can be calculated from the experimental conductance of the cell, G, and the cell constant, C, by the equation:

  κ = G/C

- The conductivity of a solution can also be calculated from the molar conductivities of the ions in the solution, Λ, and their concentrations, c, by the equation:

  κ = ΣciΛi

  where i denotes the different ions in the solution.

- The molar conductivity of an ion, Λ, is the conductivity of a solution containing one mole of the ion per unit volume. It has the units of siemens meter squared per mole (S m^2^ mol^-1^) and depends on the charge and mobility of the ion, as well as the temperature and the nature of the solvent.
- The molar conductivity of an ion can be measured by extrapolating the conductivity of a solution containing the ion to infinite dilution, where the ion is completely dissociated and free from any interaction with other ions or molecules. Alternatively, the molar conductivity of an ion can be estimated from the limiting molar conductivities of the ions, Λ^0^, and their degree of dissociation, α, by the equation:

  Λ = αΛ^0^

  where Λ^0^ is the molar conductivity of the ion at infinite dilution and α is the fraction of the ion that is dissociated in the solution.

### 15. Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

- Esters are organic compounds that have the general formula RCOOR', where R and R' are alkyl or aryl groups.
- Esters can undergo hydrolysis, which is a chemical reaction with water, to produce carboxylic acids and alcohols.
- The hydrolysis of esters can be catalyzed by acids or bases, depending on the conditions.
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters depends on several factors, such as the nature of the ester, the concentration of the ester, the concentration of the catalyst, the temperature, and the solvent.
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters can be expressed by the following rate law:

`rate = k[ester][catalyst]`

where k is the rate constant, [ester] is the concentration of the ester, and [catalyst] is the concentration of the acid or base catalyst.

- The rate constant k can be determined experimentally by measuring the rate of hydrolysis of a known concentration of ester under controlled conditions.
- One method to measure the rate of hydrolysis of esters is to use a conductometric titration, which is a technique that measures the change in electrical conductivity of a solution as a reaction proceeds.
- In a conductometric titration, a known volume of ester solution is mixed with a known volume of water and a known concentration of acid or base catalyst in a conical flask. The flask is connected to a conductivity meter, which records the conductivity of the solution as a function of time.
- As the hydrolysis of ester proceeds, the concentration of ester decreases, while the concentration of carboxylic acid and alcohol increases. The conductivity of the solution changes accordingly, depending on the nature and charge of the ions present in the solution.
- The conductivity-time curve obtained from the conductometric titration can be used to calculate the rate of hydrolysis of ester at different time intervals, by using the following formula:

`rate = -d[ester]/dt = -dC/dt`

where d[ester]/dt is the rate of change of ester concentration, dC/dt is the rate of change of conductivity, and the negative sign indicates that the ester concentration decreases as the reaction proceeds.

- The rate constant k can then be calculated by using the rate law and the known values of ester and catalyst concentrations at each time interval.
- The rate constant k can also be obtained by plotting the logarithm of ester concentration versus time, and finding the slope of the linear portion of the curve. The slope is equal to -k, according to the following equation:

`ln[ester] = -kt + ln[ester]0`

where [ester]0 is the initial concentration of ester, and t is the time.



### 16. Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds.

- Organic compounds are composed of carbon, hydrogen, and other elements such as oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur, phosphorus, halogens, etc.
- The detection and identification of these elements and functional groups in organic compounds are important for determining their structure and properties.
- There are various methods for detecting and identifying elements and functional groups in organic compounds, such as:

  - **Qualitative analysis**: This involves testing for the presence or absence of certain elements or functional groups using chemical reactions, color changes, precipitates, etc. For example, the Lassaigne's test for nitrogen, sulfur, and halogens, the ignition test for carbon and hydrogen, the bromine water test for unsaturation, etc.
  - **Quantitative analysis**: This involves measuring the amount or percentage of certain elements or functional groups in organic compounds using gravimetric, volumetric, or instrumental methods. For example, the estimation of carbon and hydrogen by Liebig's method, the estimation of nitrogen by Kjeldahl's method, the estimation of halogens by Carius method, etc.
  - **Spectroscopic analysis**: This involves using electromagnetic radiation to study the interaction of organic compounds with different regions of the electromagnetic spectrum, such as infrared, ultraviolet-visible, nuclear magnetic resonance, mass spectrometry, etc. This can provide information about the types, number, and arrangement of atoms and bonds in organic compounds. For example, the identification of functional groups by infrared spectroscopy, the identification of molecular formula by mass spectrometry, the identification of molecular structure by nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy, etc.

## Course Outcomes:

- Course outcomes are statements that describe what students are expected to know and be able to do by the end of a course.
- Course outcomes are aligned with the course objectives, which are derived from the program outcomes and the institutional mission and vision.
- Course outcomes are measurable, observable, and achievable within the scope and duration of the course.
- Course outcomes are written in terms of student learning, using action verbs that indicate the level of cognitive skills required.
- Course outcomes are used to guide the design of the course content, activities, assessments, and feedback.
- Course outcomes are communicated to the students at the beginning of the course and throughout the course as a way of clarifying the expectations and the purpose of the course.
- Course outcomes are evaluated at the end of the course to determine the extent to which the students have achieved the desired learning outcomes and to identify areas for improvement.

### Upon completion of the course the student should be able to:

- Define the basic concepts and terminology of artificial intelligence, such as agents, environments, rationality, search, knowledge representation, inference, planning, learning, natural language processing, computer vision, and robotics.
- Apply various search algorithms, such as uninformed search, informed search, local search, adversarial search, and constraint satisfaction, to solve problems that require finding optimal or near-optimal solutions.
- Represent and manipulate knowledge using propositional logic, first-order logic, and other formalisms, such as semantic networks, frames, and ontologies.
- Use inference methods, such as resolution, forward chaining, backward chaining, and probabilistic reasoning, to draw conclusions from knowledge bases.
- Design and implement planning systems that can generate sequences of actions to achieve goals in dynamic and uncertain environments.
- Explain and apply the basic principles and techniques of machine learning, such as supervised learning, unsupervised learning, reinforcement learning, and deep learning, to various domains and tasks.
- Analyze and process natural language texts and speech using methods such as tokenization, parsing, semantic analysis, information extraction, sentiment analysis, and dialogue systems.

### Course Outcomes Bloom's Taxonomy

- Course outcomes are brief statements that describe what students will be expected to learn by the end of the course.
- Course outcomes should be aligned with the course content, activities, and assessments, and should reflect the level of cognitive skills that students need to demonstrate.
- Bloom's taxonomy is a model of cognitive skills that can be used to classify educational learning objectives and to design course outcomes .
- Bloom's taxonomy consists of six levels of cognition, from lower to higher: remember, understand, apply, analyze, evaluate, and create.
- Each level of cognition has a set of verbs that can be used to write course outcomes that indicate the expected level of learning .
- For example, at the remember level, verbs such as define, list, recall, and identify can be used to write course outcomes that require students to recall factual information .
- At the create level, verbs such as design, construct, produce, and synthesize can be used to write course outcomes that require students to generate new ideas or products .
- Below are some examples of course outcomes that use Bloom's taxonomy:

  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **describe** the colonization of the Americas by the British, French and Spanish (remember level).
  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **explain** the main concepts and theories of social psychology (understand level).
  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **apply** the principles of accounting to prepare financial statements (apply level).
  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **analyze** the outcomes of the Civil War (analyze level).
  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **evaluate** the strengths and weaknesses of different research methods (evaluate level).
  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **create** a portfolio of original artworks that demonstrate their artistic skills and vision (create level).

- Bloom's taxonomy can help instructors design courses that support student learning at multiple levels of cognition, and can help students monitor their own learning progress and goals .

### CO2: Molecular and System Properties

- CO2 is a linear molecule with a bond angle of 180 degrees and a bond length of 116.3 pm.
- CO2 has a molecular mass of 44.0095 g/mol and a molar volume of 22.26 L/mol at STP.
- CO2 is a non-polar molecule with a dipole moment of zero and a low solubility in water.
- CO2 has a critical temperature of 304.13 K and a critical pressure of 7.38 MPa.
- CO2 has a triple point at 216.58 K and 0.517 MPa, where it can coexist as a solid, liquid, and gas.
- CO2 has a sublimation point at 194.65 K and 1 atm, where it can change directly from a solid to a gas.
- CO2 has a density of 1.977 kg/m3 at 0 °C and 1 atm, and 1.842 kg/m3 at 25 °C and 1 atm.
- CO2 has a specific heat capacity of 0.844 kJ/kg K at 25 °C and 1 atm, and 0.657 kJ/kg K at 100 °C and 1 atm.
- CO2 has a thermal conductivity of 0.0166 W/m K at 0 °C and 1 atm, and 0.0257 W/m K at 100 °C and 1 atm.
- CO2 has a viscosity of 0.0148 mPa s at 0 °C and 1 atm, and 0.0152 mPa s at 25 °C and 1 atm.
- CO2 has a surface tension of 0.012 N/m at its triple point, and 0.001 N/m at its critical point.
- CO2 has a low surface tension compared to water (0.072 N/m at 25 °C) because of its weak intermolecular forces.
- CO2 can form weak hydrogen bonds with water molecules, which lowers the surface tension of water.
- CO2 can react with water to form carbonic acid (H2CO3), which can further dissociate into bicarbonate (HCO3-) and carbonate (CO32-) ions.
- CO2 can also react with some organic compounds, such as amines, to form carbamates, which can be used for carbon capture and storage .



### Viscosity, conductance of solution, chloride and iron content in the water

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to deformation under shear stress. It is related to the internal friction of the fluid molecules. The SI unit of viscosity is the pascal-second (Pa s), which is equivalent to the newton-second per square meter (N s/m2) or the kilogram per meter per second (kg/m s).
- Conductance of solution is a measure of the ability of a solution to conduct electric current. It is the reciprocal of the resistance of the solution. The SI unit of conductance is the siemens (S), which is equivalent to the ampere per volt (A/V) or the coulomb per second per volt (C/s V).
- Chloride and iron content in the water are measures of the concentration of chloride and iron ions in the water. Chloride ions are negatively charged and are present in seawater, salt lakes, and some groundwater sources. Iron ions are positively charged and are present in some groundwater sources, especially those affected by iron-rich rocks or soils. The SI unit of concentration is the mole per cubic meter (mol/m3), which is equivalent to the gram per liter (g/L) or the milligram per milliliter (mg/mL).
- Some factors that affect the viscosity, conductance, and chloride and iron content of water are:

  - Temperature: As the temperature increases, the viscosity of water decreases, the conductance of water increases, and the solubility of chloride and iron ions increases. This means that warmer water is less viscous, more conductive, and has higher chloride and iron content than colder water.
  - Pressure: As the pressure increases, the viscosity of water increases, the conductance of water decreases, and the solubility of chloride and iron ions decreases. This means that higher pressure water is more viscous, less conductive, and has lower chloride and iron content than lower pressure water.
  - Dissolved solids: As the amount of dissolved solids increases, the viscosity of water increases, the conductance of water increases, and the chloride and iron content of water depend on the type and amount of dissolved solids. This means that water with more dissolved solids is more viscous, more conductive, and may have higher or lower chloride and iron content than water with less dissolved solids.
  - Dissolved gases: As the amount of dissolved gases increases, the viscosity of water decreases, the conductance of water decreases, and the chloride and iron content of water depend on the type and amount of dissolved gases. This means that water with more dissolved gases is less viscous, less conductive, and may have higher or lower chloride and iron content than water with less dissolved gases.



### K3

- K3 is a synthetic, artificially produced form of vitamin K that doesn’t occur naturally .
- K3 is also known as menadione .
- K3 may be converted into K2 in your liver.
- K3 is important for blood clotting and bone health.
- K3 has demonstrated anticancer and antibacterial properties in test-tube studies.
- K3 is not approved for human use by the FDA and may cause serious side effects such as liver damage, anemia, and oxidative stress .
- K3 is sometimes used as a supplement for animals, especially poultry and fish, to prevent bleeding disorders .
- K3 is also the name of a schedule form for reporting a partner’s share of income, deductions, credits, etc. from international sources for U.S. tax purposes.



### CO-3 Measure the hardness and alkalinity of the water. K3

- Hardness and alkalinity are two parameters that indicate the water quality and the suitability of water for various purposes.
- Hardness measures the total concentration of divalent salts, mainly calcium and magnesium, that can form insoluble deposits or scale in pipes, boilers, and appliances.
- Alkalinity measures the total amount of bases, mainly bicarbonate and carbonate, that can neutralize acids and buffer the pH of water .
- Hardness and alkalinity are related through the common ions formed in aquatic systems. The counter-ions associated with the bicarbonate and carbonate fraction of alkalinity are the principal cations responsible for hardness.
- To measure the hardness and alkalinity of water, the following steps are required:

  - Collect a representative water sample in a clean container and label it.
  - Determine the total hardness of the water sample by titrating it with a standard solution of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) using a suitable indicator such as eriochrome black T or calmagite.
  - Record the volume of EDTA used and calculate the total hardness in mg/L as calcium carbonate (CaCO3) using the formula:

    `Total hardness (mg/L as CaCO3) = (Volume of EDTA used in mL) x (Concentration of EDTA in mg/L as CaCO3) / (Volume of water sample in mL)`

  - Determine the total alkalinity of the water sample by titrating it with a standard solution of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) using a suitable indicator such as phenolphthalein or bromocresol green-methyl red.
  - Record the volume of H2SO4 used and calculate the total alkalinity in mg/L as CaCO3 using the formula:

    `Total alkalinity (mg/L as CaCO3) = (Volume of H2SO4 used in mL) x (Concentration of H2SO4 in mg/L as CaCO3) / (Volume of water sample in mL)`

  - Compare the results with the guidelines for water hardness and alkalinity for different purposes . For example, water with hardness less than 60 mg/L as CaCO3 is classified as soft, while water with alkalinity less than 20 mg/L as CaCO3 is classified as low.



### CO-4 Know the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol

Phenol is an aromatic compound with a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a benzene ring. It is also known as carbolic acid or phenic acid. Phenol has antiseptic and disinfectant properties and is used in the manufacture of plastics, dyes, explosives, and drugs.

There are several methods to prepare phenol from different sources, such as haloarenes, benzene, cumene, diazonium salts, and coal tar. Some of the common methods are:

- **From haloarenes:** Haloarenes are aromatic compounds with a halogen atom (-X) attached to a benzene ring. Examples of haloarenes are chlorobenzene, bromobenzene, and iodobenzene. When haloarenes are fused with sodium hydroxide at high temperature and pressure, sodium phenoxide is produced. Sodium phenoxide is then acidified with dilute acid or carbon dioxide to give phenol. This reaction is known as the Dow process or the Fries process.

  - Example: Chlorobenzene + NaOH → Sodium phenoxide + NaCl
  - Sodium phenoxide + HCl → Phenol + NaCl

- **From benzene:** Benzene is a simple aromatic compound with six carbon atoms and six hydrogen atoms. When benzene is treated with concentrated sulfuric acid, it forms benzene sulfonic acid. Benzene sulfonic acid is then fused with sodium hydroxide to give sodium phenoxide, which is acidified to give phenol. This reaction is known as the Raschig process.

  - Example: Benzene + H2SO4 → Benzene sulfonic acid + H2O
  - Benzene sulfonic acid + NaOH → Sodium phenoxide + Na2SO4
  - Sodium phenoxide + HCl → Phenol + NaCl

- **From cumene:** Cumene is an organic compound with the formula C6H5CH(CH3)2. It is also known as isopropylbenzene. When cumene is oxidized with air, it forms cumene hydroperoxide. Cumene hydroperoxide is then cleaved with dilute acid to give phenol and acetone. This reaction is known as the cumene process or the Hock process.

  - Example: Cumene + O2 → Cumene hydroperoxide
  - Cumene hydroperoxide + H2SO4 → Phenol + Acetone + H2O

- **From diazonium salts:** Diazonium salts are organic compounds with the general formula R-N2+X-, where R is an aryl group and X is a halide ion. They are formed by the reaction of aromatic amines with nitrous acid. When diazonium salts are treated with water, they undergo hydrolysis to give phenol and nitrogen gas. This reaction is known as the Sandmeyer reaction.

  - Example: C6H5NH2 + HNO2 → C6H5N2+Cl- + H2O
  - C6H5N2+Cl- + H2O → Phenol + N2 + HCl

- **From coal tar:** Coal tar is a dark brown liquid obtained from the distillation of coal. It contains various aromatic compounds, such as benzene, toluene, naphthalene, and phenol. When coal tar is fractionally distilled, phenol is obtained from the middle oil fraction at a temperature range of 443-503 K. This is one of the oldest methods of preparing phenol.

### Formaldehyde
- Formaldehyde is a naturally occurring organic compound with the formula CH2O and structure H−CHO.

- Formaldehyde is a by-product of combustion and certain natural processes, such as forest fires, automobile exhaust, and tobacco smoke. It can also be formed in the atmosphere by the action of sunlight and oxygen on atmospheric methane and other hydrocarbons .
- Formaldehyde exposure can cause irritation of the eyes, nose, and throat, as well as respiratory and skin problems. It can also increase the risk of some cancers, such as leukemia and nasopharyngeal cancer .

### Urea formaldehyde resin
- Urea formaldehyde resin is a synthetic polymer made by the condensation of urea and formaldehyde.
- Urea formaldehyde resin is a thermosetting resin, which means it can be cured by heat and pressure to form a hard and durable material.
- Urea formaldehyde resin is widely used as an adhesive for wood products, such as particleboard, plywood, and fiberboard. It is also used as a coating for paper, textiles, and electrical appliances.
- Urea formaldehyde resin can release formaldehyde gas during its production, processing, and use, which can pose health hazards to workers and consumers.
- Urea formaldehyde resin can be modified by adding other chemicals, such as melamine, phenol, or resorcinol, to improve its properties and reduce its formaldehyde emission.

### Adipic acid
- Adipic acid is an organic compound with the formula C6H10O4 and structure HOOC−(CH2)4−COOH.
- Adipic acid is a white, crystalline solid that is slightly soluble in water and alcohol.
- Adipic acid is mainly used as a raw material for the production of nylon 6,6, a synthetic fiber and plastic that is used in clothing, carpets, and engineering applications.
- Adipic acid is also used as a food additive, such as a flavoring agent, a gelling agent, and a leavening agent. It can impart a sour or tart taste to foods, such as soft drinks, candies, and baked goods.
- Adipic acid is usually produced by the oxidation of cyclohexane or cyclohexanol with nitric acid, which generates nitrous oxide, a greenhouse gas, as a by-product. Alternative methods of production, such as biotechnology or electrochemistry, are being developed to reduce the environmental impact of adipic acid manufacturing.

### Paracetamol
- Paracetamol is an organic compound with the formula C8H9NO2 and structure HOC6H4NHCOCH3.
- Paracetamol is a white, crystalline powder that is slightly soluble in water and alcohol.
- Paracetamol is a common over-the-counter medicine that is used to relieve pain and fever. It is also known as acetaminophen in the United States and Canada.
- Paracetamol works by inhibiting the synthesis of prostaglandins, which are chemical messengers that mediate inflammation and pain in the body.
- Paracetamol is generally safe and effective when taken as directed, but it can cause liver damage or failure if taken in large doses or with alcohol. Paracetamol overdose is a serious medical emergency that requires immediate treatment.




### K3

- K3 is a type of surface mount technology (SMT) component package that is used for integrated circuits (ICs).
- K3 stands for **Kilopass 3**, which is a trademark of Kilopass Technology Inc., a company that specializes in non-volatile memory (NVM) solutions.
- K3 is a **one-time programmable (OTP)** memory technology that can store data permanently without the need for external power or batteries.
- K3 is based on **antifuse** technology, which means that the memory cells are initially in a high-resistance state and can be programmed to a low-resistance state by applying a high voltage pulse.
- K3 has several advantages over other NVM technologies, such as:
  - High density: K3 can store up to 4 megabits (Mb) of data in a single chip, which is equivalent to 512 kilobytes (KB) of memory.
  - Low cost: K3 does not require any additional masks or process steps during fabrication, which reduces the manufacturing cost and time.
  - High reliability: K3 is immune to radiation, temperature, and voltage variations, which makes it suitable for harsh environments and long-term storage.
  - High security: K3 is resistant to reverse engineering, tampering, and hacking, which makes it ideal for storing sensitive data and intellectual property.
- K3 has several applications in various fields, such as:
  - Automotive: K3 can store calibration data, firmware updates, and diagnostic codes for electronic control units (ECUs) and sensors in vehicles.
  - Consumer electronics: K3 can store configuration data, user preferences, and encryption keys for smart phones, tablets, laptops, and other devices.
  - Industrial: K3 can store identification data, calibration data, and firmware updates for sensors, meters, and controllers in industrial systems.
  - Medical: K3 can store patient data, device settings, and firmware updates for medical devices and implants.
  - Military: K3 can store encryption keys, authentication codes, and firmware updates for communication devices, weapons, and satellites.

```markdown
### CO-5 Estimate the rate constant of reaction. K3

- The rate constant of a reaction, K3, is a measure of how fast the reaction proceeds at a given temperature and concentration of reactants.
- The rate constant of a reaction can be estimated by using experimental data, theoretical models, or empirical correlations.
- One method to estimate the rate constant of a reaction is to use the Arrhenius equation, which relates the rate constant to the activation energy and the temperature of the reaction:

  K3 = A * exp(-Ea / RT)

  where A is the pre-exponential factor, Ea is the activation energy, R is the gas constant, and T is the temperature.

- The Arrhenius equation can be used to estimate the rate constant of a reaction if the activation energy and the pre-exponential factor are known or can be determined from experimental data.
- Another method to estimate the rate constant of a reaction is to use the collision theory, which assumes that the reaction rate is proportional to the frequency and effectiveness of collisions between the reactant molecules:

  K3 = Z * f

  where Z is the collision frequency and f is the fraction of collisions that result in a reaction.

- The collision frequency can be estimated by using the kinetic theory of gases, which relates the average speed and the concentration of the gas molecules:

  Z = N * sqrt(8 * k * T / pi * m)

  where N is the number of molecules per unit volume, k is the Boltzmann constant, T is the temperature, and m is the mass of a molecule.

- The fraction of collisions that result in a reaction can be estimated by using the activation energy and the temperature of the reaction:

  f = exp(-Ea / RT)

  where Ea is the activation energy, R is the gas constant, and T is the temperature.

- The collision theory can be used to estimate the rate constant of a reaction if the activation energy and the concentration of the reactant molecules are known or can be determined from experimental data.
- A third method to estimate the rate constant of a reaction is to use empirical correlations, which are mathematical expressions that relate the rate constant to some measurable properties of the reaction system, such as the temperature, the pressure, the pH, the catalyst, etc.
- Empirical correlations are usually derived from fitting experimental data to a functional form, such as a power law, a logarithmic function, a polynomial function, etc.
- Empirical correlations can be used to estimate the rate constant of a reaction if the correlation parameters and the properties of the reaction system are known or can be measured.
- Empirical correlations may have limited applicability and accuracy, depending on the range and quality of the experimental data used to derive them.
```

# Nuclear Fusion

Nuclear fusion is the process of combining two or more atomic nuclei into a single, heavier nucleus, releasing a large amount of energy in the process. Nuclear fusion is the source of energy for the Sun and other stars.

Some of the main points to know about nuclear fusion are:

- Nuclear fusion requires high temperatures and pressures to overcome the electrostatic repulsion between positively charged nuclei. The conditions for fusion are achieved in the cores of stars, where the temperature is about 15 million degrees Celsius and the pressure is about 200 billion atmospheres.
- The most common fusion reaction in stars is the proton-proton chain, which converts four hydrogen nuclei (protons) into one helium nucleus (alpha particle), releasing two positrons, two neutrinos, and gamma rays. The mass of the helium nucleus is slightly less than the mass of the four protons, and the difference is converted into energy according to Einstein's equation E = mc^2.
- Another fusion reaction that occurs in stars is the carbon-nitrogen-oxygen (CNO) cycle, which uses carbon, nitrogen, and oxygen as catalysts to fuse hydrogen into helium. The CNO cycle is dominant in stars that are more massive and hotter than the Sun.
- Nuclear fusion can also be achieved artificially in devices called fusion reactors, which use magnetic fields or lasers to confine and heat plasma, a state of matter where atoms are ionized. The most common fusion reaction in fusion reactors is the deuterium-tritium (D-T) reaction, which fuses a deuterium nucleus (one proton and one neutron) and a tritium nucleus (one proton and two neutrons) into a helium nucleus and a neutron, releasing a lot of energy.
- Nuclear fusion has many potential advantages over nuclear fission, which is the process of splitting heavy nuclei into lighter ones, releasing energy. Nuclear fusion produces more energy per unit mass of fuel, does not produce radioactive waste, does not rely on scarce or unstable materials, and does not pose the risk of a nuclear meltdown or a nuclear weapon proliferation. However, nuclear fusion also faces many technical challenges, such as achieving and maintaining the required plasma conditions, extracting the energy efficiently, and dealing with the neutron radiation and the plasma instabilities.

# The topic is

The topic is a general term for the subject matter or main idea of a text, speech, or conversation. A topic can be broad or narrow, depending on the scope and purpose of the communication. Some examples of topics are:

- Climate change
- Artificial intelligence
- World War II
- Shakespeare's plays
- Healthy eating

To write or talk about a topic, you need to:

- Define the topic and its scope. For example, if you want to write about climate change, you need to specify what aspects of climate change you will focus on, such as its causes, effects, or solutions.
- Research the topic and gather relevant information from credible sources, such as books, journals, websites, or experts. You need to evaluate the quality and reliability of the sources and cite them properly.
- Organize the information into a logical structure, such as an outline, a mind map, or a diagram. You need to identify the main points and subpoints of your topic and arrange them in a coherent order.
- Write or speak about the topic using clear and concise language, appropriate tone and style, and supporting evidence and examples. You need to follow the conventions of the genre and format of your communication, such as an essay, a report, a presentation, or a podcast.
- Review and revise your communication to check for accuracy, clarity, coherence, and completeness. You need to proofread and edit your writing or speech to correct any errors, gaps, or inconsistencies. You may also seek feedback from others to improve your communication.

These are some general steps to write or talk about a topic. Depending on the specific topic, you may need to adapt or modify these steps to suit your needs and goals.
