# Page 31 of 40

## Topic: Nuclear Fusion

- Nuclear fusion is the process of combining two or more lighter nuclei into a heavier nucleus, releasing a large amount of energy in the process.
- Nuclear fusion is the source of energy for the Sun and other stars, where hydrogen nuclei fuse into helium nuclei under high temperature and pressure.
- Nuclear fusion has the potential to provide clean, abundant and sustainable energy for human civilization, but it is very difficult to achieve and control on Earth.
- The main challenges of nuclear fusion are:
  - Achieving the high temperature and pressure required to overcome the electrostatic repulsion between positively charged nuclei.
  - Sustaining the fusion reaction for a long enough time to produce more energy than is consumed by the heating and confinement systems.
  - Dealing with the high-energy neutrons and radiation that are produced by the fusion reaction, which can damage the materials and components of the fusion reactor.
- The main approaches to achieve nuclear fusion on Earth are:
  - Magnetic confinement fusion, which uses strong magnetic fields to confine and compress a plasma of hydrogen isotopes (deuterium and tritium) in a doughnut-shaped device called a tokamak or a stellarator.
  - Inertial confinement fusion, which uses powerful lasers or ion beams to heat and compress a tiny pellet of hydrogen isotopes to the point of ignition.
  - Alternative concepts, such as muon-catalyzed fusion, which uses muons (heavy electrons) to replace the electrons in hydrogen atoms and reduce the electrostatic repulsion, or fusion-fission hybrids, which use a fusion reactor to trigger a fission reaction in a subcritical mass of uranium or plutonium.
- The main advantages of nuclear fusion are:
  - It produces no greenhouse gases or long-lived radioactive waste, unlike nuclear fission or fossil fuels.
  - It uses abundant and widely available fuels, such as seawater and lithium, unlike oil, coal or uranium.
  - It has a high energy density, meaning that a small amount of fuel can produce a large amount of energy, unlike solar or wind power.
  - It has a low risk of accidents, proliferation or terrorism, unlike nuclear fission or fossil fuels.
- The main disadvantages of nuclear fusion are:
  - It is very expensive and complex to build and operate a fusion reactor, and the technology is still in the experimental stage, unlike nuclear fission or fossil fuels.
  - It requires a large amount of electricity to start and maintain the fusion reaction, and the net energy gain is still uncertain, unlike solar or wind power.
  - It produces high-energy neutrons and radiation that can damage the reactor and pose a health and environmental hazard, unlike solar or wind power.

# Course Objectives

- Course objectives are statements that describe what students should be able to do or know by the end of a course.
- Course objectives are usually derived from the course goals, which are broader and more general statements of the overall purpose and direction of the course.
- Course objectives are important for several reasons:
  - They provide a clear and measurable way to assess the learning outcomes of the course.
  - They guide the selection and organization of the course content, activities, and assessments.
  - They communicate the expectations and standards of the course to the students and other stakeholders.
- Course objectives should be SMART, which stands for Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, and Time-bound.
  - Specific: The objective should state exactly what the student should be able to do or know, using action verbs and clear criteria.
  - Measurable: The objective should be observable and quantifiable, using appropriate tools and methods to evaluate the student's performance.
  - Achievable: The objective should be realistic and attainable, considering the level and background of the students, the resources and time available, and the scope and difficulty of the course.
  - Relevant: The objective should be aligned with the course goals, the program outcomes, and the discipline standards.
  - Time-bound: The objective should indicate when and how often the student should demonstrate the desired skill or knowledge, using deadlines and benchmarks.
- Course objectives should be written in the following format: By the end of this course, the student will be able to (action verb) (criteria) (condition).
  - For example: By the end of this course, the student will be able to write a persuasive essay using at least three sources and following the APA style guidelines.

# 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, biological, or environmental properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles, applications, or techniques, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments can be used for various purposes, such as quality control, research and development, diagnosis, forensics, environmental monitoring, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different components, such as sources, detectors, transducers, amplifiers, filters, processors, displays, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different characteristics, such as sensitivity, selectivity, accuracy, precision, resolution, range, speed, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different advantages and limitations, such as cost, complexity, reliability, robustness, portability, etc.

Some of the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments are:

- Signal: A signal is a representation of information that can be measured, transmitted, processed, or displayed by an analytical instrument. A signal can be analog or digital, continuous or discrete, periodic or aperiodic, deterministic or stochastic, etc.
- Noise: Noise is any unwanted or random variation in a signal that can affect the quality or accuracy of an analytical instrument. Noise can be classified into different types, such as thermal noise, shot noise, flicker noise, environmental noise, etc.
- Signal-to-noise ratio (SNR): SNR is a measure of the quality of a signal relative to the noise level. SNR can be expressed in decibels (dB) as SNR = 10 log10 (Psignal / Pnoise), where Psignal and Pnoise are the average power of the signal and the noise, respectively. A higher SNR indicates a better signal quality and a lower noise level.
- Calibration: Calibration is the process of adjusting or correcting an analytical instrument to ensure that it produces accurate and reliable results. Calibration can be done by comparing the instrument output with a known standard or reference, or by applying a known correction factor or function.
- Validation: Validation is the process of verifying that an analytical instrument meets the specifications and requirements for its intended use. Validation can be done by testing the instrument performance, accuracy, precision, repeatability, reproducibility, etc. using appropriate methods and criteria.
- Maintenance: Maintenance is the process of keeping an analytical instrument in good working condition and preventing or repairing any faults or damages. Maintenance can be done by following the manufacturer's instructions, cleaning, lubricating, replacing, or upgrading the instrument components, or by performing regular checks and tests.



# Analysis of Chloride Content, Hardness, and Alkalinity

- Chloride content is the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in a water sample. Chloride ions are derived from sources such as salt, rocks, soil, industrial effluents, and sewage. Chloride content is an important parameter for assessing the quality of drinking water, irrigation water, and seawater.
- Hardness is the measure of the concentration of calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) ions in a water sample. These ions are derived from sources such as limestone, dolomite, gypsum, and other minerals. Hardness affects the properties of water such as taste, corrosiveness, scaling, and soap consumption. Hardness is expressed in terms of equivalent calcium carbonate (CaCO3) concentration.
- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of a water sample to neutralize acids. Alkalinity is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonate (HCO3-), carbonate (CO32-), and hydroxide (OH-) ions in water. These ions are derived from sources such as limestone, dolomite, carbon dioxide, and photosynthesis. Alkalinity affects the pH, buffering, and biological activity of water.

## Methods of Analysis

- Chloride content can be determined by titration with silver nitrate (AgNO3) solution using potassium chromate (K2CrO4) as an indicator. The end point is marked by the formation of a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2CrO4). The chloride content is calculated by the following formula:

  `Cl- (mg/L) = (AgNO3 (mL) x N x 35.45) / sample (mL)`

  where N is the normality of AgNO3 solution.

- Hardness can be determined by titration with ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) solution using eriochrome black T (EBT) as an indicator. The end point is marked by the change of color from wine red to blue. The hardness is calculated by the following formula:

  `Hardness (mg/L as CaCO3) = (EDTA (mL) x N x 1000) / sample (mL)`

  where N is the normality of EDTA solution.

- Alkalinity can be determined by titration with sulfuric acid (H2SO4) solution using phenolphthalein and methyl orange as indicators. The end point with phenolphthalein is marked by the change of color from pink to colorless. The end point with methyl orange is marked by the change of color from yellow to orange. The alkalinity is calculated by the following formula:

  `Alkalinity (mg/L as CaCO3) = (H2SO4 (mL) x N x 50) / sample (mL)`

  where N is the normality of H2SO4 solution.



# Water

Water is a substance composed of the chemical elements hydrogen and oxygen and existing in gaseous, liquid, and solid states. It is one of the most plentiful and essential of compounds. A tasteless and odourless liquid at room temperature, it has the important ability to dissolve many other substances.

Some of the properties and characteristics of water are:

- Water is a tiny molecule. It consists of three atoms: two of hydrogen and one of oxygen. Water molecules cling to each other because of a force called hydrogen bonding.
- Water is a shape-shifter. It exists in three states on Earth: liquid, gas, and solid. Liquid water is the most common form of water on Earth. Gas water is water vapor, which is invisible and can be found in the air. Solid water is ice, which can take various forms such as snow, hail, and glaciers.
- Water is the liquid that makes life on Earth possible. As water cycles from the air to the land to the sea and back again, water shapes our planet and nearly every aspect of our lives. All living things, from tiny cyanobacteria to giant blue whales, need water to survive.
- Water is an inorganic compound with the chemical formula H2O. It is a transparent, tasteless, odorless, and nearly colorless chemical substance, and it is the main constituent of Earth's hydrosphere and the fluids of all known living organisms (in which it acts as a solvent).
- Water has a high specific heat capacity, meaning it can absorb and release a lot of heat without changing its temperature much. This helps regulate the climate and the body temperature of living organisms.
- Water has a high surface tension, meaning it can form droplets and bubbles and resist external forces. This allows water to flow, stick to surfaces, and support some insects and plants on its surface.
- Water has a high latent heat of vaporization, meaning it takes a lot of energy to change water from liquid to gas. This helps cool the environment and the body when water evaporates.
- Water has a high dielectric constant, meaning it can reduce the electric force between charged particles. This makes water a good solvent for many substances, especially ionic compounds and polar molecules.
- Water has a low density as a solid, meaning ice floats on liquid water. This prevents water from freezing from the bottom up and allows life to exist under the ice.

Water is also used for various purposes by humans, such as:

- Drinking, cooking, and washing
- Agriculture, irrigation, and livestock
- Industry, manufacturing, and energy
- Transportation, navigation, and recreation
- Waste management, sanitation, and hygiene
- Firefighting, cooling, and heating
- Culture, religion, and art

Water is a vital resource for humans and the environment, but it is also facing many challenges, such as:

- Scarcity, drought, and desertification
- Pollution, contamination, and degradation
- Overuse, wastage, and inefficiency
- Conflict, inequality, and injustice
- Climate change, sea level rise, and extreme weather

Water is a complex and fascinating topic that can be studied from many perspectives and disciplines, such as:

- Chemistry, physics, and biology
- Geology, hydrology, and meteorology
- Ecology, environmental science, and conservation
- Engineering, technology, and innovation

# 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of

- pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in moles per liter. The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, with 7 being neutral, lower values being acidic, and higher values being basic. Examples of solutions with different pH values are lemon juice (pH 2), vinegar (pH 3), water (pH 7), baking soda (pH 9), and ammonia (pH 11).
- Surface tension is a measure of the energy required to increase the surface area of a liquid, or the force required to increase the length of a liquid surface by a given amount. It is caused by the cohesive forces between molecules at the surface of a liquid, which tend to minimize the surface area. Surface tension is expressed in units of joules per square meter (J/m^2) or newtons per meter (N/m). Examples of liquids with different surface tensions are water (72.8 mN/m), ethanol (22.3 mN/m), and mercury (485 mN/m).
- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow. It is caused by the frictional forces between the molecules of a fluid, which oppose their relative motion. Viscosity is expressed in units of pascal-seconds (Pa·s) or poise (P). Examples of fluids with different viscosities are honey (10 Pa·s), water (0.001 Pa·s), and air (0.000018 Pa·s).



# A liquid

- A liquid is one of the four states of matter, along with solid, gas, and plasma.
- A liquid is a fluid that can flow and take the shape of its container, but has a constant volume.
- A liquid has a definite surface, unlike a gas, but can change its shape, unlike a solid.
- A liquid is composed of molecules that are loosely bound by intermolecular forces, such as hydrogen bonds, dipole-dipole interactions, or van der Waals forces.
- A liquid can undergo phase transitions to a solid (freezing) or a gas (evaporation or boiling) depending on the temperature and pressure.
- A liquid can also form mixtures with other liquids (solutions) or solids (suspensions or colloids) depending on the solubility and polarity of the substances involved.
- A liquid has some properties that depend on its specific composition, such as density, viscosity, surface tension, boiling point, and freezing point.
- A liquid has some properties that are common to all fluids, such as pressure, buoyancy, and Bernoulli's principle.



# Preparation of different resins

Resins are natural or synthetic organic substances that are usually solid or semi-solid, and have a high molecular weight. Resins are widely used in various industries, such as paints, adhesives, plastics, rubber, and pharmaceuticals.

There are different types of resins, such as thermosetting resins, thermoplastic resins, and natural resins. The preparation of different resins depends on their chemical structure and properties.

## Thermosetting resins

Thermosetting resins are resins that undergo irreversible chemical reactions when heated, and form cross-linked networks that are rigid and insoluble. Thermosetting resins include phenolic resins, epoxy resins, polyester resins, and urea-formaldehyde resins.

### Phenolic resins

Phenolic resins are the oldest and most widely used thermosetting resins. They are obtained by the condensation of phenol and formaldehyde in the presence of an acid or a base catalyst. Phenolic resins have high mechanical strength, thermal stability, and resistance to fire and chemicals. They are used for making laminates, plywood, adhesives, coatings, and molded products.

### Epoxy resins

Epoxy resins are resins that contain one or more epoxy groups in their molecules. They are prepared by the reaction of epichlorohydrin and bisphenol A, or by the oxidation of aliphatic or aromatic compounds. Epoxy resins have excellent adhesion, electrical insulation, and corrosion resistance. They are used for making adhesives, coatings, composites, and electrical components.

### Polyester resins

Polyester resins are resins that contain ester groups in their molecules. They are prepared by the condensation of dicarboxylic acids and diols, or by the ring-opening polymerization of cyclic esters. Polyester resins have good flexibility, durability, and weather resistance. They are used for making fibers, films, bottles, and boat hulls.

### Urea-formaldehyde resins

Urea-formaldehyde resins are resins that contain urea and formaldehyde units in their molecules. They are prepared by the condensation of urea and formaldehyde in the presence of an acid or a base catalyst. Urea-formaldehyde resins have low cost, high hardness, and good thermal stability. They are used for making particle boards, adhesives, and foams.

## Thermoplastic resins

Thermoplastic resins are resins that can be softened and reshaped by heating, and hardened by cooling. Thermoplastic resins include polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, polyvinyl chloride, and nylon.

### Polyethylene

Polyethylene is the most common and simplest thermoplastic resin. It is prepared by the polymerization of ethylene, either by free radical initiation or by coordination catalysis. Polyethylene has low density, high strength, and good chemical resistance. It is used for making bags, bottles, pipes, and films.

### Polypropylene

Polypropylene is a thermoplastic resin that is similar to polyethylene, but has a methyl group attached to every other carbon atom in the chain. It is prepared by the polymerization of propylene, either by free radical initiation or by coordination catalysis. Polypropylene has higher melting point, stiffness, and hardness than polyethylene. It is used for making fibers, carpets, containers, and automotive parts.

### Polystyrene

Polystyrene is a thermoplastic resin that is derived from styrene, a monomer that contains a benzene ring. It is prepared by the polymerization of styrene, either by free radical initiation or by coordination catalysis. Polystyrene has high clarity, rigidity, and impact resistance. It is used for making cups, plates, packaging, and insulation.

### Polyvinyl chloride

Polyvinyl chloride is a thermoplastic resin that is derived from vinyl chloride, a monomer that contains a chlorine atom. It is prepared by the polymerization of vinyl chloride, either by free radical initiation or by coordination catalysis. Polyvinyl chloride has high versatility, durability, and flame retardance. It is used for making pipes, cables, flooring, and clothing.

### Nylon

Nylon is a thermoplastic resin that is derived from the condensation of diamines and dicarboxylic acids, or from the ring-opening polymerization of lactams. Nylon has high strength, elasticity, and abrasion resistance. It is used for making fibers, fabrics, carpets, and gears.

## Natural resins

Natural resins are resins that are obtained from plants or animals, and have



# Synthesis of Adipic Acid

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid with the formula C6H10O4. It is mainly used for the production of nylon, polyurethanes, and other polymers.
- The conventional method for the synthesis of adipic acid is the oxidation of a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol (KA oil) with nitric acid  .
- The oxidation of KA oil involves several steps and intermediate products, such as nitrosonium ion, nitrosocyclohexane, 6-hydroxyimino-6-nitrohexane, 6-oxo-6-nitrohexanoic acid, and glutaric acid.
- The overall reaction can be written as:

```
C6H10O + 4 HNO3 → C6H10O4 + 4 NO2 + 2 H2O
```

- The main drawback of this method is the formation of nitrous oxide (N2O), a greenhouse gas and an ozone-depleting agent, as a by-product .
- Alternative methods for the synthesis of adipic acid from renewable sources, such as glucose and its derivatives, have been explored using various catalysts and oxidants.
- Some examples of these methods are:

  - Oxidation of glucose with nitric acid to give gluconic acid, which is then dehydrated to 2-keto-gluconic acid, and further oxidized to adipic acid.
  - Oxidation of glucaric acid, a product of glucose oxidation, with hydrogen peroxide to give adipic acid.
  - Oxidation of muconic acid, a product of glucose fermentation, with hydrogen peroxide or ozone to give adipic acid.
  - Oxidation of cyclohexene, a product of glucose hydrogenation, with hydrogen peroxide or oxygen to give adipic acid.

- These methods have the advantages of using renewable feedstocks, avoiding the use of nitric acid, and reducing the emission of N2O. However, they also face some challenges, such as low selectivity, high cost, and catalyst deactivation.



# Acid and Paracetamol by Conventional and Green Route

- Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is a common painkiller and antipyretic drug that is derived from phenol.
- The conventional route for the synthesis of paracetamol involves the following steps :
  - Nitration of phenol with nitric acid to form p-nitrophenol.
  - Reduction of p-nitrophenol with tin and hydrochloric acid to form p-aminophenol.
  - Acetylation of p-aminophenol with acetic anhydride to form paracetamol.
- The conventional route has some drawbacks, such as:
  - Low yield and selectivity of p-nitrophenol due to the formation of o-nitrophenol as a by-product.
  - Generation of toxic and corrosive wastes, such as nitric acid, tin chloride, and acetic acid.
  - Use of hazardous and expensive reagents, such as nitric acid, tin, and acetic anhydride.
- The green route for the synthesis of paracetamol involves the following steps  :
  - Hydroxylation of phenol with hydrogen peroxide and a catalyst, such as iron(III) chloride, to form hydroquinone.
  - Amidation of hydroquinone with acetic acid and a catalyst, such as sodium acetate, under microwave irradiation to form paracetamol.
- The green route has some advantages, such as:
  - High yield and selectivity of paracetamol due to the direct conversion of phenol to paracetamol without intermediate steps.
  - Reduction of environmental impact and cost by using benign and cheap reagents, such as hydrogen peroxide, acetic acid, and sodium acetate.
  - Elimination of solvent and heating by using microwave irradiation as an energy source.



# LIST OF EXPERIMENTS

- An experiment is a scientific procedure that aims to test a hypothesis, answer a question, or demonstrate a known fact.
- Experiments usually involve manipulating one or more variables, measuring their effects, and comparing them with a control group.
- Experiments can be classified into different types based on their purpose, design, and outcome.

## Types of experiments

- **Descriptive experiments** describe the characteristics or behavior of a phenomenon or a group of subjects. For example, observing the color of a flower or the height of a plant.
- **Relational experiments** examine the relationship between two or more variables. For example, testing the correlation between smoking and lung cancer or between temperature and pressure.
- **Causal experiments** test the cause-and-effect relationship between an independent variable (the cause) and a dependent variable (the effect). For example, testing the effect of fertilizer on plant growth or of a drug on blood pressure.
- **Quasi-experiments** are similar to causal experiments, but they lack random assignment of subjects to groups or conditions. For example, comparing the academic performance of students in different schools or of patients in different hospitals.
- **Natural experiments** exploit natural variations in the independent variable to study its effect on the dependent variable. For example, studying the impact of a natural disaster, a policy change, or a historical event on a population or an outcome.

## Design of experiments

- **Experimental design** is the process of planning and conducting an experiment to ensure its validity, reliability, and efficiency.
- Experimental design involves choosing the appropriate type of experiment, selecting the variables, defining the hypothesis, determining the sample size, assigning the subjects to groups or conditions, controlling the confounding factors, applying the treatments, measuring the outcomes, and analyzing the data.
- Some common experimental designs are:

  - **Randomized controlled trial (RCT)**: A causal experiment that randomly assigns subjects to either a treatment group or a control group, and compares their outcomes. RCTs are considered the gold standard for testing the efficacy of interventions in medicine, education, and social sciences.
  - **Factorial design**: An experiment that tests the effect of two or more independent variables (factors) and their interactions on a dependent variable. For example, testing the effect of temperature and humidity on plant growth.
  - **Repeated measures design**: An experiment that measures the same subjects under different conditions or at different times. For example, testing the effect of caffeine on alertness before and after drinking coffee.
  - **Crossover design**: A type of repeated measures design that switches the subjects between the treatment and control groups. For example, testing the effect of two different drugs on pain relief by giving each subject both drugs in a random order.
  - **Matched pairs design**: An experiment that pairs subjects with similar characteristics and assigns them to different groups or conditions. For example, testing the effect of a diet on weight loss by matching subjects based on their age, gender, and body mass index.

## Outcome of experiments

- The outcome of an experiment is the result or the conclusion that can be drawn from the data collected and analyzed.
- The outcome of an experiment can be expressed in different ways, such as:

  - **Statistical significance**: The probability that the observed difference or effect is due to chance rather than to the independent variable. A low p-value (usually less than 0.05) indicates that the difference or effect is statistically significant, meaning that it is unlikely to occur by chance.
  - **Effect size**: The magnitude or the strength of the difference or effect. A large effect size indicates that the difference or effect is meaningful or important, regardless of the statistical significance.
  - **Confidence interval**: The range of values that contains the true value of the difference or effect with a certain level of confidence (usually 95%). A narrow confidence interval indicates that the estimate is precise and reliable.
  - **Practical significance**: The relevance or the usefulness of the difference or effect for the real-world situation or problem. A practically significant difference or effect is one that has a noticeable or beneficial impact on the outcome of interest.



# Calibration of Analytical Equipment and Apparatus

- Calibration is the process of verifying the accuracy and precision of an analytical instrument or device by comparing its measurements with a known standard of reference.
- Calibration ensures that the instrument or device is functioning properly and producing reliable and consistent results.
- Calibration is essential for maintaining the quality and validity of analytical data and for complying with regulatory requirements and standards.
- Calibration should be performed periodically, before and after each use, or whenever there is a change in the operating conditions or performance of the instrument or device.
- Calibration should be documented and recorded, and the calibration status should be indicated on the instrument or device.
- Calibration can be classified into two types: external and internal calibration.
  - External calibration involves using a separate device or material that has a known value or property to calibrate the instrument or device. For example, using a thermometer to calibrate a temperature sensor, or using a certified reference material to calibrate a spectrophotometer.
  - Internal calibration involves using a built-in feature or function of the instrument or device to calibrate itself. For example, using a zero-adjustment knob to calibrate a balance, or using a self-test function to calibrate a pH meter.
- Calibration can also be classified into two levels: single-point and multi-point calibration.
  - Single-point calibration involves using only one standard value or property to calibrate the instrument or device. For example, using a pure water sample to calibrate a conductivity meter, or using a blank solution to calibrate a colorimeter.
  - Multi-point calibration involves using two or more standard values or properties to calibrate the instrument or device. For example, using a series of standard solutions to calibrate a titrator, or using a calibration curve to calibrate a chromatograph.
- Calibration can be affected by various factors, such as environmental conditions, human errors, instrument drift, wear and tear, and contamination. Therefore, calibration should be performed under controlled and consistent conditions, following standard operating procedures, and using appropriate and traceable standards and methods.



# 2. Determination of Hardness of water sample by EDTA method.

- Hardness of water is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water.
- Hard water causes scaling, corrosion, and reduced efficiency of boilers, pipes, and appliances.
- EDTA (ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) is a chelating agent that forms stable complexes with metal ions, such as Ca2+ and Mg2+.
- EDTA method is a titrimetric method that uses a standard solution of EDTA to titrate a water sample against an indicator that changes color at the end point.
- The indicator used is Eriochrome Black T (EBT), which forms a wine-red complex with free Ca2+ and Mg2+ ions in water.
- As EDTA is added, it reacts with the metal ions and releases the EBT, which turns blue.
- The end point is reached when the last trace of red color disappears and the solution becomes blue.
- The hardness of water can be calculated from the volume of EDTA used and its concentration.

## Procedure:

- Pipette out 50 mL of the water sample into a conical flask.
- Add 2 mL of buffer solution (pH 10) and 2 drops of EBT indicator.
- Titrate the solution with 0.01 M EDTA from a burette until the color changes from wine-red to blue.
- Note down the burette reading at the end point.
- Repeat the titration with two more aliquots of the water sample and take the average of the three readings.

## Calculation:

- The hardness of water is expressed in terms of ppm (parts per million) of CaCO3 equivalent.
- 1 ppm of CaCO3 equivalent = 1 mg of CaCO3 in 1 L of water.
- The molar mass of CaCO3 is 100 g/mol.
- The molar mass of EDTA is 292.24 g/mol.
- The molarity of EDTA is 0.01 mol/L.
- The stoichiometry of the reaction between EDTA and Ca2+ or Mg2+ is 1:1.

- Therefore, the hardness of water (in ppm) can be calculated by the formula:

`Hardness = (Volume of EDTA x Molarity of EDTA x 1000 x 100) / Volume of water sample`

- For example, if the average volume of EDTA used is 12.5 mL, then the hardness of water is:

`Hardness = (12.5 x 0.01 x 1000 x 100) / 50`

`Hardness = 500 ppm`



# Determination of Alkalinity of Water Sample

- Alkalinity is the measure of the ability of water to neutralize acids.
- Alkalinity is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides in water.
- Alkalinity is important for aquatic life, corrosion control, and water treatment processes.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid, using a suitable indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange.
- The endpoint of the titration is marked by a color change of the indicator from pink to colorless (phenolphthalein) or from yellow to orange (methyl orange).
- The amount of acid used to reach the endpoint is proportional to the alkalinity of the water sample.
- The alkalinity can be expressed in different units, such as milligrams per liter (mg/L) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3), milliequivalents per liter (meq/L), or degrees of hardness (dH).
- The alkalinity can be classified into different types, such as total alkalinity, phenolphthalein alkalinity, methyl orange alkalinity, carbonate alkalinity, bicarbonate alkalinity, and hydroxide alkalinity, depending on the pH range and the predominant species in the water sample.



# Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a solution of unknown concentration (analyte).
- pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H+].
- Determination of pH by titrimetric method involves using a pH meter to monitor the change in pH of the analyte solution as the titrant is added gradually.
- A pH meter consists of a glass electrode that is sensitive to H+ ions and a reference electrode that provides a constant potential. The potential difference between the two electrodes is proportional to the pH of the solution.
- A plot of pH versus volume of titrant added is called a titration curve. The shape of the titration curve depends on the nature and strength of the acid and base involved in the reaction.
- The point where the titration curve changes slope sharply is called the equivalence point. It corresponds to the point where the moles of titrant and analyte are equal, according to the stoichiometry of the reaction.
- The point where the titration curve crosses the pH value of the indicator used is called the endpoint. It corresponds to the point where the indicator changes color, signaling the completion of the titration.
- The equivalence point and the endpoint are ideally the same, but in practice there may be a small difference due to the limitations of the indicator. The choice of indicator depends on the pH range of the equivalence point.
- The concentration of the analyte can be calculated from the volume and concentration of the titrant at the equivalence point or the endpoint, using the formula: C_a V_a = C_t V_t, where C_a and C_t are the concentrations of the analyte and the titrant, respectively, and V_a and V_t are the volumes of the analyte and the titrant, respectively.



# 5. Determination of surface tension of given liquid

- Surface tension is a property of liquids that causes them to behave as if they have a thin elastic skin on their surface.
- Surface tension is caused by the cohesive forces between the molecules of the liquid, which tend to minimize the surface area of the liquid.
- Surface tension can be measured by various methods, such as the capillary rise method, the drop weight method, the ring method, and the stalagmometer method.
- In this experiment, we will use the stalagmometer method to determine the surface tension of a given liquid.
- A stalagmometer is a device that consists of a glass tube with a bulb at one end and a fine nozzle at the other end.
- The tube is filled with the liquid to be tested and then inverted over a graduated cylinder.
- The liquid drips out of the nozzle due to gravity and the surface tension of the liquid.
- The number of drops that fall out of the tube until the liquid level reaches a certain mark on the tube is counted and recorded.
- The same procedure is repeated with a reference liquid, such as distilled water, whose surface tension is known.
- The surface tension of the given liquid is then calculated using the formula:

$$\gamma = \frac{n_1 \gamma_1}{n_2}$$

where $\gamma$ is the surface tension of the given liquid, $n_1$ is the number of drops of the reference liquid, $\gamma_1$ is the surface tension of the reference liquid, and $n_2$ is the number of drops of the given liquid.

- The experiment should be performed at a constant temperature and with clean and dry apparatus.
- The sources of error and uncertainty in the experiment include the variation in the size and shape of the drops, the evaporation of the liquid, the contamination of the liquid, and the parallax error in reading the liquid level.



# 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied force.
- Viscosity depends on the intermolecular forces, temperature and pressure of the fluid.
- A viscometer is a device that measures the viscosity of a fluid by observing its flow through a narrow tube or a capillary.
- The flow rate of the fluid depends on the pressure difference between the two ends of the tube, the length and diameter of the tube, and the viscosity of the fluid.
- The most common type of viscometer is the Ostwald viscometer, which consists of a U-shaped glass tube with two bulbs and a constant diameter capillary connecting them.
- The procedure to determine the viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer is as follows:

  - Fill the viscometer with the liquid to be tested and immerse it in a water bath at a constant temperature.
  - Suck some of the liquid into the upper bulb using a rubber tube and a suction pump, until it reaches above the upper mark on the capillary.
  - Close the rubber tube with a clamp and remove the suction pump.
  - Open the clamp and allow the liquid to flow down the capillary under gravity, and note the time taken for the liquid to flow from the upper mark to the lower mark on the capillary. This is the flow time of the liquid, t.
  - Repeat the experiment for at least three times and take the average value of t.
  - Repeat the same procedure for a standard liquid of known viscosity, such as water or glycerin, and obtain the average flow time, t0.
  - Calculate the relative viscosity of the liquid, ηr, using the formula:

    ηr = t / t0

  - Calculate the viscosity of the liquid, η, using the formula:

    η = ηr × η0

    where η0 is the viscosity of the standard liquid at the same temperature.

  - Record the observations and results in a tabular form.

```markdown
# Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator

## Aim
To determine the strength of a given solution of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using an external indicator.

## Principle
Ferrous ammonium sulfate (FAS) is a salt of iron (II) that can be oxidized by a standard solution of potassium permanganate (KMnO4) in acidic medium. The reaction is:

5FeSO4(NH4)2SO4.6H2O + KMnO4 + 8H2SO4 -> 5Fe2(SO4)3 + K2SO4 + 2(NH4)2SO4 + 6MnSO4 + 24H2O

The end point of the titration is detected by using an external indicator, such as starch solution, which forms a blue-black complex with iodine. A small amount of potassium iodide (KI) is added to the FAS solution, which reacts with a trace of KMnO4 to produce iodine:

2KMnO4 + 10KI + 8H2SO4 -> 6K2SO4 + 2MnSO4 + 8H2O + 5I2

The iodine liberated is then titrated with a standard solution of sodium thiosulfate (Na2S2O3), which reduces it to iodide:

I2 + 2Na2S2O3 -> 2NaI + Na2S4O6

The end point of this titration is detected by adding a few drops of starch solution, which turns blue-black in the presence of iodine. When all the iodine is consumed, the blue-black color disappears.

## Procedure
- Pipette out 10 mL of the given FAS solution into a conical flask and add 10 mL of 1 M sulfuric acid and 2 g of KI crystals.
- Titrate the solution with the given KMnO4 solution until a faint pink color persists for 30 seconds.
- Note down the volume of KMnO4 used as V1.
- Add 2 mL of starch solution to the flask and continue the titration until the blue-black color disappears.
- Note down the volume of KMnO4 used as V2.
- Repeat the above steps for two more trials and take the average of V1 and V2.

## Calculations
- The strength of FAS solution can be calculated by using the following formula:

Strength of FAS = (V1 - V2) x N x 0.392 / V

where V1 and V2 are the average volumes of KMnO4 used in the two titrations, N is the normality of KMnO4 solution, 0.392 is the equivalent weight of FAS, and V is the volume of FAS solution taken for titration.

- The percentage purity of FAS can be calculated by using the following formula:

Percentage purity of FAS = (Strength of FAS / Theoretical strength of FAS) x 100

where the theoretical strength of FAS is 3.92 g/100 mL.
```



# Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a reducing agent such as ferrous ammonium sulphate.
- The reaction between potassium dichromate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is as follows:

  $$K_2Cr_2O_7 + 6FeSO_4 + 7H_2SO_4 \rightarrow Cr_2(SO_4)_3 + 3K_2SO_4 + 6Fe_2(SO_4)_3 + 7H_2O$$

- The end point of the titration can be detected by the change in colour of the solution from green to reddish-brown, due to the formation of ferric ions.
- This colour change is the internal indicator of the titration, and no external indicator is required.
- The strength of potassium dichromate can be calculated by using the following formula:

  $$\text{Strength of potassium dichromate} = \frac{6 \times \text{Normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate} \times \text{Volume of ferrous ammonium sulphate used}}{\text{Volume of potassium dichromate used}}$$

- The normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate can be determined by titrating it against a standard solution of potassium permanganate, using phenolphthalein as an external indicator. The reaction is as follows:

  $$5FeSO_4 + KMnO_4 + 8H_2SO_4 \rightarrow 5Fe_2(SO_4)_3 + K_2SO_4 + MnSO_4 + 8H_2O$$

- The end point of the titration is marked by the disappearance of the pink colour of the permanganate solution.
- The normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate can be calculated by using the following formula:

  $$\text{Normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate} = \frac{5 \times \text{Normality of potassium permanganate} \times \text{Volume of potassium permanganate used}}{\text{Volume of ferrous ammonium sulphate used}}$$

- The procedure for the determination of the strength of potassium dichromate using internal indicator is as follows:

  - Prepare a standard solution of potassium permanganate by dissolving about 3.2 g of KMnO4 in distilled water and make up the volume to 1 L in a volumetric flask.
  - Prepare a solution of ferrous ammonium sulphate by dissolving about 7 g of FeSO4.(NH4)2SO4.6H2O in distilled water and add 20 mL of dilute sulphuric acid to prevent oxidation. Make up the volume to 250 mL in a volumetric flask.
  - Pipette out 10 mL of the ferrous ammonium sulphate solution into a conical flask and add 20 mL of dilute sulphuric acid.
  - Titrate the solution against the potassium permanganate solution, using phenolphthalein as an indicator, until the pink colour of the permanganate solution disappears. Note down the volume of potassium permanganate used.
  - Repeat the titration for two more times and take the average volume of potassium permanganate used.
  - Calculate the normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate by using the formula given above.
  - Pipette out 10 mL of the potassium dichromate solution into another conical flask and add 20 mL of dilute sulphuric acid.
  - Titrate the solution against the ferrous ammonium sulphate solution, using the colour change of the solution as the internal indicator, until the solution turns from green to reddish-brown. Note down the volume of ferrous ammonium sulphate used.
  - Repeat the titration for two more times and take the average volume of ferrous ammonium sulphate used.
  - Calculate the strength of potassium dichromate by using the formula given above.



# Determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder

- Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium hypochlorite (Ca(OCl)2), calcium chloride (CaCl2), and calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH)2).
- Available chlorine is the amount of chlorine that can be liberated from bleaching powder by the action of acids.
- Available chlorine is expressed as a percentage of the mass of bleaching powder.
- The determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder is based on the reaction of bleaching powder with excess iodide in acidic medium, followed by titration of the liberated iodine with sodium thiosulfate.

## Procedure

- Weigh accurately about 0.3 g of bleaching powder and transfer it to a 250 mL conical flask.
- Add about 100 mL of distilled water and shake well to dissolve the bleaching powder.
- Filter the solution through a filter paper into another 250 mL conical flask.
- Pipette out 20 mL of the filtrate into a 250 mL conical flask and add 10 mL of 10% potassium iodide solution and 5 mL of 2 M sulfuric acid.
- Titrate the liberated iodine with 0.1 M sodium thiosulfate solution using starch as an indicator.
- Repeat the titration with a blank solution prepared by adding 20 mL of distilled water, 10 mL of 10% potassium iodide solution and 5 mL of 2 M sulfuric acid in a 250 mL conical flask.

## Calculation

- Let V1 be the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used for the sample titration and V2 be the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used for the blank titration.
- The amount of iodine liberated in the sample titration is equal to the amount of available chlorine in the bleaching powder.
- The amount of iodine liberated in the blank titration is due to the impurities in the reagents.
- The net amount of iodine liberated in the sample titration is given by:

  I2 = (V1 - V2) x N x 0.0127

  where N is the normality of sodium thiosulfate solution and 0.0127 is the equivalent mass of iodine.

- The percentage of available chlorine in the bleaching powder is given by:

  % available chlorine = (I2 x 100) / W

  where W is the mass of bleaching powder taken for the analysis.



# Determination of chloride content in water sample

- Chloride ions in water can be determined by various methods, such as argentometric, potentiometric, or colorimetric methods.
- One of the most common and simple methods is the argentometric method, also known as Mohr's method, which involves titration of the water sample with silver nitrate solution using potassium chromate as an indicator.
- The principle of this method is based on the precipitation reaction between silver ions and chloride ions, forming a white precipitate of silver chloride:

    Ag+ + Cl- -> AgCl(s)

- The endpoint of the titration is detected by the formation of a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate, which indicates that all the chloride ions have been precipitated and the excess silver ions have reacted with the chromate ions:

    2Ag+ + CrO4^2- -> Ag2CrO4(s)

- The pH of the water sample should be adjusted to 7-8 by adding acid or alkali, as the chromate indicator is sensitive to pH changes and may give false results if the pH is too low or too high.
- The concentration of chloride ions in the water sample can be calculated from the volume of silver nitrate solution used and its molarity, using the following formula:

    Cl- (mg/L) = (AgNO3 (mL) x AgNO3 (M) x 35.45 x 1000) / V (mL)

    where AgNO3 (mL) is the volume of silver nitrate solution used in the titration, AgNO3 (M) is the molarity of silver nitrate solution, 35.45 is the atomic weight of chlorine, and V (mL) is the volume of water sample taken for the titration.

- The procedure for the determination of chloride content in water sample by Mohr's method is as follows:

    1. Take 50 mL of water sample (V mL) and dilute to 100 mL with distilled water in a volumetric flask.
    2. If the water sample is highly colored, add 3 mL of aluminum hydroxide and shake well, allow to settle, filter, wash and collect the filtrate.
    3. Bring the pH of the water sample to 7-8 by adding acid or alkali, using a pH meter or pH paper to check the pH.
    4. Add 1 mL of potassium chromate indicator to the water sample and mix well.
    5. Titrate the water sample with standard silver nitrate solution from a burette until a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate is obtained. Note the final volume of silver nitrate solution used (AgNO3 (mL)).
    6. Repeat the titration with another aliquot of water sample and take the average volume of silver nitrate solution used.
    7. Calculate the concentration of chloride ions in the water sample using the formula given above.

- The advantages of this method are that it is simple, rapid, and accurate. The disadvantages are that it requires a standard silver nitrate solution, which is unstable and needs to be standardized frequently, and that it may give erroneous results if the water sample contains other ions that can precipitate with silver ions, such as bromide, iodide, cyanide, sulfide, or thiocyanate.



# Preparation of Phenol Formaldehyde (PF) Resin

- Phenol formaldehyde resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the condensation reaction of phenol and formaldehyde .
- Phenol formaldehyde resin can be prepared by either acid- or base-catalyzed method . The choice of the catalyst affects the properties and applications of the resin.
- In the acid-catalyzed method, phenol and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1:1 or less, and a strong acid such as hydrochloric acid or sulfuric acid is added as the catalyst . The reaction proceeds at a low temperature (below 100°C) and produces a linear resin with a high molecular weight and a high degree of cross-linking . This resin is called novolac and it is hard, brittle, and insoluble in most solvents . Novolac resin requires a curing agent such as hexamethylenetetramine to form a three-dimensional network and become a thermoset .
- In the base-catalyzed method, phenol and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of more than 1:1, and a weak base such as ammonia or sodium hydroxide is added as the catalyst . The reaction proceeds at a high temperature (above 100°C) and produces a branched resin with a low molecular weight and a low degree of cross-linking . This resin is called resol and it is soft, flexible, and soluble in some solvents . Resol resin can self-cure without a curing agent and form a thermoset .
- Phenol formaldehyde resin can also be modified by adding other monomers such as urea, melamine, or furfural. These co-condensed resins have different properties and applications than the pure phenol formaldehyde resin. For example, phenol-urea-formaldehyde resin has improved water resistance and reduced formaldehyde emission than urea-formaldehyde resin.



# Preparation of Urea Formaldehyde (UF) Resin

- Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF resin is synthesized by the condensation reaction of urea and formaldehyde in the presence of a base or an acid catalyst .
- The reaction can be carried out in one or more steps, depending on the desired properties and applications of the resin  .
- The general steps for the preparation of UF resin are as follows:

  1. Mixing of urea and formaldehyde in an aqueous solution at a molar ratio of 2-3:1 and a pH of 6-11. This step produces methylolureas, which are intermediates that have one or more hydroxymethyl groups attached to the nitrogen atom of urea.
  2. Heating the mixture to at least 80°C to promote further condensation and polymerization of the methylolureas. This step results in the formation of linear or branched oligomers with various degrees of cross-linking.
  3. Adjusting the pH of the mixture to 0.5-3.5 by adding a mineral or organic acid. This step lowers the solubility and increases the viscosity of the resin, making it suitable for coating or molding applications.
  4. Optionally, neutralizing the resin with ammonia or another base to reduce the free formaldehyde content and improve the stability and shelf life of the resin. This step also affects the curing rate and the mechanical properties of the resin.

- The final product is a viscous liquid or a solid powder that can be cured by heat, pressure, or catalysts to form a hard and durable material. The curing process involves the formation of methylene and methylene ether bridges between the urea units, as well as the elimination of water and formaldehyde.
- The properties and performance of UF resin depend on various factors, such as the molar ratio of urea and formaldehyde, the type and amount of catalyst, the reaction conditions, the degree of polymerization, the curing conditions, and the additives .
- Some of the advantages of UF resin are its low cost, high strength, good adhesion, fast curing, and high water resistance. Some of the disadvantages are its low thermal stability, poor weatherability, limited color range, and potential health hazards due to formaldehyde emission.

# Determination of Cell Conductance of a Solution

- Cell conductance of a solution is the reciprocal of its resistance, i.e. G = 1/R, where G is the conductance and R is the resistance.
- Cell conductance of a solution depends on the concentration, charge, and mobility of the ions present in the solution, as well as the geometry of the cell used to measure it.
- A cell used to measure the conductance of a solution is called a conductance cell. It consists of two electrodes immersed in the solution, connected to a source of alternating current and a device to measure the resistance, such as a Wheatstone bridge.
- The resistance of the solution between the electrodes is given by R = ρl/A, where ρ is the resistivity of the solution, l is the distance between the electrodes, and A is the area of cross-section of the electrodes.
- The cell constant, C, is defined as C = l/A, and it has the units of m^-1. The cell constant depends on the shape and size of the electrodes and the cell, and it can be determined by measuring the resistance of a solution of known conductivity, such as 0.0200 M KCl.
- The conductivity, κ, of a solution is the reciprocal of its resistivity, i.e. κ = 1/ρ, and it has the units of S m^-1 or Ω^-1 m^-1. The conductivity of a solution is proportional to its cell conductance, i.e. κ = GC, where G is the cell conductance and C is the cell constant.
- To determine the cell conductance of a solution, the following steps are followed:
  - Fill the conductance cell with the solution and connect it to the Wheatstone bridge and the alternating current source.
  - Adjust the Wheatstone bridge until the galvanometer shows zero deflection, indicating that the bridge is balanced.
  - Read the value of the resistance, R, from the bridge.
  - Calculate the cell conductance, G, by using the formula G = 1/R.
  - Calculate the conductivity, κ, by using the formula κ = GC, where C is the cell constant.

# 15. Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

- Hydrolysis of esters is a chemical reaction in which an ester is converted into a carboxylic acid and an alcohol by the addition of water.
- The general equation for the hydrolysis of esters is:

`R-CO-OR' + H2O -> R-COOH + R'-OH`

where R and R' are alkyl or aryl groups.

- The hydrolysis of esters can be catalyzed by either acids or bases. In this experiment, we will use an acid-catalyzed hydrolysis of ethyl acetate, which is an ester of acetic acid and ethanol.

`CH3-CO-OCH2-CH3 + H2O -> CH3-COOH + CH3-CH2-OH`

- The rate of the hydrolysis of ethyl acetate depends on the concentration of ethyl acetate, water, and the acid catalyst. The rate law for the reaction is:

`rate = k[CH3-CO-OCH2-CH3][H2O][H+]`

where k is the rate constant, and [ ] denotes the concentration of the species.

- To determine the rate constant of the hydrolysis of ethyl acetate, we will use a method called conductometry, which measures the electrical conductivity of a solution. The conductivity of a solution depends on the number and type of ions present in the solution. As the hydrolysis of ethyl acetate proceeds, the concentration of ions in the solution changes, and so does the conductivity.

- The procedure for the experiment is as follows:

  - Prepare a 0.1 M solution of ethyl acetate and a 0.1 M solution of hydrochloric acid in separate beakers.
  - Set up a conductivity meter and a burette with a clamp and a stand.
  - Fill the burette with the ethyl acetate solution and adjust the initial volume to zero.
  - Pour 50 mL of the hydrochloric acid solution into a 100 mL conical flask and place it on a magnetic stirrer with a stirring bar.
  - Connect the conductivity meter to the conical flask with electrodes and turn on the meter.
  - Record the initial conductivity of the solution.
  - Start a stopwatch and add 1 mL of the ethyl acetate solution from the burette to the conical flask. Stir the solution well and record the conductivity and the time.
  - Repeat the above step until 25 mL of the ethyl acetate solution is added. Record the final conductivity and the time.
  - Plot a graph of conductivity versus time and find the slope of the graph.
  - Use the slope of the graph and the following equation to calculate the rate constant of the hydrolysis of ethyl acetate:

  `slope = -k[CH3-CO-OCH2-CH3]0[H2O]0[H+]0`

  where [CH3-CO-OCH2-CH3]0, [H2O]0, and [H+]0 are the initial concentrations of ethyl acetate, water, and hydrochloric acid, respectively.

- The rate constant of the hydrolysis of ethyl acetate can be used to compare the reactivity of different esters and to study the effect of temperature, catalyst, and solvent on the rate of hydrolysis.



# Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

    - **Nitrogen detection**: This element is present in many organic compounds, such as amines, amides, nitriles, etc. It can be detected by the Lassaigne's test, which involves heating the organic compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube. The nitrogen forms sodium cyanide, which can be extracted with water and tested with iron(II) sulfate and iron(III) chloride solutions. The formation of Prussian blue color indicates the presence of nitrogen.
    - **Halogen detection**: These elements are present in many organic compounds, such as alkyl halides, aryl halides, etc. They can be detected by the Beilstein test, which involves heating the organic compound with a copper wire in a flame. The halogens form volatile copper halides, which impart a characteristic green color to the flame.
    - **Sulfur detection**: This element is present in many organic compounds, such as thiols, sulfides, sulfones, etc. It can be detected by the sodium fusion test, which is similar to the Lassaigne's test for nitrogen. The sulfur forms sodium sulfide, which can be extracted with water and tested with lead acetate solution. The formation of a black precipitate of lead sulfide indicates the presence of sulfur.
    - **Phosphorus detection**: This element is present in some organic compounds, such as phosphines, phosphates, etc. It can be detected by the sodium fusion test, which is similar to the Lassaigne's test for nitrogen. The phosphorus forms sodium phosphide, which can be extracted with water and tested with silver nitrate solution. The formation of a yellow precipitate of silver phosphide indicates the presence of phosphorus.

  - **Functional group identification**: These methods are based on the characteristic reactions of certain functional groups with specific reagents. Some examples are:

    - **Alcoholic –OH group**: This functional group is present in alcohols, phenols, etc. It can be identified by the following tests:

      - **Lucas test**: This test involves adding a few drops of the organic compound to a solution of zinc chloride in concentrated hydrochloric acid. The formation of a turbid solution or a separate layer indicates the presence of an alcoholic –OH group. The rate of turbidity depends on the type of alcohol: tertiary alcohols react fastest, followed by secondary alcohols, and primary alcohols react very slowly or not at all.
      - **Esterification test**: This test involves heating the organic compound with an acid anhydride or acyl chloride in the presence of a few drops of concentrated sulfuric acid. The formation of a pleasant fruity smell indicates the presence of an alcoholic –OH group. The smell is due to the formation of an ester, which can be identified by its boiling point and solubility.
      - **Iodoform test**: This test involves adding a few drops of the organic compound to a solution of iodine and sodium hydroxide. The formation of a yellow precipitate of iodoform indicates the presence of an alcoholic –OH group. This test is specific for alcohols that have a methyl group attached to the –OH group, such as ethanol, propan-2-ol, etc.

# Course Outcomes and Bloom's Taxonomy

## Course Outcomes
- Course outcomes are statements that describe what students are expected to know and be able to do by the end of a course.
- Course outcomes are usually aligned with the program outcomes, which are broader statements that reflect the goals and objectives of the academic program or department.
- Course outcomes are important for designing, delivering, and assessing the course content and activities, as well as for communicating the expectations and standards to the students and other stakeholders.
- Course outcomes should be specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART), and should reflect the level and depth of learning that the course intends to achieve.
- Course outcomes should be written from the student's perspective, using active verbs that indicate the cognitive, affective, or psychomotor domain of learning.

## Bloom's Taxonomy
- Bloom's taxonomy is a framework for classifying the levels of cognitive learning, from the lowest to the highest, as follows:
  - Remember: The ability to recall or recognize facts, terms, concepts, or procedures.
  - Understand: The ability to explain, interpret, summarize, paraphrase, or compare information or ideas.
  - Apply: The ability to use or apply knowledge, skills, or principles to new or different situations or problems.
  - Analyze: The ability to break down information or ideas into components, relationships, or patterns, and to identify the underlying assumptions, causes, or effects.
  - Evaluate: The ability to judge, critique, or assess the value, quality, or validity of information, ideas, arguments, or solutions, based on criteria or standards.
  - Create: The ability to generate, produce, or synthesize new or original information, ideas, products, or solutions, by combining or reorganizing existing elements.
- Bloom's taxonomy can be used to design course outcomes that match the intended level of learning and to assess the students' achievement of the outcomes using appropriate methods and tools.
- Bloom's taxonomy can also be extended or modified to include other domains of learning, such as affective (attitudes, values, emotions) or psychomotor (physical skills, movements, actions).

# Level

- A level is a concept that measures the amount of something in a system or a process.
- A level can be quantitative or qualitative, depending on the type of measurement used.
- A level can be absolute or relative, depending on the reference point or the scale used.
- A level can be static or dynamic, depending on the rate of change or the variation over time.
- A level can be discrete or continuous, depending on the granularity or the smoothness of the measurement.

## Examples of levels

- The level of water in a tank is a quantitative, absolute, dynamic and continuous level. It can be measured in liters, gallons, or cubic meters, and it changes over time depending on the inflow and outflow of water.
- The level of difficulty of a game is a qualitative, relative, static and discrete level. It can be measured in terms of easy, medium, hard, or expert, and it does not change over time unless the player changes the settings.
- The level of happiness of a person is a qualitative, relative, dynamic and continuous level. It can be measured in terms of sad, neutral, happy, or ecstatic, and it changes over time depending on the person's mood and circumstances.

# CO-1 Get an understanding of the use of different analytical instruments. K3

- Analytical instruments are devices that measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples, materials, or systems.
- Different analytical instruments have different uses, such as:
  - Spectroscopy: the study of the interaction of matter and electromagnetic radiation. Spectroscopic instruments can measure the absorption, emission, reflection, or scattering of light by a sample, and provide information about its structure, composition, or concentration.
  - Chromatography: the separation of a mixture into its components based on their different affinities to a stationary phase and a mobile phase. Chromatographic instruments can separate and identify the components of a complex mixture, such as drugs, pollutants, or biomolecules.
  - Mass spectrometry: the determination of the mass-to-charge ratio of ions generated from a sample. Mass spectrometric instruments can identify the molecular formula, structure, or isotopic composition of a sample, or quantify the amount of a substance in a sample.
  - Electrochemistry: the study of the relationship between electricity and chemical reactions. Electrochemical instruments can measure the electrical potential, current, or resistance of a sample, and provide information about its redox state, pH, conductivity, or electrochemical activity.
  - Microscopy: the use of a microscope to magnify and observe objects that are too small to be seen by the naked eye. Microscopic instruments can reveal the morphology, structure, or function of cells, tissues, or materials at different scales and resolutions.

## Viscosity
- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to deformation under shear stress.
- Viscosity is also known as the internal friction of a fluid.
- Viscosity depends on the intermolecular forces, temperature, and pressure of the fluid.
- Viscosity is expressed in units of pascal-seconds (Pa·s) or poise (P).
- Viscosity can be measured by various methods, such as capillary viscometer, rotational viscometer, or falling ball viscometer.

- Conductance of solution depends on the concentration, type, and mobility of the ions present in the solution, as well as the temperature and pressure of the solution.
- Conductance of solution is expressed in units of siemens (S) or mho (℧).
- Conductance of solution can be measured by using a conductivity meter, which consists of a pair of electrodes connected to an alternating current source and a voltmeter.

# K3

- K3 is a type of surface in mathematics that is named after three mathematicians: Kummer, Kähler, and Kodaira.
- A K3 surface is a complex algebraic surface that has trivial canonical bundle and no curves of negative self-intersection.
- A K3 surface is also a Calabi-Yau manifold, which means that it has a Ricci-flat metric and a holomorphic volume form.
- K3 surfaces are important in various fields of mathematics and physics, such as algebraic geometry, string theory, and mirror symmetry.
- Some examples of K3 surfaces are:

  - The quartic surface in projective 3-space defined by a homogeneous polynomial of degree 4.
  - The double cover of the projective plane branched along a smooth sextic curve.
  - The Kummer surface, which is obtained by taking the quotient of a 2-torus by an involution and resolving the 16 singular points.
  - The Fermat quartic surface, which is defined by the equation x^4 + y^4 + z^4 + w^4 = 0 in projective 3-space.

# CO-3 Measure the hardness and alkalinity of the water. K3

- Hardness and alkalinity are two important parameters that indicate the quality of water for various purposes, such as drinking, industrial use, irrigation, etc.
- Hardness is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water, which can form insoluble deposits or scale on pipes, boilers, heaters, etc. Hardness can also affect the taste, lather formation, and detergent efficiency of water.
- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids, which is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium, sodium, and potassium. Alkalinity can also affect the pH, corrosion, and buffering capacity of water.
- The hardness and alkalinity of water can be measured by various methods, such as titration, colorimetry, spectrophotometry, etc. The most common method is titration, which involves adding a known amount of a standard solution of a chemical reagent to a sample of water until a color change or an endpoint is reached.
- The titration method for measuring hardness is based on the reaction of calcium and magnesium ions with ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA), which is a chelating agent that forms stable complexes with metal ions. The EDTA solution is added to the water sample until all the calcium and magnesium ions are complexed, and the endpoint is indicated by a color change of a metal indicator, such as eriochrome black T (EBT) or calmagite, from red to blue.
- The titration method for measuring alkalinity is based on the reaction of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides with a strong acid, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid. The acid solution is added to the water sample until all the alkaline substances are neutralized, and the endpoint is indicated by a color change of a pH indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange, from pink or orange to colorless or yellow.
- The hardness and alkalinity of water are usually expressed in terms of milligrams per liter (mg/L) or parts per million (ppm) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3) equivalent, which is the amount of CaCO3 that would produce the same effect as the actual substances present in water. The hardness and alkalinity of water can be classified into different categories, such as soft, moderately hard, hard, very hard, low, moderate, high, etc., depending on their values.



# CO-4 Know the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol

Phenol is an aromatic compound with a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a benzene ring. It is also known as carbolic acid or phenic acid. Phenol has antiseptic and disinfectant properties and is used in the manufacture of plastics, dyes, explosives, and drugs.

There are several methods to prepare phenol from different sources, such as haloarenes, benzene, cumene, diazonium salts, and coal tar. Some of the common methods are:

- **From haloarenes**: Haloarenes are aromatic compounds with a halogen atom (-X) attached to a benzene ring. Examples of haloarenes are chlorobenzene, bromobenzene, and iodobenzene. When haloarenes are fused with sodium hydroxide at high temperature and pressure, sodium phenoxide is produced. Sodium phenoxide is then acidified with dilute acid or carbon dioxide to give phenol. This method is also known as the Dow process.

  - Example: Chlorobenzene + NaOH → Sodium phenoxide + NaCl
  - Sodium phenoxide + HCl → Phenol + NaCl

- **From benzene**: Benzene is a simple aromatic compound with six carbon atoms and six hydrogen atoms. When benzene is treated with concentrated sulfuric acid, benzene sulfonic acid is formed. Benzene sulfonic acid is then fused with sodium hydroxide to produce sodium phenoxide, which is then acidified to give phenol. This method is also known as the Raschig process.

  - Example: Benzene + H2SO4 → Benzene sulfonic acid + H2O
  - Benzene sulfonic acid + NaOH → Sodium phenoxide + Na2SO4
  - Sodium phenoxide + HCl → Phenol + NaCl

- **From cumene**: Cumene is an organic compound with the formula C6H5CH(CH3)2. It is also known as isopropylbenzene. When cumene is oxidized by air in the presence of a catalyst, cumene hydroperoxide is formed. Cumene hydroperoxide is then cleaved by dilute acid to give phenol and acetone. This method is also known as the cumene process.

  - Example: Cumene + O2 → Cumene hydroperoxide
  - Cumene hydroperoxide + H2SO4 → Phenol + Acetone

- **From diazonium salts**: Diazonium salts are organic compounds with the general formula R-N2+X-, where R is an aryl group and X is a halide ion. They are formed by the reaction of aromatic amines with nitrous acid. When diazonium salts are treated with water, they undergo hydrolysis to give phenol and nitrogen gas. This method is also known as the Sandmeyer reaction.

  - Example: C6H5NH2 + HNO2 → C6H5N2+Cl- + H2O
  - C6H5N2+Cl- + H2O → Phenol + N2 + HCl

- **From coal tar**: Coal tar is a dark brown liquid obtained from the distillation of coal. It contains various aromatic compounds, including phenol. When coal tar is fractionally distilled, phenol is obtained from the middle oil fraction at a temperature range of 443-503 K. This method is also known as the coal tar process.

  - Example: Coal tar → Fractional distillation → Phenol + Other compounds

These are some of the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol. Phenol is an important industrial chemical and has many applications in various fields. Phenol can also undergo various reactions, such as electrophilic aromatic substitution, oxidation, reduction, and esterification. Phenol can also form polymers, such as bakelite and nylon. Phenol is a versatile and useful compound, but it is also toxic and corrosive, so it should be handled with care.



# Formaldehyde & Urea Formaldehyde Resin, Adipic Acid and Paracetamol

## Formaldehyde
- Formaldehyde is a colorless, flammable, strong-smelling chemical that is used in building materials and to produce many household products.
- Formaldehyde is also a naturally occurring substance in the environment. It is produced by the combustion of organic matter and by the metabolic processes of living organisms.
- Formaldehyde is classified as a human carcinogen by the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) and the US National Toxicology Program (NTP). Exposure to high levels of formaldehyde may cause irritation of the eyes, nose, and throat, and increase the risk of certain cancers, such as nasopharyngeal cancer and leukemia.

## Urea Formaldehyde Resin
- Urea formaldehyde resin (UF) is a nontransparent thermosetting resin or polymer that is produced from urea and formaldehyde.
- UF is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects. UF has advantages of low cost, high strength, water resistance, and fast curing.
- UF also has disadvantages of emitting formaldehyde during curing and service, which may cause health and environmental problems. UF may also show the appearance of crystal domains, which may affect its mechanical and thermal properties .
- UF can be modified by adding melamine, polyvinyl alcohol, or adipic acid dihydrazide to reduce the free formaldehyde content and improve its performance.

## Adipic Acid
- Adipic acid is a white crystalline organic acid that is mainly used as a precursor for the production of nylon.
- Adipic acid is also used as a food additive, a plasticizer, a lubricant, and a component of some polyurethane foams.
- Adipic acid is produced industrially by the oxidation of cyclohexane or cyclohexanol with nitric acid, or by the biological oxidation of glucose or fatty acids.
- Adipic acid is biodegradable and has a low toxicity, but it may cause skin irritation, eye irritation, and respiratory irritation if inhaled or contacted.

## Paracetamol
- Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is a widely used analgesic (pain reliever) and antipyretic (fever reducer) drug.
- Paracetamol is effective for mild to moderate pain, such as headache, toothache, muscle ache, menstrual cramps, and arthritis. It is also used to reduce fever and to treat cold and flu symptoms.
- Paracetamol is generally safe and well tolerated, but it may cause liver damage or failure if taken in large doses or with alcohol. Paracetamol overdose is a common cause of poisoning and death worldwide.
- Paracetamol is synthesized from phenol and acetic anhydride, or from p-aminophenol and acetic acid. Paracetamol is metabolized in the liver by various enzymes, and excreted in the urine as glucuronide or sulfate conjugates.
