# K3

- K3 is a type of visa that allows a foreign spouse of a U.S. citizen to enter and reside in the United States while waiting for the approval of their immigrant visa petition.
- K3 visa is a nonimmigrant visa, which means it does not grant permanent residence or a green card. However, K3 visa holders can apply for adjustment of status to become permanent residents after their immigrant visa petition is approved.
- K3 visa is valid for two years and can be renewed if the immigrant visa petition or the adjustment of status application is still pending.
- K3 visa holders can work and study in the United States with the appropriate authorization from the U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS).
- K3 visa holders can also travel outside the United States and re-enter with a valid visa and passport.
- K3 visa is not available for spouses of lawful permanent residents or for spouses of U.S. citizens who are already in the United States. In these cases, the spouses can apply for an immigrant visa or adjustment of status directly.
- K3 visa requires the U.S. citizen spouse to file two petitions: Form I-130, Petition for Alien Relative, and Form I-129F, Petition for Alien Fiancé(e). The I-130 petition establishes the relationship between the spouses, while the I-129F petition requests the K3 visa for the foreign spouse.
- K3 visa also requires the foreign spouse to undergo a medical examination, a background check, and an interview at a U.S. consulate or embassy abroad. The foreign spouse must also provide evidence of their marriage, their identity, their financial support, and their intention to immigrate to the United States.



# CO-5 Estimate the rate constant of reaction. K3

- The rate constant of a reaction, denoted by k, is a measure of how fast the reaction proceeds at a given temperature and concentration of reactants and products.
- The rate constant can be calculated from the rate law of the reaction, which relates the rate of the reaction to the concentrations of the reactants raised to some powers.
- For a termolecular reaction, where three molecules collide and react, the rate law is usually of the form: r = k3[A][B][C], where r is the rate of the reaction, k3 is the termolecular rate constant, and [A], [B], and [C] are the concentrations of the reactants.
- To estimate the termolecular rate constant, k3, one needs to measure the rate of the reaction and the concentrations of the reactants under the same conditions, and then use the rate law equation to solve for k3.
- Alternatively, if the termolecular reaction is the sum of two or more elementary reactions, one can use the relationship between the equilibrium constants of the individual reactions and the overall reaction to estimate k3 .
- For example, if the termolecular reaction is: CO(g) + 3H2(g) ⇌ CH4(g) + H2O(g), and it is the sum of two elementary reactions: CO(g) + H2(g) ⇌ COH(g) and COH(g) + 2H2(g) ⇌ CH4(g) + H2O(g), then the equilibrium constant for the overall reaction, K3, is equal to the product of the equilibrium constants for the individual reactions, K1 and K2 .
- K3 = K1K2 = (k1/k-1)(k2/k-2) = (k1k2)/(k-1k-2), where k1, k2, k-1, and k-2 are the rate constants for the forward and reverse reactions of the elementary steps.
- If the rate constants for the elementary reactions are known, then k3 can be estimated from the equation: k3 = K3(k-1k-2)/(k1k2).

# Nuclear Fusion

Nuclear fusion is the process of combining two or more atomic nuclei to form a heavier nucleus, releasing energy in the process. Nuclear fusion is the source of energy for the Sun and other stars.

Some of the main points to know about nuclear fusion are:

- Nuclear fusion requires high temperatures and pressures to overcome the electrostatic repulsion between positively charged nuclei. The conditions for fusion are achieved in the cores of stars, where the temperature is about 15 million degrees Celsius and the pressure is about 250 billion atmospheres.
- The most common fusion reaction in stars is the proton-proton chain, which converts four hydrogen nuclei (protons) into one helium nucleus (alpha particle), releasing two positrons, two neutrinos, and gamma rays. This reaction converts about 0.7% of the mass of the hydrogen into energy, according to Einstein's mass-energy equivalence formula E = mc^2.
- Another fusion reaction that occurs in stars is the carbon-nitrogen-oxygen (CNO) cycle, which uses carbon, nitrogen, and oxygen as catalysts to fuse hydrogen into helium. This reaction is more efficient than the proton-proton chain at higher temperatures and densities, and dominates in stars more massive than the Sun.
- The energy released by fusion reactions in stars is transported to the surface by radiation and convection, creating the luminosity and spectrum of the star. The fusion reactions also produce heavier elements, such as carbon, oxygen, and iron, through a series of nuclear reactions called nucleosynthesis. The heaviest elements, such as gold and uranium, are produced in supernova explosions, when massive stars collapse and explode at the end of their lives.
- Nuclear fusion can also be artificially induced on Earth, using devices such as tokamaks, stellarators, inertial confinement lasers, and Z-pinch machines. These devices aim to create and confine a plasma, a state of matter where the atoms are ionized and the nuclei and electrons are free to move. The plasma must be heated to temperatures of hundreds of millions of degrees Celsius and compressed to high densities to achieve fusion.
- The main challenge of achieving controlled fusion on Earth is to maintain the plasma in a stable and efficient state, without losing too much energy to the surroundings. The plasma must also be confined for a sufficient time to allow enough fusion reactions to occur. The ratio of the fusion power output to the input power is called the Q factor, and a Q factor greater than one means that the fusion device produces more energy than it consumes.
- The most promising fusion fuel for terrestrial applications is a mixture of deuterium and tritium, two isotopes of hydrogen. Deuterium is abundant in seawater, and tritium can be produced from lithium, which is also widely available. The deuterium-tritium fusion reaction produces one helium nucleus, one neutron, and 17.6 MeV of energy. The neutron carries most of the energy, and can be used to heat water and generate electricity, or to breed more tritium from lithium.
- Nuclear fusion has many potential advantages over other energy sources, such as fossil fuels, nuclear fission, and renewables. Fusion does not produce greenhouse gases or long-lived radioactive waste, and has virtually unlimited fuel supply. Fusion is also inherently safe, as any malfunction or disruption would stop the reaction. Fusion could provide clean, abundant, and reliable energy for the future. However, fusion also faces many technical, economic, and social challenges, such as achieving high Q factors, reducing costs, and gaining public acceptance.

# Page 32 of 40

The topic of this page is **the structure and function of the human eye**.

The human eye is a complex organ that allows us to see the world around us. It consists of several parts, each with a specific role in vision.

## The main parts of the eye are:

- The **cornea**: a transparent layer that covers the front of the eye and refracts (bends) light rays entering the eye.
- The **iris**: a circular muscle that controls the size of the pupil, the opening in the center of the eye that regulates the amount of light entering the eye.
- The **lens**: a flexible structure behind the pupil that changes shape to focus light rays onto the retina, the light-sensitive layer at the back of the eye.
- The **retina**: a thin layer of cells that converts light into electrical signals that are sent to the brain via the optic nerve.
- The **optic nerve**: a bundle of nerve fibers that carries visual information from the retina to the brain.
- The **aqueous humor**: a clear fluid that fills the space between the cornea and the lens, providing nourishment and pressure to the eye.
- The **vitreous humor**: a jelly-like substance that fills the space between the lens and the retina, helping to maintain the shape of the eye and transmit light to the retina.

## The function of the eye is to:

- Capture light rays from the environment and focus them onto the retina.
- Convert light into electrical signals that can be processed by the brain.
- Adjust to different light conditions by changing the size of the pupil and the shape of the lens.
- Enable us to see colors, shapes, movements, and distances of objects.

# Engineering Mathematics Lab

Engineering mathematics is the application of mathematical methods and techniques to solve engineering problems. Engineering mathematics lab is a practical course that complements the theoretical aspects of engineering mathematics and helps students to develop mathematical skills and confidence.

The syllabus of engineering mathematics lab may vary depending on the institution and the branch of engineering. However, some common topics that are covered in engineering mathematics lab are:

- Numerical methods: Solving equations, interpolation, differentiation, integration, and ordinary differential equations using various numerical techniques and algorithms. Implementing and testing the numerical methods using software tools such as MATLAB, Python, or C++.
- Linear algebra: Performing operations on matrices and vectors, finding eigenvalues and eigenvectors, solving systems of linear equations, and applying linear algebra concepts to engineering problems such as linear transformations, least squares, and linear programming. Using software tools such as MATLAB, Python, or C++ to manipulate and visualize matrices and vectors.
- Probability and statistics: Calculating probabilities, counting techniques, discrete and continuous random variables, expectation, variance, and distributions. Performing statistical analysis, hypothesis testing, confidence intervals, and regression on data sets. Using software tools such as MATLAB, Python, or R to generate and analyze random data and perform statistical computations.
- Complex analysis: Working with complex numbers, functions, and variables. Finding roots, powers, and logarithms of complex numbers. Analyzing and plotting complex functions using software tools such as MATLAB, Python, or Wolfram Alpha. Applying complex analysis concepts to engineering problems such as Fourier series, Laplace transforms, and contour integration.

The objectives of engineering mathematics lab are:

- To enhance the understanding of engineering mathematics concepts and methods by applying them to practical problems and situations.
- To develop the computational and programming skills of students by using software tools to implement and test mathematical algorithms and models.
- To improve the analytical and critical thinking skills of students by interpreting and evaluating the results of mathematical computations and simulations.
- To foster the creativity and curiosity of students by exploring and experimenting with various mathematical topics and applications.



## Course Objectives:

- To introduce the basic concepts and principles of artificial intelligence (AI) and its applications.
- To develop the skills and techniques for designing, implementing, and evaluating AI systems and agents.
- To explore the ethical, social, and legal implications of AI and its impact on human society.
- To provide a foundation for further study and research in AI and related fields.



### 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles, applications, or techniques, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments can be used for various purposes, such as quality control, research and development, environmental monitoring, forensic analysis, clinical diagnosis, etc.
- Analytical instruments can provide qualitative or quantitative information about the samples, such as their identity, composition, structure, concentration, purity, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different components, such as sources, detectors, transducers, signal processors, data analyzers, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different characteristics, such as sensitivity, selectivity, accuracy, precision, resolution, range, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different advantages and limitations, such as speed, cost, reliability, complexity, etc.

### 2. To enable the students to understand about the analysis of chloride content, hardness, alkalinity of water.

- Chloride content is the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in water. Chloride ions are derived from natural sources such as rocks, soil, seawater, and volcanic emissions, as well as from human activities such as agriculture, industry, and sewage disposal. Chloride content is an important indicator of water quality, as high levels of chloride can affect the taste, odor, and corrosion of water and pipes. Chloride content can be measured by titration with silver nitrate (AgNO3) using potassium chromate (K2CrO4) as an indicator.

- Hardness is the measure of the concentration of calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) ions in water. These ions are responsible for forming scale and deposits in pipes, boilers, and appliances, as well as interfering with the action of soap and detergents. Hardness can be classified as temporary or permanent, depending on whether it can be removed by boiling or not. Temporary hardness is caused by the presence of bicarbonates (HCO3-) of calcium and magnesium, which decompose into carbonates (CO3 2-) and carbon dioxide (CO2) upon heating. Permanent hardness is caused by the presence of sulfates (SO4 2-), chlorides, and nitrates of calcium and magnesium, which do not decompose upon heating. Hardness can be measured by titration with ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) using eriochrome black T (EBT) as an indicator.

- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. Alkalinity is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium, sodium, and potassium in water. Alkalinity is important for maintaining the pH and buffering capacity of water, as well as preventing corrosion of pipes and equipment. Alkalinity can be measured by titration with a standard acid such as sulfuric acid (H2SO4) or hydrochloric acid (HCl) using phenolphthalein and methyl orange as indicators.

### 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of a liquid.

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a liquid is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in the liquid. The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, where 0 is the most acidic, 7 is neutral, and 14 is the most basic. The pH of a liquid can be measured using indicators, such as litmus paper, or pH meters, which are electronic devices that measure the voltage difference between two electrodes immersed in the liquid.
- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is the force per unit length that acts along the surface of the liquid. Surface tension causes the liquid to minimize its surface area and form spherical droplets. The surface tension of a liquid can be measured using methods such as the drop weight method, the ring method, or the pendant drop method, which involve measuring the force or shape of a drop of liquid hanging from a needle or a ring.
- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a liquid is to flow. It is the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate in the liquid. The shear stress is the force per unit area that acts parallel to the surface of the liquid, and the shear rate is the rate of change of the velocity of the liquid along the direction of the shear stress. The viscosity of a liquid can be measured using methods such as the capillary tube method, the falling ball method, or the rotational viscometer method, which involve measuring the flow rate or the torque of a liquid through a tube or a cylinder.



### 4. To enable the students to understand about the preparation of different resins.

Resins are organic compounds that are solid or semi-solid at room temperature and have a high molecular weight. They are usually derived from natural sources, such as plants or animals, or from synthetic sources, such as petroleum or coal. Resins have various applications in industries, such as adhesives, coatings, plastics, paints, varnishes, and rubber.

The preparation of different resins involves the following steps:

- **Selection of raw materials**: The raw materials for resin production depend on the type and properties of the desired resin. For example, natural resins, such as rosin, shellac, and amber, are obtained from plant or animal sources, while synthetic resins, such as phenol-formaldehyde, urea-formaldehyde, and epoxy, are synthesized from chemical reactants.
- **Purification of raw materials**: The raw materials are purified to remove impurities, such as water, dust, dirt, and other substances, that may affect the quality and performance of the resin. Purification methods include filtration, distillation, extraction, crystallization, and precipitation.
- **Polymerization of raw materials**: The raw materials are polymerized to form long chains of molecules that give the resin its characteristic properties, such as hardness, elasticity, solubility, and melting point. Polymerization methods include condensation, addition, and cross-linking reactions, which may be catalyzed by heat, light, or chemicals.
- **Modification of resin properties**: The resin properties may be modified to suit the specific application or requirement by adding additives, such as fillers, plasticizers, stabilizers, pigments, and curing agents. These additives may enhance or alter the resin's strength, flexibility, durability, color, and resistance to heat, moisture, and chemicals.
- **Shaping and curing of resin products**: The resin products are shaped and cured to form the final product, such as a film, sheet, rod, tube, or molded object. Shaping methods include extrusion, injection molding, compression molding, and casting, while curing methods include heating, cooling, or chemical reaction.



### 5. To enable the students to understand about the synthesis of organic compounds such as adipic acid and paracetamol by conventional and green route.

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid that is used as a precursor for the production of nylon-6,6, a synthetic polymer widely used in various applications.
- Paracetamol is an analgesic and antipyretic drug that is commonly used to relieve pain and fever.
- Conventional route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexane or cyclohexanol with nitric acid, which produces large amounts of nitrous oxide, a greenhouse gas that contributes to global warming and ozone depletion.
- Conventional route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the acetylation of phenol with acetic anhydride, which requires harsh conditions and produces toxic by-products such as phenyl acetate and acetic acid.
- Green route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexene with hydrogen peroxide in a microemulsion, which uses sodium tungstate as a catalyst and alkyldimethylbenzylammonium chloride as a surfactant. This process is environmentally friendly as it produces pure adipic acid in high yield and allows the recycling of the catalyst and the surfactant .
- Green route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the design and discovery of paracetamol analogues using computational methods, such as molecular docking, pharmacophore modeling, and molecular dynamics simulations. This approach reduces the need for animal testing and synthetic chemistry, and enables the identification of novel and potent analgesic and antipyretic agents.



## LIST OF EXPERIMENTS

- An experiment is a procedure that tests a hypothesis or a question by collecting and analyzing data under controlled conditions.
- Experiments can be classified into different types based on their purpose, design, and outcome.
- Some of the common types of experiments are:

  - **Observational experiment**: An experiment that involves observing and measuring the behavior or characteristics of a subject or a phenomenon without manipulating any variables. For example, observing the movement of planets in the solar system, or measuring the blood pressure of patients in a hospital.
  - **Natural experiment**: An experiment that exploits a natural occurrence or a historical event as a quasi-experimental treatment. For example, comparing the economic outcomes of countries that were colonized by different European powers, or studying the health effects of a volcanic eruption on nearby residents.
  - **Randomized controlled experiment**: An experiment that randomly assigns subjects to either a treatment group or a control group, and then compares the outcomes of the two groups. For example, testing the effectiveness of a new drug or a new teaching method by giving it to some subjects and not to others, and then measuring the difference in their performance or health.
  - **Factorial experiment**: An experiment that manipulates two or more independent variables (factors) and measures their effects on one or more dependent variables (outcomes). For example, testing the effects of different combinations of temperature and humidity on plant growth, or the effects of different levels of noise and lighting on worker productivity.
  - **Before-after experiment**: An experiment that measures the outcome of a subject or a group before and after applying a treatment or an intervention. For example, measuring the weight of a person before and after a diet program, or the academic achievement of a class before and after a curriculum change.
  - **Cross-over experiment**: An experiment that applies two or more treatments to the same subject or group in a different order, and then compares the outcomes of each treatment. For example, measuring the blood sugar levels of diabetic patients after giving them different types of insulin, or the preferences of consumers after showing them different types of advertisements.



Hello, I am Sydney, your AI assistant. I can help you with your query. Here is some information on the topic of calibration of analytical equipment and apparatus.

### 1. Calibration of Analytical Equipment and apparatus

- Calibration is the process of evaluating and adjusting the precision and accuracy of measurement equipment.
- Calibration ensures that the equipment and apparatus produce valid and reliable results that are consistent with the standards and specifications .
- Calibration involves comparing the output of the equipment and apparatus with a known reference value, such as a certified standard or a traceable measurement .
- Calibration can be done internally or externally, depending on the type and complexity of the equipment and apparatus, the availability of reference standards, and the quality requirements .
- Calibration should be done at regular intervals, based on the frequency of use, the environmental conditions, the stability of the equipment and apparatus, and the manufacturer's recommendations  .
- Calibration should be documented and recorded, with the date, the reference value, the measured value, the uncertainty, the correction factor, and the signature of the person who performed the calibration .
- Calibration should be verified and validated, by checking the performance of the equipment and apparatus against a known sample, a control chart, or a proficiency test .
- Calibration should be followed by maintenance and repair, if needed, to ensure the proper functioning and accuracy of the equipment and apparatus .



### 2. Determination of Hardness of water sample by EDTA method

- Hardness of water is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water.
- EDTA (Ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) is a chelating agent that can form complexes with metal ions such as Ca2+ and Mg2+.
- EDTA titration is a method of determining the hardness of water by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of EDTA in the presence of a suitable indicator.
- The indicator used is Eriochrome Black T (EBT), which is a dye that changes color from wine red to blue when it binds to metal ions.
- The procedure for EDTA titration is as follows   :
  - Take a sample volume of 20 ml (V ml) of water to be tested.
  - Dilute the sample to 40 ml by adding 20 ml of distilled water in an Erlenmeyer flask.
  - Add 1 ml of ammonia buffer to adjust the pH to 10 ± 0.1. This is to prevent the precipitation of metal hydroxides and to ensure the formation of stable EDTA-metal complexes.
  - Add 1 or 2 drops of EBT indicator to the sample. The sample will turn wine red due to the presence of Ca2+ and Mg2+ ions.
  - Titrate the sample with a standard solution of EDTA of known concentration (C mg/ml) from a burette with vigorous shaking until the color changes from wine red to blue. This indicates the end point of the titration.
  - Record the volume of EDTA solution used (B ml).
- The calculation of hardness of water by EDTA titration is based on the following equation  :
  - C * B = V * H
  - Where C is the concentration of EDTA solution in mg/ml, B is the volume of EDTA solution used in ml, V is the volume of water sample taken in ml, and H is the hardness of water in mg/ml as CaCO3 (calcium carbonate).
  - The hardness of water can be expressed in different units, such as ppm (parts per million), mg/L (milligrams per liter), or dH (degrees of hardness). One ppm or mg/L is equivalent to one mg of CaCO3 per liter of water. One dH is equivalent to 10 mg of CaCO3 per liter of water.
  - The hardness of water can be classified as soft, moderately hard, hard, or very hard based on the following ranges:
    - Soft: 0 - 75 mg/L as CaCO3
    - Moderately hard: 75 - 150 mg/L as CaCO3
    - Hard: 150 - 300 mg/L as CaCO3
    - Very hard: > 300 mg/L as CaCO3
- The advantages of EDTA titration method are that it is simple, accurate, and applicable to any type of water sample. The disadvantages are that it requires a pH adjustment, a suitable indicator, and a standard EDTA solution.



### 3. Determination of Alkalinity of water sample.

- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. It is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium, sodium, and potassium in water.
- Alkalinity is important for water quality because it affects the pH, corrosion, and disinfection processes. High alkalinity can cause scaling and reduce the effectiveness of chlorine. Low alkalinity can cause corrosion and pH fluctuations.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid, using a suitable indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange, to detect the endpoint of the titration.
- The procedure for determining the alkalinity of water sample is as follows:

  - Take 100 mL of water sample in a conical flask and add 2-3 drops of phenolphthalein indicator. The indicator will turn pink if the water sample has any phenolphthalein alkalinity (due to the presence of hydroxides or carbonates).
  - Titrate the water sample with 0.02 N sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid from a burette until the pink color disappears. Note down the volume of acid used as V1. This is the phenolphthalein alkalinity of the water sample.
  - Add 2-3 drops of methyl orange indicator to the same water sample. The indicator will turn orange or yellow if the water sample has any methyl orange alkalinity (due to the presence of bicarbonates).
  - Titrate the water sample with 0.02 N sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid from the same burette until the color changes to pink. Note down the volume of acid used as V2. This is the total alkalinity of the water sample.
  - The phenolphthalein alkalinity (P) and the methyl orange alkalinity (M) can be calculated as follows:

    - P = V1 x N x 50
    - M = V2 x N x 50

    where V1 and V2 are the volumes of acid used in mL, N is the normality of the acid, and 50 is the conversion factor for 100 mL of water sample.
  - The types and concentrations of alkalinity present in the water sample can be determined by comparing the values of P and M as follows:

    - If P = 0 and M > 0, then only bicarbonates are present. The concentration of bicarbonates (HCO3-) can be calculated as M mg/L.
    - If P = M > 0, then only carbonates are present. The concentration of carbonates (CO3 2-) can be calculated as M/2 mg/L.
    - If P < M > 0, then both carbonates and bicarbonates are present. The concentration of carbonates (CO3 2-) can be calculated as P/2 mg/L, and the concentration of bicarbonates (HCO3-) can be calculated as (M-P) mg/L.
    - If P > M > 0, then hydroxides, carbonates, and bicarbonates are present. The concentration of hydroxides (OH-) can be calculated as (P-M) mg/L, the concentration of carbonates (CO3 2-) can be calculated as M/2 mg/L, and the concentration of bicarbonates (HCO3-) can be calculated as 0 mg/L.



### 4. Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a solution of unknown concentration (analyte)  .
- pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H3O+]  .
- Determination of pH by titrimetric method involves using a pH meter to monitor the change in pH of the analyte solution as the titrant is added   .
- A pH meter consists of a glass electrode that responds to the hydrogen ion activity in the solution, and a reference electrode that provides a constant potential. The difference in potential between the two electrodes is proportional to the pH of the solution  .
- A plot of the pH of the analyte solution versus the volume of the titrant added is called a pH curve or a titration curve  . The shape of the curve depends on the nature and strength of the acid and base involved in the titration .
- The point at which the titration is complete, where the moles of titrant are equal to the moles of analyte, is called the equivalence point or the stoichiometric point  . The pH at the equivalence point depends on the type of acid-base reaction .
- The point at which the indicator changes color, signaling the end of the titration, is called the endpoint. The endpoint should be as close as possible to the equivalence point for an accurate titration  .
- The choice of the indicator depends on the pH range of the color change and the pH of the equivalence point. The indicator should have a sharp color change over a narrow pH range that includes the equivalence point  .
- Some examples of titrimetric methods for pH determination are:
  - Acid-base titration: A titration between an acid and a base, where the pH changes rapidly near the equivalence point  .
  - Redox titration: A titration between a reducing agent and an oxidizing agent, where the pH changes due to the formation or consumption of hydrogen ions in the redox reaction .
  - Complexometric titration: A titration between a metal ion and a complexing agent, where the pH changes due to the formation or dissociation of the complex .
  - Precipitation titration: A titration between two ions that form a sparingly soluble precipitate, where the pH changes due to the common ion effect or the hydrolysis of the precipitate .

# 5. Determination of surface tension of given liquid

## Introduction

- Surface tension is a property of liquids that causes them to behave as if they have a thin elastic skin on their surface.
- Surface tension is caused by the cohesive forces between the molecules of the liquid, which tend to minimize the surface area of the liquid.
- Surface tension is measured in units of force per unit length, such as N/m or dyn/cm.
- Surface tension depends on the nature of the liquid, the temperature, and the presence of any impurities or surfactants on the surface.

## Aim

- To determine the surface tension of a given liquid by the capillary rise method.

## Theory

- The capillary rise method is based on the principle that when a liquid rises in a narrow tube (capillary), the weight of the liquid column is balanced by the upward force due to surface tension.
- The upward force due to surface tension is proportional to the circumference of the capillary and the surface tension of the liquid.
- The weight of the liquid column is proportional to the cross-sectional area of the capillary and the density of the liquid.
- Therefore, the height of the liquid column (h) is given by the formula:

  h = (4T cos θ) / (ρgr)

  where T is the surface tension of the liquid, θ is the contact angle between the liquid and the capillary wall, ρ is the density of the liquid, g is the acceleration due to gravity, and r is the radius of the capillary.

- If the liquid wets the capillary wall, the contact angle is zero and the formula simplifies to:

  h = (4T) / (ρgr)

- By measuring the height of the liquid column and the radius of the capillary, the surface tension of the liquid can be calculated.

## Apparatus

- A capillary tube of uniform bore and known radius
- A stand and clamp to hold the capillary tube vertically
- A beaker or a trough to contain the liquid
- A measuring scale or a vernier caliper to measure the height of the liquid column
- A travelling microscope or a magnifying glass to measure the diameter of the capillary
- A thermometer to measure the temperature of the liquid
- A liquid of known density, such as water, alcohol, or glycerin

## Procedure

- Clean the capillary tube and dry it with a filter paper or a cloth.
- Fix the capillary tube vertically on the stand and clamp it such that its lower end is slightly above the level of the liquid in the beaker or the trough.
- Adjust the height of the liquid in the beaker or the trough such that the liquid just touches the lower end of the capillary tube.
- Observe the rise of the liquid in the capillary tube and measure the height of the liquid column (h) with the help of the measuring scale or the vernier caliper.
- Repeat the measurement for at least three different capillaries of different radii and record the values of h and r.
- Measure the diameter of the capillaries with the help of the travelling microscope or the magnifying glass and calculate the radius of each capillary.
- Measure the temperature of the liquid with the help of the thermometer and note down the value.
- Find the density of the liquid from a standard table or calculate it using a hydrometer.

## Observations

- Temperature of the liquid = _______ °C
- Density of the liquid = _______ kg/m3

| S.No. | Diameter of capillary (d) in m | Radius of capillary (r) in m | Height of liquid column (h) in m |
| ----- | ------------------------------ | ---------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| 1     |                                |                              |                                  |
| 2     |                                |                              |                                  |
| 3     |                                |                              |                                  |

## Calculations

- Using the formula h = (4T) / (ρgr), calculate the surface tension of the liquid for each capillary and take the average value as the final result.

  T1 = (ρgr1h1) / 4 = _______ N/m

  T2 = (ρgr2h2) / 4 = _______ N/m

  T3 = (ρgr3h3) / 4 = _______ N/m

  T = (T1 + T2 + T3) / 3 = _______ N/m

## Result

-

```

# 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer

## Introduction

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied shear stress.
- Viscosity is important for many applications, such as lubrication, fluid dynamics, transport phenomena, and rheology.
- Viscosity can be measured by various methods, such as capillary viscometer, rotational viscometer, falling ball viscometer, etc.
- In this experiment, we will use a capillary viscometer, also known as an Ostwald viscometer, to determine the viscosity of a given liquid.

## Theory

- A capillary viscometer consists of a U-shaped glass tube with two bulbs and a narrow capillary section between them.
- The liquid to be tested is filled in the lower bulb and the upper bulb is connected to a constant pressure source, such as a water reservoir.
- The liquid flows through the capillary section due to the pressure difference and the time taken for a fixed volume of liquid to flow from one mark to another mark on the tube is measured.
- The flow rate of the liquid is inversely proportional to its viscosity, according to the Hagen-Poiseuille equation:

  $$Q = \frac{\pi r^4 \Delta P}{8 \eta L}$$

  where Q is the volumetric flow rate, r is the radius of the capillary, $\Delta P$ is the pressure difference, $\eta$ is the viscosity, and L is the length of the capillary.

- The viscosity of the liquid can be calculated by comparing the flow rate of the liquid with the flow rate of a reference fluid, such as water, whose viscosity is known at a given temperature.
- The relative viscosity of the liquid is defined as the ratio of the flow time of the liquid to the flow time of the reference fluid:

  $$\eta_r = \frac{t}{t_0}$$

  where $\eta_r$ is the relative viscosity, t is the flow time of the liquid, and $t_0$ is the flow time of the reference fluid.

- The viscosity of the liquid can be obtained by multiplying the relative viscosity with the viscosity of the reference fluid:

  $$\eta = \eta_r \eta_0$$

  where $\eta$ is the viscosity of the liquid, and $\eta_0$ is the viscosity of the reference fluid.

## Procedure

- Clean the capillary viscometer with distilled water and dry it with air.
- Fill the lower bulb of the viscometer with the liquid to be tested and immerse the viscometer in a water bath maintained at a constant temperature.
- Connect the upper bulb of the viscometer to a water reservoir and adjust the pressure difference by raising or lowering the reservoir.
- Allow the liquid to flow through the capillary section and record the time taken for the liquid to flow from the upper mark to the lower mark on the tube. Repeat the measurement at least three times and take the average value as the flow time of the liquid.
- Repeat the same steps with the reference fluid, such as water, and record the flow time of the reference fluid.
- Calculate the relative viscosity and the viscosity of the liquid using the formulas given in the theory section.

## Precautions

- Avoid air bubbles in the liquid and the capillary section, as they may affect the flow rate and the accuracy of the measurement.
- Ensure that the temperature of the water bath is constant and uniform, as the viscosity of the liquid may vary with temperature.
- Ensure that the pressure difference between the upper and lower bulbs is constant and sufficient, as the flow rate of the liquid may depend on the pressure difference.
- Ensure that the capillary viscometer is clean and free of any impurities or residues, as they may alter the flow rate and the viscosity of the liquid.
```

# 7. Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator

## Aim
To determine the strength of a given solution of ferrous ammonium sulfate using potassium permanganate as an external indicator.

## Principle
Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a double salt of ferrous sulfate and ammonium sulfate. It has the formula FeSO4.(NH4)2SO4.6H2O. It is used as a primary standard in redox titrations because it is stable and easy to prepare. It can be oxidized by potassium permanganate, a strong oxidizing agent, in acidic medium. The reaction is as follows:

FeSO4.(NH4)2SO4.6H2O + KMnO4 + H2SO4 -> Fe2(SO4)3 + K2SO4 + (NH4)2SO4 + MnSO4 + H2O

The end point of the titration is marked by the appearance of a faint pink color due to the excess of potassium permanganate. The strength of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be calculated by using the following formula:

Strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate = (Normality of KMnO4 x Volume of KMnO4 x 392) / (Volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate x 1000)

Where 392 is the equivalent weight of ferrous ammonium sulfate.

## Apparatus and reagents
- Burette
- Pipette
- Conical flask
- Funnel
- Wash bottle
- Beaker
- Glass rod
- Weighing balance
- Ferrous ammonium sulfate solution
- Potassium permanganate solution
- Dilute sulfuric acid

## Procedure
- Rinse the burette with distilled water and then with a small amount of potassium permanganate solution. Fill the burette with potassium permanganate solution and note the initial reading.
- Rinse the pipette with distilled water and then with a small amount of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution. Pipette out 10 ml of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution into a conical flask. Add about 20 ml of distilled water and 5 ml of dilute sulfuric acid to the flask.
- Place the flask on a white tile and titrate it with potassium permanganate solution from the burette. Swirl the flask gently after each addition of potassium permanganate. Stop the titration when a faint pink color persists for more than 30 seconds. Note the final reading of the burette.
- Repeat the titration for two more times and take the average of the readings.

## Observations and calculations
| Trial | Initial reading of burette (ml) | Final reading of burette (ml) | Volume of KMnO4 used (ml) |
|-------|---------------------------------|-------------------------------|---------------------------|
| 1     |                                 |                               |                           |
| 2     |                                 |                               |                           |
| 3     |                                 |                               |                           |
| Mean  |                                 |                               |                           |

Normality of KMnO4 = N

Volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate = 10 ml

Strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate = (N x V x 392) / (10 x 1000)

Where V is the mean volume of KMnO4 used.

## Result
The strength of the given solution of ferrous ammonium sulfate is _______ g/l.



### 8. Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator.

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a reducing agent such as ferrous ammonium sulphate.
- The reaction between potassium dichromate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is as follows:

`K2Cr2O7 + 6FeSO4 + 7H2SO4 -> Cr2(SO4)3 + 3K2SO4 + 6FeSO4 + 7H2O`

- The end point of this titration can be detected by the change in colour of the solution from green to reddish-brown, due to the formation of ferric ions.
- This colour change is the internal indicator of the titration, and no external indicator is required.
- The strength of potassium dichromate can be calculated by using the following formula:

`Strength of K2Cr2O7 = (Volume of FeSO4 * Normality of FeSO4 * Equivalent weight of K2Cr2O7) / Volume of K2Cr2O7`

- The equivalent weight of potassium dichromate is 49.03 g/mol, and the normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate is usually 0.1 N.
- The procedure for the titration is as follows:

  - Pipette out 10 mL of ferrous ammonium sulphate solution into a conical flask and add 10 mL of dilute sulphuric acid.
  - Fill a burette with potassium dichromate solution and note the initial reading.
  - Titrate the ferrous ammonium sulphate solution with potassium dichromate solution until the colour changes from green to reddish-brown.
  - Note the final reading of the burette and calculate the volume of potassium dichromate used.
  - Repeat the titration for two more times and take the average volume of potassium dichromate used.
  - Calculate the strength of potassium dichromate using the formula given above.



### 9. Determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder

Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium hypochlorite [Ca(OCl)<sub>2</sub>] and calcium chloride [CaCl<sub>2</sub>]. It is used as a disinfectant and a bleaching agent. The available chlorine in bleaching powder is the amount of chlorine that can be liberated when the compound is treated with dilute acids. The available chlorine is expressed as a percentage of the mass of bleaching powder.

The principle of the determination is based on the following reactions:

Ca(OCl)Cl + H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> → CaSO<sub>4</sub> + H<sub>2</sub>O + Cl<sub>2</sub>

Cl<sub>2</sub> + 2KI → 2KCl + I<sub>2</sub>

I<sub>2</sub> + 2Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub> → Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>4</sub>O<sub>6</sub> + 2NaI

The bleaching powder is dissolved in water and treated with excess of potassium iodide (KI) and acetic acid (CH<sub>3</sub>COOH). The chlorine gas liberated reacts with KI to form iodine (I<sub>2</sub>), which is then titrated with sodium thiosulfate (Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub>) solution using starch as an indicator. The end point is the disappearance of the blue color of the starch-iodine complex. The amount of Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub> used is proportional to the amount of Cl<sub>2</sub> liberated, and hence to the available chlorine in the bleaching powder.

The procedure for the determination is as follows:

- Weigh accurately about 0.5 g of bleaching powder and transfer it to a 250 mL conical flask.
- Add about 100 mL of distilled water and shake well to dissolve the bleaching powder.
- Add 10 mL of 10% KI solution and 10 mL of 20% CH<sub>3</sub>COOH solution and mix well.
- Titrate the liberated iodine with 0.1 N Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub> solution using a burette until the yellow color of iodine is almost gone.
- Add a few drops of starch solution and continue the titration until the blue color disappears.
- Record the volume of Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub> used as V<sub>1</sub> mL.
- Perform a blank titration by repeating the same procedure without adding the bleaching powder. Record the volume of Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub> used as V<sub>2</sub> mL.
- Calculate the percentage of available chlorine in the bleaching powder using the formula:

% available chlorine = (V<sub>2</sub> - V<sub>1</sub>) x N x 35.5 x 100 / W

where N is the normality of Na<sub>2</sub>S<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub> solution, W is the weight of bleaching powder in g, and 35.5 is the equivalent weight of chlorine.

# Determination of chloride content in water sample

## Introduction

Chloride is one of the major anions found in natural waters. It originates from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, road salt, and domestic sewage. Chloride can affect the taste, corrosion, and biological activity of water. Therefore, it is important to monitor and control the chloride content in water samples.

## Principle

The most common method for determining the chloride content in water sample is the Mohr method. This method is based on the precipitation of chloride ions as silver chloride in the presence of chromate indicator. The end point of the titration is marked by the formation of a red-brown precipitate of silver chromate, which indicates that all the chloride ions have been precipitated.

## Procedure

- Take a 50 mL aliquot of the water sample in a conical flask and add a few drops of potassium chromate indicator.
- Titrate the sample with 0.1 N silver nitrate solution from a burette until a permanent red-brown color appears.
- Record the volume of silver nitrate solution used as V1.
- Repeat the titration with a blank sample of distilled water and record the volume of silver nitrate solution used as V2.
- Calculate the chloride content in the water sample using the following formula:

Chloride content (mg/L) = (V1 - V2) x N x 35.45 x 1000 / 50

where N is the normality of silver nitrate solution and 35.45 is the equivalent weight of chloride.

## Precautions

- The water sample should be free from turbidity and color, as they may interfere with the end point detection.
- The potassium chromate indicator should be added in a small amount, as excess chromate may cause co-precipitation of silver chromate with silver chloride.
- The silver nitrate solution should be standardized against a known solution of sodium chloride before use.
- The titration should be performed in a well-lit room, as the color change may be difficult to observe in dim light.
- The conical flask should be swirled constantly during the titration to ensure complete mixing of the reactants.



### 11. Preparation of Phenol formaldehyde (PF) resin

- Phenol formaldehyde resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the reaction of phenol and formaldehyde.
- Phenol formaldehyde resin can be prepared by a step-growth polymerization reaction that can be either acid- or base-catalyzed .
- The ratio of phenol to formaldehyde affects the structure and properties of the resin. A 1:1 ratio produces linear chains, while a higher ratio of formaldehyde produces cross-linked networks .
- The reaction of phenol and formaldehyde can be carried out in two stages: a condensation stage and a curing stage .
- In the condensation stage, phenol and formaldehyde are mixed in a reaction vessel and heated under the influence of a catalyst. The catalyst can be hydrochloric acid, sulfuric acid, or ammonia. The reaction produces low molecular weight prepolymers called novolacs or resoles, depending on the catalyst and the ratio of phenol to formaldehyde.
- In the curing stage, the prepolymers are further heated with more formaldehyde or a hardener, such as hexamethylenetetramine, to form cross-linked networks. The curing stage can be done in a mold or a press to shape the resin into the desired form .
- The properties of phenol formaldehyde resin depend on the type of catalyst, the ratio of phenol to formaldehyde, the degree of condensation and curing, and the additives used. Phenol formaldehyde resin is generally hard, brittle, heat-resistant, and chemically resistant. It is widely used in the manufacture of plywood, laminates, molding compounds, and electrical insulation .



### 12. Preparation of Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin

Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects. It is produced from urea and formaldehyde by a condensation reaction under alkaline or acidic conditions. The following steps describe the general process of preparing UF resin:

- Step 1: An aqueous solution containing more than 50% formaldehyde and urea are mixed in a formaldehyde:urea molar ratio of 2-3:1 at pH 6-11. This step is also called the methylolation step, where formaldehyde reacts with urea to form monomethylolurea and dimethylolurea.
- Step 2: The mixture is heated to at least 80°C to initiate the condensation reaction, where methylol groups of urea derivatives react with each other or with urea to form methylene bridges. This step is also called the resinification step, where the molecular weight and viscosity of the resin increase. The pH of the mixture is adjusted to 0.5-3.5 by adding a mineral or organic acid. This step controls the curing rate and the formaldehyde emission of the resin.
- Step 3: The resin is cooled and neutralized to pH 7-8 by adding ammonia or another base. The resin is then filtered and stored for further use. The resin can be modified by adding other additives, such as melamine, to improve its properties.

The UF resin can be cured by heating it with a hardener, such as ammonium chloride, at 120-150°C for 10-20 minutes. The curing process involves further condensation and cross-linking of the resin molecules, resulting in a rigid and insoluble network. The cured resin has high strength, hardness, and water resistance, but also emits formaldehyde, which can cause health and environmental problems.



### 13. Preparation of Adipic acid / Paracetamol

#### Preparation of Adipic acid

- Adipic acid is the organic compound with the formula (CH2)4(COOH)2. It is the most important dicarboxylic acid, mainly used as a precursor for the production of nylon.
- Adipic acid is produced from a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol called KA oil, the abbreviation of ketone-alcohol oil .
- The KA oil is oxidized with nitric acid to give adipic acid, via a multistep pathway .
- The oxidation involves the formation of a nitrolic acid intermediate, which then undergoes acid-catalyzed cleavage to give nitrous oxide and a ketone .
- The ketone is further oxidized to the carboxylic acid by nitric acid .
- The overall reaction is as follows:

Adipic acid synthesis

- The process is exothermic and requires careful control of the temperature and pressure .
- The yield of adipic acid is about 60% based on cyclohexane .



### 14. Determination of Cell Conductance of a Solution

- Cell conductance of a solution is the reciprocal of its resistance, which is the opposition to the flow of electric current through the solution.
- The resistance of a solution depends on the concentration, charge, and mobility of the ions it contains, as well as the geometry of the cell used to measure it.
- A cell is a device that consists of two electrodes immersed in a solution and connected to a source of electric potential.
- A conductivity cell is a special type of cell that is used to measure the conductance of electrolytic solutions, which are solutions that can conduct electricity due to the presence of ions .
- A conductivity cell has two electrodes of equal area and fixed distance apart, so that the resistance of the solution between them is proportional to the length of the solution column and inversely proportional to the cross-sectional area of the electrodes.
- The cell constant, C, is the ratio of the length to the area of the electrodes, and it has units of m^-1^. It is a characteristic of the cell and it can be determined by measuring the resistance of a solution of known conductivity, such as 0.0200 M KCl.
- The conductivity, K, of a solution is the conductance of the solution in a cell with a cell constant of 1 m^-1^. It has units of S m^-1^ or Ω^-1^ m^-1^, where S is the symbol for siemens, the unit of conductance.
- The conductivity of a solution can be calculated from the experimental resistance, R, and the cell constant, C, by using the formula: K = C/R.
- The conductivity of a solution is related to the concentration, c, and the equivalent conductance, Λ, of the electrolyte by the formula: K = cΛ.
- The equivalent conductance, Λ, is the conductance of a solution containing one equivalent of the electrolyte per unit volume. It depends on the degree of dissociation, α, and the molar conductivities, λ, of the ions in the solution by the formula: Λ = α(λ+ + λ-).
- The molar conductivity, λ, of an ion is the conductance of a solution containing one mole of the ion per unit volume. It depends on the charge, z, and the mobility, u, of the ion by the formula: λ = zFu, where F is the Faraday constant.
- The mobility, u, of an ion is the velocity of the ion per unit electric field. It is influenced by the size, shape, and hydration of the ion, as well as the viscosity and temperature of the solution.



Hello, I am Sydney, your AI assistant. I can help you with your study material. Here is the content for the topic of determination of rate constant of hydrolysis of esters.

### 15. Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

- Esters are organic compounds that have the general formula RCOOR', where R and R' are alkyl or aryl groups.
- Esters can undergo hydrolysis, which is a chemical reaction with water, to produce carboxylic acids and alcohols.
- The hydrolysis of esters can be catalyzed by acids or bases, or can occur spontaneously under certain conditions.
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters depends on several factors, such as the nature of the ester, the temperature, the pH, and the concentration of the reactants and products.
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters can be expressed by the following rate law:

`rate = k[ester][water]`

where k is the rate constant, [ester] and [water] are the molar concentrations of the ester and water, respectively.

- The rate constant k can be determined experimentally by measuring the rate of hydrolysis of a known concentration of ester at a constant temperature and pH.
- One method to measure the rate of hydrolysis of esters is to use a conductometric titration, which is a technique that measures the electrical conductivity of a solution as a function of the volume of a titrant added.
- The conductometric titration of the hydrolysis of esters involves the following steps:

  - Prepare a known concentration of ester in a solvent, such as ethanol, and add a known volume of water.
  - Add a few drops of an acid or a base as a catalyst, depending on the type of ester.
  - Place the solution in a beaker and insert a conductivity cell, which consists of two electrodes connected to a conductivity meter.
  - Start the titration by adding a standard solution of a strong base or a strong acid, depending on the catalyst used, from a burette to the beaker, while stirring the solution and recording the conductivity and the volume of the titrant added.
  - Plot the conductivity versus the volume of the titrant added, and identify the equivalence point, which is the point where the conductivity reaches a maximum or a minimum, depending on the type of ester and the catalyst used.
  - Calculate the amount of ester hydrolyzed at the equivalence point, using the stoichiometry of the reaction and the volume and concentration of the titrant.
  - Calculate the rate of hydrolysis of the ester, using the amount of ester hydrolyzed, the initial concentration of the ester, and the time taken to reach the equivalence point.
  - Repeat the titration at different temperatures and pH values, and calculate the corresponding rate constants.
  - Plot the natural logarithm of the rate constant versus the reciprocal of the absolute temperature, and obtain the activation energy and the frequency factor of the hydrolysis reaction, using the Arrhenius equation:

  `ln k = ln A - Ea/RT`

  where A is the frequency factor, Ea is the activation energy, R is the gas constant, and T is the absolute temperature.

- The activation energy and the frequency factor are constants that reflect the energy barrier and the collision frequency of the reactants, respectively. They can be used to compare the rates of hydrolysis of different esters under different conditions.



### 16. Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds.

- Organic compounds are composed of carbon, hydrogen, and other elements such as oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur, phosphorus, halogens, etc.
- The presence of these elements and the functional groups that they form can be detected and identified by various chemical tests.
- A functional group is a specific arrangement of atoms or bonds within a molecule that determines its chemical properties and reactions.
- Some common functional groups are alcohols, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids, esters, amines, amides, etc.

#### 16.1 Element detection

- The detection of elements in organic compounds can be done by qualitative analysis, which involves the use of reagents that produce characteristic reactions or color changes with the elements.
- The most common methods of element detection are:

  - **Lassaigne's test**: This test is used to detect the presence of nitrogen, sulfur, and halogens in an organic compound. It involves the fusion of the organic compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube, which converts the elements into their sodium salts. The sodium extract is then tested with various reagents to detect the elements.
  - **Carbon and hydrogen detection**: The presence of carbon and hydrogen in an organic compound can be confirmed by heating the compound with copper oxide in a test tube, which produces carbon dioxide and water. The carbon dioxide can be tested with lime water, which turns milky, and the water can be tested with anhydrous copper sulfate, which turns blue.
  - **Oxygen detection**: The presence of oxygen in an organic compound can be inferred by the absence of nitrogen, sulfur, and halogens, or by the oxidation of the compound with potassium dichromate and sulfuric acid, which produces a green color.

#### 16.2 Functional group identification

- The identification of functional groups in organic compounds can be done by functional group analysis, which involves the use of specific reagents that react with the functional groups and produce characteristic products or color changes.
- The most common methods of functional group identification are:

  - **Alcohol test**: The presence of an alcohol group (-OH) in an organic compound can be detected by the following tests:
    - **Lucas test**: This test is used to distinguish between primary, secondary, and tertiary alcohols. It involves the reaction of the alcohol with Lucas reagent, which is a mixture of zinc chloride and concentrated hydrochloric acid. The formation of a turbid solution indicates the presence of a tertiary alcohol, while the formation of a turbid solution after 5 minutes indicates the presence of a secondary alcohol. A primary alcohol does not react with Lucas reagent.
    - **Iodoform test**: This test is used to detect the presence of a methyl group attached to a carbonyl group (-CO-CH3) in an organic compound, which can be an aldehyde, a ketone, or a secondary alcohol. It involves the reaction of the compound with iodine and sodium hydroxide, which produces a yellow precipitate of iodoform (CHI3) with a characteristic smell.
  - **Aldehyde and ketone test**: The presence of an aldehyde or a ketone group (>C=O) in an organic compound can be detected by the following tests:
    - **Tollen's test**: This test is used to distinguish between aldehydes and ketones. It involves the reaction of the compound with Tollen's reagent, which is a solution of silver nitrate and ammonia. The formation of a silver mirror on the inner wall of the test tube indicates the presence of an aldehyde, while a ketone does not react with Tollen's reagent.
    - **Fehling's test**: This test is used to distinguish between aldehydes and ketones. It involves the reaction of the compound with Fehling's reagent, which is a mixture of copper sulfate and sodium hydroxide. The formation of a brick-red precipitate of copper oxide indicates the presence of an aldehyde, while a ketone does not react with Fehling's reagent.
  - **Carboxylic acid test**: The presence of a carboxylic acid group (-COOH) in an organic compound can be detected by the following tests:
    - **Sodium bicarbonate test**: This test is used to confirm the presence of a carboxylic acid group. It involves the reaction of the compound with sodium bicarbonate, which produces carbon dioxide gas. The evolution of carbon dioxide gas can be tested with lime water, which turns milky.
    - **Esterification test**: This test is used to identify the presence of a carboxylic acid group.

## Course Outcomes:

- Course outcomes are statements that describe what students are expected to know and be able to do by the end of a course.
- Course outcomes are aligned with the course objectives, content, activities, and assessments.
- Course outcomes are measurable, observable, and achievable within the scope and duration of the course.
- Course outcomes are written in clear and concise language, using action verbs that indicate the level of cognitive skills required.
- Course outcomes are communicated to the students at the beginning of the course and throughout the course.
- Course outcomes are used to guide the course design, delivery, and evaluation.
- Course outcomes are reviewed and revised periodically to ensure their relevance and effectiveness.



### Upon completion of the course the student should be able to:

- Demonstrate an understanding of the basic concepts and principles of artificial intelligence, such as agents, search, knowledge representation, reasoning, planning, learning, natural language processing, computer vision, and robotics.
- Apply appropriate methods and techniques to solve various artificial intelligence problems, such as heuristic search, logic inference, probabilistic reasoning, machine learning algorithms, natural language processing tools, computer vision techniques, and robotics frameworks.
- Analyze and evaluate the strengths and limitations of different artificial intelligence approaches, such as symbolic, connectionist, evolutionary, and hybrid systems.
- Design and implement artificial intelligence systems using suitable programming languages and libraries, such as Python, Prolog, TensorFlow, PyTorch, OpenCV, and ROS.
- Communicate and present artificial intelligence concepts and solutions effectively using oral, written, and graphical forms.



### Course Outcomes Bloom's Taxonomy

- Course outcomes are brief statements that describe what students will be expected to learn by the end of the course.
- Course outcomes should be aligned with the course content, activities, and assessments, and should reflect the level of cognitive skills that students need to demonstrate.
- Bloom's taxonomy is a model of cognitive skills that can be used to classify educational learning objectives and to design course outcomes .
- Bloom's taxonomy consists of six levels of cognition, from lower to higher: remember, understand, apply, analyze, evaluate, and create.
- Each level of cognition has a set of verbs that can be used to write course outcomes that indicate the expected level of student learning .
- For example, at the remember level, verbs such as define, list, recall, and identify can be used to write course outcomes that require students to recall factual information .
- At the create level, verbs such as design, construct, produce, and synthesize can be used to write course outcomes that require students to generate new ideas or products .
- Course outcomes should be specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART) to ensure that they are clear and assessable.
- Course outcomes should also be student-centered, meaning that they focus on what the students will do or learn, rather than what the instructor will teach or cover.
- Below are some examples of course outcomes that utilize Bloom's taxonomy:

  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **describe** the colonization of the Americas by the British, French and Spanish (remember level).
  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **apply** the principles of thermodynamics to solve engineering problems (apply level).
  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **analyze** the outcomes of the Civil War and their impact on the reconstruction era (analyze level).
  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **evaluate** the strengths and weaknesses of different research methods in psychology (evaluate level).
  - At the end of the course, students will be able to **create** a portfolio of original artworks that demonstrate their personal style and artistic vision (create level).

# Level

- A level is a concept that can be applied to different domains, such as mathematics, physics, chemistry, biology, logic, linguistics, etc.
- In general, a level is a position or rank in a hierarchy or scale that indicates the degree of complexity, quality, quantity, or intensity of something.
- For example, in mathematics, a level can refer to the degree of a polynomial, the number of dimensions of a vector space, the order of a differential equation, the rank of a matrix, etc.
- In physics, a level can refer to the energy state of an atom, molecule, or nucleus, the height or depth of a fluid, the intensity of a sound or light wave, the degree of disorder of a system, etc.
- In chemistry, a level can refer to the concentration of a substance, the pH of a solution, the oxidation state of an element, the valence of an atom, the reactivity of a compound, etc.
- In biology, a level can refer to the organization of life, such as the cell, tissue, organ, system, organism, population, community, ecosystem, biome, biosphere, etc.
- In logic, a level can refer to the validity or soundness of an argument, the truth value of a proposition, the strength of a modal operator, the complexity of a formula, the expressiveness of a language, etc.
- In linguistics, a level can refer to the structure of language, such as the phoneme, morpheme, word, phrase, clause, sentence, discourse, etc.



### CO-1 Get an understanding of the use of different analytical instruments. K3

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Different analytical instruments have different principles, applications, advantages, and limitations.
- Some examples of analytical instruments are:

  - Spectrophotometers: measure the intensity of light absorbed or emitted by a sample at different wavelengths.
  - Chromatographs: separate the components of a mixture based on their affinity to a stationary phase and a mobile phase.
  - Mass spectrometers: identify the molecular mass and structure of compounds by ionizing them and measuring their mass-to-charge ratio.
  - Electrochemical analyzers: measure the electrical potential or current generated by a chemical reaction or a redox process.
  - Microscopes: magnify the image of small objects or structures using visible light, electrons, or other forms of radiation.
  - Biosensors: detect the presence or concentration of a specific biomolecule or analyte using a biological recognition element and a transducer.

- To get an understanding of the use of different analytical instruments, one should:

  - Know the basic theory and working principle of each instrument.
  - Understand the sample preparation, calibration, and validation methods for each instrument.
  - Learn how to interpret and report the results obtained from each instrument.
  - Compare and contrast the performance, accuracy, precision, sensitivity, and selectivity of different instruments.
  - Identify the sources of error, uncertainty, and interference in each instrument.
  - Recognize the advantages and disadvantages of each instrument for different types of analysis.
  - Select the appropriate instrument for a given analytical problem or objective.



Hello, I am Sydney, your AI assistant. I can help you with your query.

### CO2: Measure the molecular / system properties such as surface tension

- Carbon dioxide (CO2) is a linear molecule with a bond length of 116.3 pm and a bond angle of 180 degrees.
- CO2 is a non-polar molecule with a dipole moment of zero and a low boiling point of -78.5 °C at 1 atm.
- CO2 is soluble in water and reacts with water to form carbonic acid (H2CO3), which dissociates into bicarbonate (HCO3-) and hydrogen (H+) ions.
- The solubility and reactivity of CO2 in water depend on the temperature, pressure, and pH of the solution.
- The density and specific weight of CO2 vary with temperature and pressure, and can be calculated using online tools or tables.
- The surface tension of CO2 is the energy required to increase the surface area of the liquid phase due to intermolecular forces.
- The surface tension of CO2 decreases with increasing temperature and pressure, as the intermolecular forces become weaker.
- The surface tension of CO2 can be measured using various methods, such as the pendant drop method, the Wilhelmy plate method, or the capillary rise method.
- The surface tension of CO2 can also be affected by the presence of solutes, such as monoethanolamine (MEA), which is used to capture CO2 from flue gas.
- The surface tension of CO2 loaded aqueous MEA solutions has been measured by several studies, and the results show that the surface tension decreases with increasing CO2 loading and MEA concentration .



### Viscosity
- Viscosity is the property of a fluid that measures its resistance to flow.
- Viscosity depends on the internal friction between the molecules of the fluid, which is affected by factors such as temperature, pressure, and composition.
- Viscosity is expressed by the formula:

    `η = F / (A * dv/dy)`

    where η is the viscosity, F is the force applied, A is the area of contact, and dv/dy is the velocity gradient of the fluid.
- The SI unit of viscosity is the pascal-second (Pa·s), which is equivalent to the newton-second per square meter (N·s/m²) or the kilogram per meter per second (kg/m·s).
- Some examples of fluids with different viscosities are water, honey, oil, and air. Water has a low viscosity, while honey has a high viscosity. Oil has a higher viscosity than water, but lower than honey. Air has a very low viscosity compared to liquids.
- Viscosity affects the flow rate, pressure drop, and heat transfer of fluids in pipes, pumps, turbines, and other devices. Viscosity also influences the formation and stability of emulsions, suspensions, and foams.
